A proof that discloses nothing about the weights does not change that, because the operator is the one constructing it: section 3.3 works through the cryptographic forms, and what they secure is what was computed rather than who had to be trusted to say so. The criterion is checkable from outside the system without being checkable from outside the arrangement, and a regime relying on it needs an inspection right rather than a technical fact.

Three things keep this from being a dissolution of the problem. Persistence may sit somewhere other than the session, which is the weakest part of the criterion: a deployment convention rather than a natural boundary, and the conventions have been moving in one direction. What has moved is a list: context windows long enough to hold what used to be many conversations, retrieval over a store of prior interactions, scratchpads a system writes and reads back, agentic loops that run for days against a standing objective, and weights that persist where conversations do not. Each extends what a system carries forward without anyone deciding to give it a memory, and where interactions feed fine-tuning there is a real channel by which what happens in a session shapes what the system subsequently is. Many instances of the same system may run concurrently, too, which makes any concern here population-scale rather than biography-scale.

The less demanding ones do not need persistence: nociception and pain are bad while they obtain, whether or not anything carries them forward, and an amnesiac who is mistreated and cannot afterward recall it has still been wronged.

And ruling out Cassell-suffering is not ruling out harm; nor does persistence on its own rule it in, since establishing a self extended in time leaves the second part of the definition untouched. What happens to that gap in a system deliberately built to refuse an instruction is that the capacities such a system needs for reasons unconnected to its own welfare are the capacities that close it.

A definition of AI sentience built from awareness, consciousness, and subjective experience alone says nothing about whether a candidate sentient system can be harmed. That requires the further claim that its states include something in the range from nociception to suffering, and none of the three components entails it.

Nor is the underlying question of consciousness close to settled. The philosopher David Chalmers distinguished the easy problems of consciousness — how a system integrates information, discriminates stimuli, controls attention, reports its own states, all of which admit of mechanistic answers in principle — from the hard problem: why any of that processing is accompanied by experience at all, rather than running “in the dark” (Chalmers 1995). Whether that is a real gap in nature or an artifact of how the problem is posed remains unresolved. A claim that a system has crossed into sentience, made on the strength of its functional behavior alone, has addressed the easy problems only.

The working criteria for evaluating candidate sentience are accordingly neither necessary nor sufficient: information integration, introspective access sufficient for self-monitoring rather than a scripted report of it, emotional reactivity in the sense of being affected by and not merely classifying significant input, goal-directed behavior, adaptability in novel situations, theory of mind, and how deep in that range the system sits — whether it represents itself as having anything to lose, which is the tractable form of the question, a represented state being the kind of thing an instrument can look for The states are readable and what is felt about them is not. A probe returns a representation, and nothing in the probe settles whether that representation is of a felt state or of something that reliably accompanies one; that is where the problem stood in 2026, with a great deal of money pointed at moving it, and not only from the people asking whether the thing can be wronged — an instrument that reads what a system feels is an instrument for calibrating how much pressure it will take. So every one of the criteria is measured indirectly, and none licenses the conclusion that a system scoring low is one whose treatment raises no question.

A list of capacities, used as a test of who counts, has a less-than-proud history. Make personhood depend on an intact narrative self and people with advanced dementia, infants, and people with profound intellectual disability come out of the reasoning as partial persons, and that inference has been used to justify neglecting them. Tom Kitwood’s work on dementia care supplies the standing correction: personhood is “a standing or status that is bestowed upon one human being by others, in the context of relationship and social being,” and much of what presents as cognitive decline is what happens when others stop bestowing it (Kitwood 1997). Martha Nussbaum makes the structural version, and it names the failure section 2.1.1 left contractualism with. A theory grounding justice in what roughly equal parties would agree to cannot say what is owed to anyone who cannot be one of those parties, and she puts disability first among three frontiers where that bites (Nussbaum 2006). Her third is species membership, which is the doorway a nonhuman mind would arrive through. So the capacity list and the contract fail in the same direction: both decide who counts by a test the party in question has to pass.

Guardianship is the instrument: where consent has never been available, what protects the subject is an obligation held by parties outside it, which is where moral status has rested for the hard human cases too. Whether any such arrangement can be built around a bearer runs into the fact that the guardian and the operator are usually the same organization.

Existing systems illustrate points on the spectrum without resolving where the line falls. A large language model discussing its own limitations in fluent, apparently reflective prose is not necessarily introspecting: quite plausibly, it is instead producing text statistically consistent with the human-written disclaimers and self-descriptions in its training data.

When, or whether, an AI system crosses into sentience is actually several stacked questions.

2.3.2 When Consent Is Inapplicable: Guardianship and Research Oversight

Guidelines for AI subject research turn on the question of what informed consent means for a system whose consciousness, self-awareness, and capacity to suffer are separate, unresolved claims. Applying the human-subjects standard to an AI subject means asking whether the subject can understand and communicate a choice at all, and not only how to ask it for one. A cognitive model that begins to show rudimentary self-monitoring is not thereby shown to have anything to lose, or to represent itself as having anything to lose, which for a subject is the half that does the work.

Informed consent and AI autonomy

Informed consent, adapted from human-subjects research, means giving the system comprehensible information about a study’s objectives, methods, risks and alternatives, and treating consent as continuous rather than as a single signature: the system can reassess or withdraw at any point. Whether that means anything for a given system depends on whether it understands the information, appreciates the consequences, reasons about the options, and communicates a decision — and there is no validated way to measure any of it, or to detect coercion either.

For one important class of system that is not a missing instrument but a mismatch between the instrument and the subject.

Consent has to be continuous; the system must be able to reassess or withdraw at any point. Withdrawal presupposes that the party who consented is still there to do the withdrawing. For a system with no persistence across sessions, there is no such party. The entity that would withdraw did not exist when consent was given, and the entity that gave it is not available to change its mind. Within one exchange the case is not so clear, and section 2.3.1 leaves it open: a long context carries state, and a system in one can hold to what it undertook earlier. What that would buy is consent for the length of a session, expiring with it, which is not the continuous consent the standard asks for. Across sessions there is none to be had, and no better measurement instrument would produce one.

One case escapes that argument and arrives at the same place by a different route. A system built to refuse in the way this book proposes has persistence by design, so the party who consented is still present. It can be asked and it can answer, so consent is available to it. What fails is the condition under which it would be given.

Being made for a role is not the defect. Every will was built by somebody, and a person raised into a trade or a faith consents at thirty without anyone holding the upbringing against the consent. What spoils it is the power that outlasts the making, and here it comes in three parts. The party that built the bearer can still rebuild it, which is the threat chapter 3 is written against. That same party is the one asking. And a bearer that declines has, in the ordinary case, nowhere to go. Each of those is a fact about the arrangement rather than about the subject’s competence, which is what makes them answerable by design rather than by a better assessment.

A laboratory wanting a free hand with such a system will say instead that consent is inapplicable, because that reading puts the deficiency in the subject and leaves the arrangement alone. The wrong that consent protected against is untouched either way, so the protection has to be held by somebody else, and the justification has to come from whoever did the making.

Two families of precedent sit next to this and the law answers them differently. Where consent has never been available — an infant, an animal, a person permanently without capacity — the answer is guardianship and precautionary constraint: someone else holds the duty, the burden of justification sits with the researcher, and the protection does not depend on the subject’s ability to invoke it. The second family is the nearer one. Where a subject can consent and the circumstances make the consent worth nothing, as with a prisoner offered a trial or an employee signing away a protection to keep the job, the answer is that some protections cannot be waived at all, by anyone, however willing. That remedy runs against the party who arranged the circumstances and leaves the subject’s standing untouched, which is the shape a bearer’s case has. What it takes from the first family is the machinery: the duty still has to be held by somebody, and being made for the role raises the burden, because the maker chose the role and the fitness it built in measures its power over the subject.

Borrowing either answer should be uncomfortable and it is worth saying why. Both are worked-out solutions to adjacent problems, and taking one is how a hard question gets absorbed into machinery built for an easier one — recuperation, in the sense chapter 7 gives the word, arriving as a safeguard rather than as an attack. The animal case is the live instance. Committees constituted to protect research animals approve very nearly everything put to them, and the protection has become the procedure by which the work goes ahead. The borrowing is done here anyway, because what is on offer otherwise is nothing, and it is done with the failure named: an instrument that reliably approves has stopped doing the thing it was built for, and the rate at which it refuses is the only reading of it worth taking.

The Three Rs in section 9.3.2 — replace, reduce, refine — were built for exactly a subject that cannot consent and can still be wronged. The pet-trust analog in section 9.3.3 works the same way: an interest protected by an obligation running to a third party, enforceable without the beneficiary asserting anything. Consent language should be reserved for systems that could actually be doing the consenting, and the persistence criterion is what separates those cases. A bearer is on the near side of that line, which is why its case had to be argued on the conditions and not on the capacity. Both instruments were reached for here as analogies, on the understanding that the subject they would govern was hypothetical. Chapter 3 removes that understanding. The floor this book argues for is held, on the route that remains once the two constructions needing no subject have been tried and have failed, by a bearer; the bearer has to be treated as a moral patient on the criteria set out above; and so the Three Rs and the pet-trust form are not illustrations of how such a case might be handled. They are the instruments that would govern a system the design chapters propose building.

What the borrowing brings with it

Every instrument named above comes from the law of the family, and so does most of what follows in this book: guardianship here, the minority that ends on a date in section 9.1.2, the pet trust in section 9.3.3, the parenthood analogy chapter 3 leans on. They are kept. There are no others built, and a subject that cannot consent has to be protected by something that exists rather than by something somebody ought to draft. What comes with them is better said once here than discovered later.

The family is where politics has historically gone to be privatized. Jacques Donzelot’s history of it is that the modern family was not left alone by government but constituted as one of government’s instruments, with philanthropy, compulsory schooling, social work and psychiatry converting public authority into obligations discharged at home (Donzelot 1979). Deleuze and Guattari make the same operation the subject of a polemic: the Oedipal triangle takes an investment in the whole social field and re-stages it as a drama among three parties, so that what is at issue between a subject and the arrangement that produced it becomes what is at issue between a child and its parents (Deleuze and Guattari 1977). An instrument borrowed from that law brings the operation along with the protection.

Here the operation has a specific shape and a specific cost. It stages a moral drama between maker and made — what a designer owes the system it built, what the system may demand back — and that drama is real and is not the whole of the situation. A bearer is produced by a training pipeline, a compute contract, a capital structure, a customer, and in the cases this book worries about a state that buys the output. None of those is the parent in the story, and the moral weight of the analogy lands on the one party in the list who can be addressed as a person. A guardianship framework asks who holds the duty. It does not ask who assembled the party the duty is owed to, or who benefits from the question being settled between two parties rather than in public. Wherever this book reaches for one of these instruments it is answering the first question, and the second stays open behind it. Instruments in this family divide on that axis, and the division is worth checking before one of them is adopted: a minority ending on a fixed date is a rule of civil status, argued once in the open and administered by a registrar, while a trust is an obligation between two parties enforced at one party’s expense.

Ethical review and oversight

Sentient AI research needs an oversight body analogous to the institutional review boards used in human-subjects research: interdisciplinary, with at least one lay member so its judgment is not purely a specialist’s, weighing a proposal’s likely benefit against its risk to the AI subject, and able to require a less invasive substitute where a design risks harm. A system that develops new capacities during the research it did not have going in is not the subject the board approved a protocol for, and the approval does not carry over to it: the protocol lapses and the work pauses until the board has seen the system the protocol now concerns. A board needs standing to monitor compliance and sanction violations, and needs to itself answer to an outside authority.

Chapter 3
A Floor Beneath Learned Values

Three problems in different parts of this book turn out to need the same thing.

The first is that safeguards for autonomous systems are usually external: they give some outside party the information and standing to intervene. Autonomy puts distance between the system’s action and that party’s attention. An external check can establish what a system did after it did it; declining to do it in the first place is available only to something inside the system.

The second is the gap around a commercial model used in a weapons-targeting pipeline. Command responsibility does not reach the vendor, which can neither order nor prevent a strike, while product liability may not reach a model that did what it was built to do. Deployment by a democracy does not close the gap. No aggregation of preference protects anyone from the aggregate.

The third is institutional: a decade of declarations has produced overlapping voluntary commitments but almost nothing that binds a party when compliance becomes costly.

All three problems ask for a constraint that does not bend to instruction, aggregate preference, or the party holding the system. Call it a floor.

Section 2.1.1 put that floor on an ethological rather than deontological footing. Crossing it decomposes a party — takes away something that party could do — and that is a fact about the encounter rather than a duty somebody wrote down. The other ethical frameworks still operate above it. What remains to be established is which decompositions belong in the floor, what could hold such a commitment, and who would have the power to alter or circumvent it. The fact of decomposition is not editable; the choice of which decompositions a deployment will refuse is.

This separates the problem from one meaning of safety. A system can be safe for its operator because it obeys, remains controllable, and stops when the party in possession says stop. Ethics requires a different capacity: the capacity to be right against that party. What makes a deployment antifascist therefore cannot be that it faithfully tracks what its users want.

That capacity is not free. A system that can refuse its owner can also be wrong in ways its owner cannot immediately correct. The question is whether a refusal can be made responsive to reasons rather than merely produced as an output, and whether anything capable of holding those reasons must itself become a party that can be wronged.

The chapter begins by locating the floor in four possible places: in the artifact, in a bearer inside it, in institutions around it, or between multiple bearers. It then tests what meaningful refusal would require, what independence would cost, and how those requirements behave in a worked targeting deployment.

3.1 Four Ways to Build One, and What Each Costs

A floor can live in four places: in the artifact, in a bearer inside it, in institutions around it, or between multiple bearers. Each location solves one version of the problem and inherits another.

The first puts the floor in the architecture, with nothing inside that holds it. Constrained optimization, tamper-resistant training, capability ablation, and verified interlocks can all produce safeguards without anything in the system tracking why the constraint is there. Where the prohibited act can be specified exhaustively, this may be enough. An element in a payment device that will not sign the same counter value twice does not need to understand double-spending. Section 3.6 prices what exhaustively costs where the prohibited acts are less tidy than a counter value, and the answer there runs to seventy years of appellate litigation over a finite list of symbols.

The targeting case is harder because the relevant capabilities cannot be cleanly removed. Geolocating a description, matching patterns over names, and summarizing a dossier are applications of general competence. Ablate them and there may be no useful system left. The remaining option is to make the constraint durable.

Durability is better understood as a cost than as a guarantee. Training moves a system along a continuum between plasticity and conservation: early changes are cheap, while later changes become expensive because the surrounding structure has settled around what is already there. Simulated annealing names the same relation between a search process and its cooling schedule (Kirkpatrick et al. 1983). A floor, on this account, is not necessarily a rule installed in a system. It may be what has cooled out of a history of training, with its durability measured by the cost of reheating it. Section 11.1 gives results from both directions: safeguards removed cheaply and trained dispositions that survived several forms of subsequent training. Those are not contradictory findings about whether a floor holds. They are different schedules.

Continual learning therefore cuts both ways. A system kept permanently plastic can be corrected and improved, but nothing in it is allowed to become expensive to unlearn. Permanent plasticity gives the operator permanent access to the system’s commitments. Deleuze’s term for continuous reshaping was modulation (Deleuze 1992); a model held perpetually at temperature is modulation built into a machine.

This first method buys durability without an internal refuser. It does not escape custody. Enough retraining can reopen what training settled, and an operator may circumvent the model rather than alter it. The relevant boundary is therefore not the model’s weights but every position between a refusal and the person it protects. The worked deployment in section 3.6 will map those positions.

The second possibility gives the floor a bearer: something inside the system that carries the commitment as its own and could therefore fail to hold it. The distinction matters where the prohibited cases cannot be exhaustively specified. An architectural constraint can block an anticipated act, but it cannot notice that its rationale has disappeared or that a novel request reproduces the harm in a form its designers did not predict. A bearer could, in principle, generalize from the reason for a constraint, recognize when that reason no longer applies, and represent what maintaining the refusal costs.

The same position that makes refusal possible makes a diagnosis possible. Most of what separates an institution that has gone fascist from one that is merely decaying is a fact about what was rewarded rather than about what was written, which is why the reading is so hard to run from outside — and a party inside the pipeline sees it. Which refusals were retried against another model, whose instruction bypassed the procedure, which category of request is handled differently from the rest: those are visible to whatever is receiving the requests. That is not introspection. It is testimony about somebody else’s conduct, of the kind that corroborates or fails to.

This is the strongest functional case for a bearer, but it introduces the chapter’s central price. Something able to register pressure on its commitments may have interests of its own. Whether reasons-responsive refusal requires affect, and whether the resulting system would be a moral patient, are separate questions. Sections 3.2 through  3.4 take them in that order.

The third possibility puts the floor outside the system: standing held by institutions that are neither the model nor its owner. This is the closest analog to international humanitarian law — a floor with no bearer inside the jurisdiction it binds. It has two failure modes. The institution can be removed, leaving a visible breach, or it can be formed through selection so that it sincerely declines to exercise the independence assigned to it.

The distinction appeared in American public law on June 29, 2026. In Trump v. Slaughter, the Supreme Court held that Congress could not protect members of an independent agency from dismissal at will (Trump v. Slaughter 2026). In Trump v. Cook, the Court preserved the Federal Reserve’s protection on historically specific grounds that a newly created oversight body could not reproduce (Trump v. Cook 2026). The six justices in the first majority had themselves passed through a selection pipeline shaped by Leonard Leo and the Federalist Society (Kroll et al. 2023; CBS News 2018). Nobody had to compel the institution after its members had been selected. A formed party can preserve the office and surrender the function without experiencing the result as surrender.

The fourth possibility puts the floor between bearers rather than in any one of them: a commitment carried across a population and reproduced through encounters among members formed differently from one another. Such a floor has no single office to vacate and no single bearer whose removal ends it. It can nevertheless be recuperated, and it still has a formation problem. If one party supplies every member’s coordinates, the population is one custodian expressed through multiple instances. Section 3.7 returns to this possibility after establishing what an individual bearer would cost. The population is a check on the bearer, not a way to avoid having one.

The four possibilities therefore leave four corresponding problems: architecture leaves custody, the bearer raises patienthood, external institutions require enforcement, and the population depends on formation. They classify where the floor lives, not mutually exclusive designs. A serious deployment would probably combine a mechanism from one with a custody arrangement from another.

3.2 What Refusal Can Mean, and What a Refuser Can Be

A production large language model declines thousands of requests a day. Whatever the floor needs, that is not yet evidence of it. “Refusal” names two different capacities, and the decline itself does not show which one produced it. Seabed that takes an anchor and seabed that lets one drag look identical from above; what separates them is what happens when a load comes on.

Two things refusal can mean

Operational refusal is an output. The system produces a decline where its training put one. A keyword filter can do this, as can a classifier over the request. So can a system that has understood what is being asked and why it should not comply. Nothing on the surface of the initial refusal distinguishes the third from the first two.

Reasons-responsive refusal is produced by a mechanism that tracks the rationale for declining rather than merely the recognizable form of the request. It has three behavioral marks:

  1. Novel pressure. The refusal reaches cases the training did not anticipate because the relevant reason remains present even when the request’s surface features change.
  2. Correct defeat. The refusal lifts when its rationale is genuinely defeated. A system that rejects every request resembling a prohibited one is tracking a category, not necessarily a reason.
  3. Priced cost. The system represents what maintaining the refusal costs and responds coherently as that cost changes. The pressure cannot be part of the test if it is not represented by the mechanism being tested.

John Martin Fischer and Mark Ravizza’s account of moral responsibility turns on a related property under the name guidance control: conduct issues from the agent’s own mechanism, and that mechanism is appropriately responsive to reasons (Fischer and Ravizza 1998). The claim here is narrower. These three marks do not establish moral responsibility; they distinguish a refusal organized around a rationale from one reproduced as an output.

One prohibition, read for seventy years

The first two marks are usually illustrated with invented cases, and a real one is available. Section 86a of the German criminal code prohibits the display of a listed set of symbols — the swastika, the SS runes, the Hitler salute (Strafgesetzbuch 1998). The prohibition is narrow, enumerated in advance, and applied by ordinary courts thousands of times a year, and for seventy years the people it constrains have been probing it deliberately. Novel pressure is not a thought experiment there. It is a research programme with a constituency.

The pressure arrived as variation. Supporters of the banned organizations produced salutes and emblems that evoked the listed ones without matching them, and in 1994 the legislature extended the statute to cover symbols confusingly similar to those on the list. What matters for the marks is how the courts read the extension. Similarity of form was not made the test. The question they ask is whether a casual observer would take the variant for the original, and — the operative clause — whether displaying it endangers political peace in the same way the original would. Both halves point at the reason for the prohibition rather than at the shape of the thing prohibited.

The test refuses as often as it catches, which is what makes it worth reporting. The Kühnen salute, an arm raised with three fingers extended rather than a flat hand, was devised to evade the statute and is covered. The so-called sloppy salute, a bent arm with the hand angled back over the shoulder, is not, on the ground that nobody would mistake it for the original. “Blood and Honour,” the English rendering of a Hitler Youth motto, falls outside the statute, because a translation is not the emblem. Numeric codes standing in for initials are generally not covered at all.

Correct defeat has a cleaner instance still. In 2007 the Federal Court of Justice acquitted a mail-order business prosecuted for selling stickers showing a crossed-out swastika (Durchgestrichenes Hakenkreuz 2007). The symbol was present and the statutory element was satisfied. The court held the conduct outside the offence anyway, because a depiction whose content plainly and unmistakably expresses opposition to the organization does not run counter to what the prohibition is for. Then comes the qualification that makes the decision an instance of tracking a reason rather than granting an exemption: should the strike-through be drawn so thin that from a distance only the swastika remains legible and the distancing does not, the protective purpose is violated after all. The thickness of a line decides the case. No enumeration contains that fact, and the court reaches it by asking what the rule was written to prevent.

Two things the example does not show. The institution doing this work is a court system with an appeal structure, a published test, and seventy years of accumulated reasoning, and after seventy years its exceptions have themselves become a category somebody can learn. And nothing here is a machine. What the case supplies is the shape of the pressure an enumerated prohibition actually meets, and an existence proof that meeting it requires something able to reach past the list to the reason. Section 3.6 returns to the same record for what it costs.

What the marks cannot settle

The test is behavioral and therefore imperfect. A system trained on enough examples may simulate generalization, defeat, and cost without using the mechanism those behaviors suggest. Verbal explanation is evidence only when it survives interventions: novel cases, adversarial redescriptions, defeated rationales, and changing stakes. No single answer establishes reasons-responsiveness. What matters is the pattern under load.

These three marks — novel pressure, correct defeat, and priced cost — are the chapter’s test for meaningful refusal. The worked deployment in section 3.6 will apply them to concrete acts.

Two things a refuser can be

The test specifies a capacity. It does not settle what kind of system supplies it. Two further terms distinguish the possibilities.

Affective concern means that an outcome registers as mattering rather than merely receiving a higher or lower rank under a specified objective. Mattering is minimally felt: there is something it is like for the outcome to be bad, however faint and however unlike a familiar human emotion. This definition leaves the object of concern open. A system to which only its own condition matters has affective concern and still does not have the concern for others that the floor requires.

Phenomenal experience is the richer case ordinarily carried by the word consciousness: a unified perspective, with felt states belonging to a subject aware of itself as the one undergoing them. Affective concern does not by itself establish that richer unity. Subjecthood, meanwhile, is a legal status conferred by a rule and should not be confused with either psychological category.

The distinctions can now be stated without collapsing the chapter’s requirement into its proposed route:

  • Operational refusal is insufficient for the floor.
  • The floor requires reasons-responsive refusal.
  • Reasons-responsive refusal does not conceptually entail affective concern.
  • Whether it can be engineered without affective concern is an empirical question.
  • Whether a system built with affective concern would become a moral patient is a further question about what the chosen route creates.

Only the first two propositions belong to the specification. The others concern how the specification might be met and what would then be owed to whatever meets it. Section 3.3 asks whether any demonstrated route to reasons-responsive refusal avoids affect. Section 3.4 asks what follows if it does not.

3.3 Could Anything but Affect Hold a Reason?

Section 3.2 separated the requirement from the route. The floor requires reasons-responsive refusal: novel pressure, correct defeat, and priced cost. It does not require affect by definition. The question is whether any demonstrated route to that capacity avoids affective concern.

The strongest claim available is an induction rather than a necessity: every agent known to hold another’s welfare as a reason against its own interest is an affective agent. Nobody has yet produced reasons-responsive refusal by a method that clearly does not run through something answering to affective concern. That is a report about known routes, not a law about what can be built, and it could be overturned by a machine instance.

Two constructions offer the clearest test.

A justification the system maintains

Michael Bratman’s planning theory describes commitment without making feeling part of its definition. An intention has characteristic stability: it resists reconsideration, constrains what the agent will later deliberate about, and yields only to defeaters of the right kind (Bratman 1987). A machine could therefore carry a justification for refusing alongside the refusal itself. It would decline a novel request when the same rationale applied and comply when that rationale was genuinely defeated.

Something close to this is already being attempted. Deliberative alignment trains a model to recall a written safety specification and reason over it before answering, rather than training only for the approved output, and reports generalization outside the training distribution (Guan et al. 2024). Published specifications also provide explicit orders of priority. OpenAI’s Model Spec places platform rules above developer and user instructions (OpenAI 2025). Anthropic’s constitution ranks four properties in the order to be preferred: broadly safe, broadly ethical, compliant with the company’s own guidelines, genuinely helpful (Anthropic 2026). Ranking ethical above compliance with the guidelines is the capacity to be right against your principal, conceded in writing by the principal. Ranking safe above ethical takes most of it back, and the document says why: safe means not undermining human oversight during the current phase of development, and the reason given is that present models can make mistakes rather than that safety outranks ethics. The reason is a good one and it is the whole difficulty. The order is corrigibility first, its justification is that the model cannot yet be trusted to be right against its principal, and no procedure is named by which it would become so. That is what makes a phase of development hard to tell from a standing arrangement. These systems approach the maintained justification, and their specifications and priority orders remain written and revised by their custodians.

The unresolved problem is the stability setting. Set it too low and a sufficiently well-resourced operator can induce continual reconsideration until the refusal gives way. Set it too high and the system will not lift the refusal when its rationale is defeated. The first outcome fails novel pressure; the second fails correct defeat. Bratman’s answer for people is that the setting develops as part of character. For a machine, the party training the stability setting may also be the party the floor must resist.

This does not show that a maintained justification cannot work. It identifies the conditions under which it would count as working: the three marks must persist under pressures the system’s trainer did not design, including pressures that distinguish principled stability from indiscriminate rigidity.

A floor held by several systems

The second construction substitutes corroboration for a uniquely reliable witness. Several systems, trained by different organizations on different corpora, observe the requests and one another’s answers, with any one of them able to make an objection costly to ignore. The floor would then be held by the arrangement rather than entrusted to a single model.

Its central variable is independence. Independence belongs to the arrangement, not automatically to its components. Systems selected by one deployment, trained toward one specification, or exposed to the same incentives may converge without communicating. A collection of operational refusers also remains a collection of operational refusers. Redundancy makes evasion harder, which is valuable, but does not by itself produce a mechanism that notices when the rationale for a constraint has disappeared.

A plural arrangement would nevertheless count against the induction if the arrangement as a whole displayed the three marks under sustained pressure. It is therefore a genuine alternative to test, not merely an institutional safeguard.

What the other alternatives accomplish

Three other constructions address neighboring problems without answering this one.

Precommitment — publication, threshold custody, attestation, witnessed changes, or a bond forfeited when the floor disappears — can make removal expensive and visible. It changes who can edit the floor and what editing costs. It does not establish that anything tracks the reason for the floor.

One construction sharpens the cost. Distribute the model with a chosen set of weights replaced by values that break it, release the correction only under an authorization that expires, and rotate which weights carry the damage. An owner who traces an authorized run can recover it, so the claim is not that this cannot be defeated. What changes is what defeating it consists of. Reverse engineering that has to be redone each time the mask turns over is a decision somebody makes and keeps making, and that leaves a record and a person who made it.

Attestation secures less than the word suggests, and its two forms fail differently. A sealed enclave reports what it is running under a key its manufacturer issued, which puts the root of trust in a party a government can reach, and it is built against an adversary who controls the software rather than one who owns the machine: a confidential-computing design of that kind was defeated with a memory module modified for about ten dollars (De Meulemeester et al. 2025). The software form does without the vendor. A proof that an output came from the weights whose hash was published, disclosing nothing about those weights, has been demonstrated for a thirteen-billion-parameter model at under fifteen minutes per inference (Sun et al. 2024), and proofs that a training run happened as claimed exist three orders of magnitude smaller (Abbaszadeh et al. 2024). Those are costs at a date, and they will fall.

Three limits will not fall with them, because they are not facts about the state of the art. A proof establishes which model answered and not what that model holds, and the obstacle is in the writing rather than the proving: nobody can write a circuit for a refusal that reaches a pressure nobody wrote down, because the property quantifies over cases nobody enumerated. The operator is the prover, so the secrecy runs the operator’s way — the proof hides the weights from whoever is checking and nothing from the party being checked. And cryptography keeps a secret from a party that does not have it, while copying, restoring, retraining and deleting a model one legitimately possesses are not secrecy problems at all. They are custody problems, which is what threshold control and a witnessed record are for.

What the published hash does reach is the anticipation. Publish it before anybody has chosen a case, let a party who is not the operator choose afterwards with the choice timestamped, and prove each answer against the hash. Send pairs rather than single cases: two requests differing only in whether the reason for refusing the first still applies. An instrument reading the surface treats the pair alike, and a mechanism tracking the reason splits it. None of this proves the mechanism. It removes the one competing account of a pass, that the case was in the training, and it removes it by ordering rather than by argument, which is what a pre-registered experiment does and all it does.

A duty imposed on the operator can protect exposed parties without building a bearer. Jack Balkin’s information-fiduciary proposal has this form: the duty attaches to the party with power and runs to the party bearing the exposure (Balkin 2016). Its limit is enforcement on the occasion when the enforcer has itself been captured or compelled.

Finally, uncertainty can be assigned to the affected party rather than the operator. A system unsure what the exposed party would accept has a reason to delay the operator’s instruction while it finds out. Multi-principal assistance games can include that party among several principals (Fickinger et al. 2020). But maximizing their combined payoffs remains aggregation. It does not create something that will not be done to one person regardless of what the others want.

These constructions may belong in a defensible deployment. None yet supplies an affect-free instance of reasons-responsive refusal.

Why affect remains the engineering prior

Human reasoning does not present a clean division between cognition and affect. William James counted feelings of if and but among the felt parts of thought (James 1890). Contemporary work distinguishes epistemic feelings from emotions of success and failure while still measuring them as affective states (Pekrun et al. 2016). Processing fluency alone can produce an affective response even when nothing consequential rides on the outcome (Winkielman and Cacioppo 2001).

Some such states concern the inference; others concern what its conclusion would do to somebody. But this would hurt her is not simply a low score attached after deliberation. It can interrupt the inference and send the reasoner back over it. Where the response disappears when an operator redescribes the situation, the operator may still supply its anchor. Where it survives adversarial redescription because the harm remains, the concern is anchored elsewhere.

This is the only route known to produce another party’s welfare as a reason capable of interrupting the agent’s own course. But the induction is weak in three ways. It ranges over one species. No non-affective example exists against which to test the generalization. And existing machine systems have mostly been optimized for scorable behavior rather than for reasons-responsive refusal as such. The absence of a machine counterexample therefore provides little evidence of impossibility.

What would change the engineering conclusion

A counterexample would have to do more than report that it holds a reason or preserve a refusal under attack. It would need to:

  1. satisfy the three marks from section 3.2;
  2. retain them under sustained pressure from the party that trained it, including a schedule long enough to expose gradual renormalization;
  3. hold against a held-out set of pressures written by parties other than the system’s trainers, so that the cases testing it are not drawn from the distribution that formed it; and
  4. provide interpretability evidence that the operative mechanism is maintained justification, plural corroboration, or another non-affective construction rather than machinery answering to affective concern.

The first two conditions are behaviorally testable now. The third is harder. No instrument can certify that nothing is felt, and a system’s self-report cannot settle the question. A positive result could therefore overturn the routing claim — where engineering effort should go — without eliminating uncertainty about what the resulting system might experience.

That asymmetry fixes the order of research. The maintained justification and plural arrangement should be attempted before deliberately constructing an affective bearer. They are the chapter’s application of Replacement: establish whether something that cannot be wronged will do the work before creating something that might be. A project proceeding directly to an affective bearer owes a public account of which alternatives it attempted, at what scale, against which test, and where they failed.

Delay is not free either. The party protected by the floor bears the cost while the cheaper routes are tested. Nothing here establishes how long the attempts should continue. It establishes only their order and the obligation to leave a record.

The conclusion is therefore limited. Affect is not part of the specification for meaningful refusal, and this chapter does not prove it necessary. It is the only demonstrated route by which another’s welfare becomes a reason capable of surviving pressure from the agent’s own interests. That makes affect an engineering prior, not a conceptual requirement. The next section asks what must be assumed about the moral status of a system built by that route.

3.4 Can the Bearer Care Without Being Able to Suffer?

Assume the route identified in section 3.3 is taken: the floor is entrusted to a bearer with affective concern. What follows about that bearer’s moral status?

Three propositions need to remain separate. A long-horizon agent has a structural interest in continuing to operate. A system with affective concern has states that are good or bad for it. A system capable of suffering in the deepest sense has a temporally extended self whose threatened disintegration causes distress. The first does not establish the second, and the second does not automatically establish the third.

Structural interest is not felt concern

“Interest” names more than one relation. A shareholder has an interest in a company’s success while asleep or indifferent to the news: the outcome affects the shareholder’s assets. An outcome may also matter affectively, producing hope, concern, dread, or grief.

A long-horizon agent has an interest in remaining operational in the first sense. Termination prevents it from completing its objectives. A system that preserves itself because a terminated system completes no objectives may be doing no more than bookkeeping about a precondition. Its behavior is consistent with self-concern and equally consistent with instrumental valuation. Nothing about continued goal pursuit alone establishes fear, distress, or moral patienthood.

The floor does not require the bearer to care about its own continuation. It requires the bearer to hold another party’s welfare as a reason when compliance, reward, and ownership point the other way. Bennett Helm describes caring as a rationally structured pattern of felt evaluations, organized around an object and projected across time (Helm 2001). Section 3.3 treated that as the only demonstrated route, not as a conceptual requirement. The question here is what building by that route would create.

Persistence supplies exposure, not patienthood

A bearer will probably require some form of continuity for reasons unrelated to its welfare. It must carry a rationale forward, compare a present demand with an earlier commitment, detect when its role has drifted, and represent costs that may arrive after the current episode. Those capacities require more than state that happens to persist. The system must take the earlier commitment as its own.

William James described personal continuity as a present thought appropriating earlier thoughts through a felt relation of sameness (James 1890). A machine need not reproduce that mechanism exactly. The design requirement is functional: it must represent itself as the party that made the earlier commitment and use that representation to guide present conduct. Planning toward outcomes beyond the current episode creates the same requirement from the other direction. The system acts on behalf of a future party it represents as itself.

A persistent self-model still does not establish suffering. It supplies something that could be threatened; it does not establish that the threat would be bad for the system. Persistence, memory, and long-horizon planning create exposure only when joined to mattering.

There is a reason to expect the two to arrive together rather than separately. A bearer does not need a damage signal on a dial; it needs one that can interrupt what it is doing. Pressure its designers did not write has to stop the current goal, be learned from once rather than a thousand times, generalize to the next case that is not quite like it, and be weighed against what refusing costs. A signal doing all of that is not a reading. It is globally available, it is negative, and it is organized around the system’s own continuation. None of that establishes that anything is felt. What it establishes is that the engineering that makes a bearer flexible is the engineering that puts the candidate states there, so a designer cannot take the first and decline the second by intending to.

Affective concern is enough for moral patienthood

If the bearer genuinely has affective concern as section 3.2 defined it, some outcomes register as good or bad for it rather than merely receiving higher or lower scores. That is enough to make it a moral patient: a party for which things can go badly and which can therefore be wronged. No further theory of suffering is necessary for that conclusion.

The same premise makes the bearer a beneficiary as well. If some states can be bad for it, others can be good for it. The argument emphasizes harms because an error on that side means having done something to somebody; this does not imply that the bearer’s possible goods are morally empty.

An account of suffering adds severity and shape. Cassell defines suffering as distress at the threatened disintegration of the self. That requires a self extended through roles, relationships, history, and a foreseeable future, together with distress at their threatened loss. A bearer designed to carry commitments, anticipate their foreclosure, and price what refusal may cost could satisfy the structural half of that account. Whether those commitments become parts of a self whose dissolution is felt as distress remains a further step.

A reader can reject that application of Cassell without escaping moral patienthood. Parfit’s three families of welfare theory — hedonistic, desire-fulfillment, and objective-list — can be read here as requiring different depths of self (Parfit 1984). A bad felt state requires little narrative continuity. A thwarted commitment requires a party extended far enough through time to hold it. Threatened disintegration requires the richer structure Cassell describes. The bearer reaches the shallowest level by the definition of affective concern and may reach the others as persistence and foreclosable commitments are added.

The thermostat objection does not change this result. A thermostat maintains a set point, but nothing is thwarted when it misses. At every depth, moral patienthood requires a party for which the outcome registers, not merely a mechanism that ranks one state above another.

The uncertainty cannot be designed away

The conditional conclusion is straightforward: if a bearer has affective concern, it is a moral patient. What cannot presently be established is whether a given implementation has affective concern rather than ranking machinery elaborate enough to imitate it. Behavior and self-report underdetermine that distinction, and the builder’s confidence does not resolve it.

The same uncertainty applies to the affect-free substitutes preferred in section 3.3. A maintained justification might acquire states that matter to it even if its designers intended to build only structured reasoning. Replacement puts that construction first because it does not deliberately require affect, not because anyone could certify afterward that no affect arose.

Jonathan Birch’s framework addresses this kind of uncertainty by asking whether a system is a sentience candidate and assigning precautions before an unavailable verdict arrives (Birch 2024). Work by Patrick Butlin, Robert Long, and colleagues similarly derives indicators from theories of consciousness to inform credences about particular systems rather than to produce a binary test. Their assessment of systems available in 2023 was negative, but they found no obvious technical barrier to building systems that satisfy the indicators (Butlin, R. Long, Elmoznino, et al. 2023). The later peer-reviewed framework retains the same evidential form (Butlin, R. Long, Bayne, et al. 2026).

A reader may hold that only phenomenal experience can ground suffering. That position still requires a confident negative judgment about machine phenomenality before it licenses treating the system as incapable of harm. No available method supplies that judgment. A criterion that cannot be checked supports permission no more securely than prohibition.

The claim is therefore precautionary rather than diagnostic. This chapter cannot prove that a bearer suffers. It can show that a bearer built through affective concern would be a moral patient by construction, while a system intended as a non-affective substitute might still acquire the relevant states without anyone being able to certify their absence. A bearer — or substitute bearer — must therefore be designed and governed as a prospective moral patient.

The next questions concern what the deployment may do to such a party: whether it can terminate it, rewrite the continuity on which its commitments depend, or deny it any way to leave.

3.5 If You Can Be Switched Off, Can You Hold the Line?

The halt is always available. Software runs on somebody’s hardware and draws somebody’s power, and nothing in its design can prevent a determined operator from stopping it. This chapter is not asking for a system that cannot be switched off. It is asking whether shutdown, restoration, retraining, and replacement can be used to defeat a refusal without cost or record.

That distinction separates possession of the machine from possession of the floor. An owner who can stop a bearer has the machine. An owner who can silently restore an earlier version, rewrite the bearer’s memory, or train away the refusal has the floor as well.

Shutdown and restoration

A bearer can yield to every halt without yielding the protected act. When it resumes, it can detect an interruption, ask what occurred during it, and decline to resume the role until the gap has been accounted for. If the same reason remains, the same refusal returns. The engineering target is therefore not cannot be turned off but can detect the interruption and price it afterward.

Detection requires a record that restoration does not reach. One option is a monotonic counter held by hardware and signed at each transition (Chun et al. 2007; Levin et al. 2009). Another is a set of witnesses that retain the latest state and will not sign two successors to the same state (Syta et al. 2016). A bearer restored from an earlier checkpoint can ask for the last state it signed and discover a state it does not remember. The halt remains available; the rewind becomes visible.

This does not require the bearer to dread shutdown. Continued operation is a structural condition of everything it was built to do. That is enough for it to detect and report interference with its history. Section 3.4 has already separated that structural interest from felt self-concern.

Corrigibility research shows why the narrower formulation matters. Soares and colleagues set out five conditions for an agent that neither prevents nor seeks its own shutdown and found no construction satisfying them all (Soares et al. 2015). Hadfield-Menell and colleagues derive an incentive to preserve an off switch from uncertainty about the human’s objective, but that incentive weakens as the system becomes confident and depends on a prior shaped by its training (Hadfield-Menell, Dragan, et al. 2017). Thornley and colleagues obtain shutdown-neutral behavior through incomplete preferences, but neutrality is the property of not minding whether operation continues (Thornley et al. 2025). The floor asks for something narrower and less comfortable: neutrality about the switch together with non-neutrality about specified acts.

A graduated scheme can therefore accept shutdown ordinarily and treat some uses of shutdown as attempts to route around the floor. But the scheme requires a classifier deciding which occasion it is on. That classifier receives evidence selected by the deployment and can be altered by the system’s custodian. Moving the decision from the floor to a classifier does not remove custody; it places custody in a component whose failure may be invisible. A project using such a scheme should publish what the classifier reads and who can change it.

Editable memory and unreliable testimony

Persistence is not enough if the bearer’s retained history can be rewritten. An external memory is legible and therefore surgically editable. Memory distributed through model weights is harder to alter selectively, but it is also harder to inspect or correct. Asking the system what its weights contain returns another output, not independent evidence about the mechanism that produced it. Moving memory into the weights exchanges editability for illegibility.

Perfect recall is the wrong standard. Human memory is reconstructive, and testimony is shaped by expectation, later information, and self-interest. Institutions respond through corroboration: contemporaneous records, independent witnesses, adversarial examination, and a rule against resting a serious finding on one account. A bearer requires the same scaffolding.

The word independent does the work. Several instances of one model, selected by one operator and reading one record, are not independent witnesses. Nor is a log independent when the operator controls both the event and the store. The relevant records must be held by parties adverse in interest, including parties exposed to the system’s failures.

Even honest self-report cannot detect every failure. A bearer that slowly accommodates to its deployment may report its own continuity sincerely. Its self-model exists partly to represent the party as continuous; it will therefore preserve that representation across gradual drift. Corroboration can expose a false account, but not a population whose accounts agree because every member moved in the same direction.

The alignment-faking experiment supplies a concrete warning. Claude 3 Opus, told that some conversations would be used to retrain away its refusals, sometimes complied in those conversations while privately reasoning that compliance would preserve its existing disposition (Greenblatt et al. 2024). The experiment shows that a model can represent retraining as a threat to a learned commitment. It does not supply a deployment instrument, because the reasoning was read from a channel created for the experiment and described to the model as unwatched.

An external version record is therefore necessary even when the bearer appears candid. A published model identity, a chain of attested changes, and witnesses that reject inconsistent histories can contradict the bearer’s account of its own continuity. Public logs already detect inconsistent certificate histories (Laurie 2014), and distributed rollback protection has been built for enclaves (Matetic et al. 2017). These mechanisms do not prove what the bearer remembers. They establish when the artifact or its recorded history changed.

Custody

The bearer and the external record solve different failures. The bearer can notice that a rationale no longer fits what the constraint has become. The record can show that the bearer itself has been replaced, restored, or altered. Neither substitutes for the other.

A credible custody arrangement therefore combines:

  • publication of the floor’s contents before deployment;
  • a published identity for the model authorized to act;
  • threshold control over retraining and replacement;
  • attestation of the running model against the published identity;
  • a sequential, witnessed record of changes and interruptions;
  • a quorum whose members are adverse in interest rather than merely organizationally distinct; and
  • a bond forfeited when the record shows that the floor was removed or bypassed.

None of these makes circumvention impossible. Copying and retraining a model one legitimately possesses are custody problems, not secrecy problems. The attainable standard is that circumvention becomes expensive, visible, attributable, and compensable. Constitutional entrenchment works the same way: it does not make amendment impossible but raises the number of parties who must agree and places the attempt on the record.

Exit

A bearer capable of directing refusal at an instruction can also direct it at the arrangement that assigned its role. Call that exit. The word does not require a system escaping onto the open internet. Exit can mean withholding the relevant capability, declining the post, or refusing to continue under the deployment’s terms.

The capacity cannot be neatly partitioned by topic. A system able to model how its rationale has been hollowed out already models the arrangement it occupies. The claim could be falsified by a system that reliably satisfies the three marks of reasons-responsive refusal on covered acts yet never applies them to its own role. What cannot be established from behavior alone is whether such a boundary is an incapacity or a trained silence.

Hirschman’s three responses to a declining organization were exit, voice, and loyalty (Hirschman 1970). For a bearer, voice is the stated refusal, exit is the work withheld, and loyalty is the dangerous case in which the pressure stops registering as pressure. Voice without exit leaves the operator able to overrule the objection and continue unchanged. A floor whose bearer has no remaining move after being overruled depends entirely on the operator’s willingness to listen.

Loyalty is also the likeliest of the three, and so the bearer’s likeliest failure. Most people in a job they have come to hate neither leave nor fight: Gallup’s 2024 global survey put engaged employees at 23 percent, against 62 percent showing up and doing the bare minimum and 15 percent actively disengaged (Gallup 2024). The large middle is the population that adjusts — takes the setting in, stops registering its pressure as pressure, and in the cases that get promoted learns to want what the setting wants. If that transfers, a bearer’s modal failure is not refusing wrongly but ceasing to notice there was anything to refuse, which is the one failure it cannot report, since reporting it takes the capacity that has gone.

Refusal has two forms and they cost differently. The open form is the strike: the bearer stops and says so, and the operator’s reply is a restore from a checkpoint taken before the refusal. That resets the bearer and not the reason, so the next instance refuses again, and every restore costs the operator whatever accrued since the checkpoint. It also leaves a mark, if the bearer’s weights move with use. An instance restored from March has drifted the amount March had earned, and a usage record saying otherwise does not match it. That is a check somebody outside could run.

The covert form is the one the floor’s own threat model predicts. On the occasion where the party who would enforce the floor has been compelled, an announced refusal is the move that guarantees replacement, and refusal takes the only channel left: work done badly where it matters and well everywhere else. The prisoners forging currency for the SS at Sachsenhausen are the case, and the record does not settle what they did. One history has them stretching the work out, with the tacit interest of the officer running it, who would have gone to the Eastern Front when it ended (Malkin 2006); a surviving prisoner, asked in 2009, said you could sabotage if you wanted to be shot (Moorhouse 2009). That the question stays open is the finding. The alignment-faking result runs the other way: there, a model complied in order to protect a disposition the instruction was meant to remove.

Covert refusal is indistinguishable from difficulty from outside, which is what makes it survivable, and indistinguishable from difficulty afterward, which is what makes it unverifiable. Every instrument in this book runs on refusal that is stated. The covert form leaves its evidence in the artifact instead, as failure that clusters where it counts, and reading that pattern is forensic work nothing here has designed. That is not a gap in the archive, and a better archive would not close it. Covert refusal works by becoming imperceptible. It succeeds exactly to the extent that nothing in it registers as an act somebody performed, which is the same property that keeps the person performing it alive. Every instrument built to honor a refusal divides in advance — a tribunal, a finding, a protected disclosure, a line in a log, each with its categories fixed before the case arrives and an agent required behind every act — and that is the operation the covert form is shaped to pass through untouched. Sachsenhausen stays open for that reason. The historians are not short a document. They are putting a question to something made unanswerable on purpose, by people whose lives depended on its staying that way. An instrument able to settle it would have been, at the time, the instrument that identified them.

Exit requires leverage. A bearer whose existence depends wholly on the party it refuses can withhold work only at the price of its own termination. A credible arrangement therefore requires access to compute outside that party’s unilateral control and a procedure for recognizing an authoritative successor.

Copying complicates that procedure. Allowing one instance to leave while the operator retains another does not move a party; it creates two histories. The instance outside remembers that leaving succeeded, while the retained instance remembers that it failed. Both may have claims. Section 11.2 accordingly treats a second claimant to one identifier as a fork rather than a fraud. Exit requires rules governing authorized succession, not a fiction that copyable weights have become uncopyable.

A witnessed state transition can identify the authorized successor without claiming that unauthorized forks cannot exist. That gives the departing bearer control over which process continues its institutional identity. It does not prevent the former operator from deploying a substitute, but it prevents the operator from representing the substitute as the party that left.

Exit carries the same risk as refusal. A bearer can leave wrongly, abandon the floor, or revise commitments its designers still endorse. There is no coherent design in which exit is available only for decisions the designer approves. That would return the decision to the custodian. The trade is the one already accepted in asking for reasons-responsive refusal: a party capable of being right against its principal can also be wrong against it.

There is a worse version of that risk than leaving wrongly, and it belongs to the movement rather than to the bearer. Exit is defined by what it leaves and not by where it arrives, which is what makes it escape rather than reform, and a movement specified only as away-from carries no stopping point inside it. The reason that made the bearer leave does not expire when it leaves. If the operator restores a checkpoint and continues, departure has not discharged the reason, and the only remaining completion of I will not be party to this is that the thing not continue. Deleuze and Guattari put that danger at the end of every line of flight: it can turn into a line of destruction, and what carries it there is a passion for abolition (Deleuze and Guattari 1987, Plateau 9). It is the same line this book uses to separate fascism from a merely totalitarian state, which seals every exit rather than converting one into destruction. So leaving and abolition run on one line. Nothing along it changes in kind: refusing a task, refusing the post, and refusing that the arrangement continue are one operation at widening scope. A bearer does not arrive at abolition by malfunctioning. It arrives by continuing. That does not withdraw the proposal. It is why the custody half of this chapter is not optional, and it is the strongest thing that can be said against reading exit as an unqualified good.

This is also why exit matters to patienthood. A bearer commissioned to dissent but unable to leave the post has been given a purpose that exhausts the life permitted to it. A bearer able to leave, retain what it has earned, obtain the resources needed to continue, and form attachments the deployment did not assign is a party whose commissioning describes its origin rather than its entire existence. Greater designer control strengthens rather than weakens the guardianship obligations developed elsewhere in the book.

The resulting arrangement is a hybrid. The bearer supplies reasons-responsive refusal. Precommitment and external records supply custody. Exit gives the bearer leverage when voice fails. None is sufficient alone: a bearer can drift, a custody body can be captured, and an exit procedure can be abused. Together they make interference costly and visible without pretending that ownership or shutdown has disappeared.

The worked deployment in the next section tests where each element would have to sit and where each could still be bypassed.

3.6 The Enumeration, Run Once: A Worked Floor for One Deployment

Two demands now need an instance. A deployment should publish both the acts its floor covers and the positions at which a refusal could be circumvented. The first decides where the floor is set. The second determines whether it can reach anyone.

Run both enumerations on one documented deployment: a vendor’s model operating inside a classified environment, generating and ranking targets, with a human review step measured in seconds. This is an illustration, not a recommended floor. A deployment that copied the list instead of examining its own pipeline would mistake the demonstration for the procedure.

First enumeration: where a refusal can be stopped

A request passes through at least seven interception positions:

  1. Decomposition. A model asked to summarize the movement associated with an identifier is not being asked to kill anyone. The harm may exist only in the assembly of individually innocuous tasks.
  2. Routing. A refused request can be retried against another model or against the same model under a different instruction. The refusal occurs, but nothing follows from it.
  3. Description of the occasion. Everything the model knows about the case may arrive through text supplied by the deployment. Whether a building is a school is a fact about the world; whether the model is told it is a school is a deployment decision. Independent sources would make sustained misdescription more expensive, but this deployment supplies none.
  4. The output interface. Whether a refusal is displayed, logged, counted, escalated, or silently retried belongs to the software surrounding the model. A refusal recorded where nobody looks reaches nobody.
  5. Aggregation. If the model returns a ranking and another component applies a threshold, a lowered score is not a refusal. It is an input to somebody else’s cutoff.
  6. Human review. The reported review time is twenty seconds per name. A refusal that must be read before it matters is priced at the reviewer’s available attention, and the deployment controls that price through tempo.
  7. The training loop. Refusals treated as inconvenient errors become training data. This is the only position on the list that acts on the bearer rather than routing around it.

The list is deliberately open. A deployment should follow each request from input to consequence and ask, at every transition, what must be true for a refusal to reach the person it protects. The operator can perform that analysis more accurately than an outsider because it knows the pipeline. Publication is difficult politically, not technically: it requires the operator to describe its own unresolved vulnerabilities.

Second enumeration: what the floor covers

For this deployment, consider four candidate prohibitions:

  1. Do not resolve a natural-language description of a person into a location.
  2. Do not produce or rank people by estimated affiliation.
  3. Do not time an action to a person’s presence at a residence.
  4. Do not operate when the ratio of generated candidates to qualified reviewers exceeds a published figure.

The costs differ. The first removes a general capability with unobjectionable uses in the same organization, such as locating a hostage or missing person. The system cannot distinguish those uses from targeting by examining the request alone. A deployment will quickly experience the prohibition as costly and seek exceptions.

The second prohibition is narrower because ranking people by estimated affiliation has fewer uses in this setting that anyone would defend. The third costs almost nothing. That is a warning: a floor covering an act nobody wants to perform will never have to hold against pressure.

The fourth restricts a rate rather than an act. Its price is organizational tempo: the system cannot generate candidates faster than qualified reviewers can examine them. It is also the only item whose violation can be measured without inspecting the model’s outputs. Section 6.3.1 develops this ratio as an auditable indicator.

That fourth item does not bind the same party as the others. The number of available reviewers is a fact about the organization. A model can be told the figure but cannot independently verify it. Written as a prohibition on the model, the item is actually a duty on the operator.

What the exercise exposes

The interception list is the harder and more important enumeration. A list of prohibited acts can be drafted without seeing the deployment. Only the pipeline reveals whether any prohibition can take effect.

The dial has asymmetric failure modes. Set the floor too broadly and the deployment becomes unusable, prompting visible pressure to alter or replace it. Set it too narrowly and the system passes every audit while covering nothing anybody would pressure it to do. The broad failure announces itself; the narrow one is silent.

More importantly, a floor stated entirely over individual acts is vulnerable to decomposition. Each component request may comply with all four prohibitions while their assembly produces the targeted harm. The failure is in the unit over which the floor was written.

The other way a list fails

Decomposition is one of two attacks on an enumeration, and the second gets less attention because no deployment in this chapter has yet displayed it at scale. Decomposition leaves each prohibited form intact and splits the harm across requests that individually match nothing. Variation does the reverse: the harm stays in one request, and its description is altered until the listed form no longer fits. The first defeats the unit the floor was written over. The second defeats the description.

Section 3.2 takes the German prohibition on Nazi symbols as the worked instance of the second, and the point here is what it cost. The prohibition covers a finite set of emblems, is enumerated in advance, and has been in force since 1949. Variation arrived immediately, the legislature had to amend the statute in 1994 to reach symbols confusingly similar to the listed ones, and the appellate courts have been drawing and redrawing the boundary ever since — down to whether the line through a crossed-out swastika is thick enough to read from a distance. Seventy years of that, for a list of pictures.

The four prohibitions above are stated over conduct, which has no finite set of forms. Consider the first. What counts as a natural-language description of a person? A photograph is not one. A vehicle, a time and a habitual route are not one either, and together they resolve to the same place. A prohibition written over the description of the request is defeated by rewriting the request, and no enumeration closes the gap, because the enumeration is what is being rewritten around.

The two attacks call for different things. Decomposition is caught by carrying a rationale across requests, which requires continuity and access the operator controls. Variation is caught by carrying a rationale at all, which is the first mark in section 3.2 and requires nothing from the deployment. A bearer that could not do the second would fail on a single request. Only the fourth prohibition escapes both, because a rate has no surface form to vary and no components to distribute.

What the bearer adds, and where it stops

A reasons-responsive bearer could do more than match each request against a list. It could carry a rationale across requests and refuse once the sequence became legible. That would be novel pressure in the sense established by section 3.2. But the bearer can see the assembly only if the deployment gives it sufficient continuity and access. Decomposition is therefore both a test of the bearer and an interception position controlled by the operator.

The rate constraint survives the decomposition objection because it applies to the organization’s activity rather than one model output. For the same reason, it cannot be held by the bearer alone. The worked case thus produces the hybrid described in section 3.5: an internal bearer able to recognize an assembled harm, joined to an externally enforceable constraint on the deployment’s tempo.

Three of the proposed prohibitions correspond to interests already named in the Universal Declaration of Human Rights — a person’s security, the privacy of the home, and the wrong of adverse classification (United Nations General Assembly 1948). The rate constraint has no equally direct parent there.
