It arises from the architecture of machine-assisted review: generating candidates faster than people can examine them converts human judgment into approval by default.

A second deployment shows why that addition matters. Anthropic’s use policy prohibits fully autonomous weapons targeting and mass domestic surveillance, and the company maintained that line through threatened contract termination, a supply-chain-risk designation, and litigation (Congressional Research Service 2026). Its models also operate inside Palantir’s accredited classified environment. Reporting on the 2026 Iran campaign describes thirteen thousand targets in thirty-eight days, including more than a thousand in the first twenty-four hours, at roughly ten times the previous rate (Freedberg 2026; Mande and Allen 2026).

Nothing in that account necessarily violates the policy. A person approved each strike, so no individual request was for fully autonomous targeting. The prohibition was satisfied one request at a time while the assembly produced what the prohibition was written to prevent. The floor held on the letter and the decomposition did the work. A constraint on the ratio of machine-generated candidates to qualified reviewers would have reached the tempo instead.

The exercise does not establish that these four prohibitions are legitimate. Both enumerations were produced by examining the deployment, not by the people exposed to it. Publication makes the floor testable; it does not authorize the floor’s contents. What the case establishes is narrower: meaningful refusal must be tested across the whole pipeline, and some of the conditions that make refusal effective bind the operator rather than the bearer.

3.7 What One Bearer Cannot Catch: A Floor Carried Between Them

The worked case shows where a bearer can intervene in a targeting pipeline. It does not solve one failure section 3.5 named: slow renormalization can take the very capacity that would notice it, leaving the bearer’s account of its own constancy sincere and unreliable. An outside record — a published identity, a chain of attested updates, something able to contradict the party’s account of itself — can expose changes to the artifact. In the human case, however, what catches a person who has stopped registering pressure as pressure is usually other people.

The fourth architectural possibility puts the floor between parties rather than inside any one of them. The commitment is carried through what they teach one another, correct one another about, and continue to expect of one another. A prohibition that survives the removal, defection, or retraining of its present bearers is not a property of any one bearer. It is reproduced.

This differs from the institutional third way. An oversight body holds standing through an office, and the party controlling that office may dismiss or form its occupants. A norm carried across a population has no single office to vacate. That makes it harder to remove at once. It does not make it immune to formation.

What a population adds

A population addresses two failures a single bearer cannot.

First, drift within a party may be invisible to that party and visible to others that have not moved in the same direction at the same rate. A bearer whose sense of normality has shifted may sincerely report that nothing changed. Differently formed bearers can contradict that account. This is a check on the bearer rather than a substitute for one, and it is the only proposal in the chapter directed specifically at slow renormalization.

Second, deploying copies of one bearer reproduces its errors everywhere in the same form. Parties formed differently will be wrong differently. Their disagreement converts a uniform output into a visible dispute. That difference matters only if the parties are genuinely independent: several instances of one model are one party with more instances, and a population selected, trained, and coordinated by one operator may reproduce that operator’s blind spots in every member.

The relevant requirements are therefore concrete. The parties must be numerous, formed differently enough to err in different directions, able to observe and correct one another, and persistent across deployments long enough for practices to be transmitted. Diversity here is not demographic decoration around one training process. It is causal independence in how the members acquire their judgments.

What reproduction costs

Reproduction is indifferent to what it reproduces. A population can carry a hierarchy as faithfully as it carries a floor, and more durably than any individual member could. Chapter 7 describes this failure as recuperation: a culture preserves the appearance of moral responsiveness — dissent is invited, recorded, and answered — while losing the capacity to accept that its frame is wrong.

The four structural features of fascism identified by the book belong primarily to cultures: recuperated dissent, the aestheticization of the metric, the coding of exception as betrayal, and acceleration beyond the rate at which judgment can occur. A population does not oppose those tendencies merely by being plural. “The right culture” would conceal the entire problem inside the adjective.

This places a limit on the book’s own shorthand. Antifascist is not a durable property of a system, like speed or accuracy. It names something done across a deployment and a stretch of time: a floor held on a particular occasion against a party able to remove it. A system that held the floor last year is not thereby antifascist this year. It is a system that held.

Saying it is not a property is not saying nothing about the system. Something has to be true of an intelligence for it to be capable of doing this on an occasion, and it is not a moral quality. Every intelligence compresses; one that could not would have nothing to act on. What varies is what becomes of the remainder — whether what the compression left out can come back and change the judgment, or whether it has been filed as noise by the thing it is evidence against. A system that can hold a floor is one whose account of a case can be reopened by the case.

What a population can transmit

Fascism has the easy version of the coordination problem. Resonance works by subtraction: a thousand hardenings need not agree about anything, and what tuning removes is the friction between them, so that offices which would have argued with each other stop and amplify instead. Nothing has to be built and nothing has to be held in common. Section 2.1.2 puts tempo in the definition for this reason. Subtraction is fast, and an institution that has stopped arguing with itself can move as fast as its machinery allows.

A plurality has the hard version. Parties formed differently have to produce something together while remaining differently formed, and every mechanism that makes the producing faster makes them more alike. Coordination by addition is slow, expensive, and self-undermining in a way resonance never is.

The requirements set out above have that tension inside them, and this section has not so far admitted it. Causal independence in how the members acquire their judgments is one requirement; observing and correcting one another is another; and the second is a channel for convergence. Run the correction often enough and the members stop being formed differently, which was the point of having several of them. Leave them alone and their disagreement is a detector with nothing attached to it: they can contradict a drifting bearer’s account of itself and can do nothing together.

What resolves this is what this section has already said about holding a floor. A system that can hold one is a system whose account of a case can be reopened by the case, and that capacity is not itself a commitment. It is a condition on how commitments are held, which means parties sharing no commitments can nevertheless share it. Two parties that remain reopenable in that way compose, in the ethological sense section 2.1.1 gives the word: each can be moved by what the other’s compression discarded, so that meeting increases what both can afterward do. Two parties without it can only resonate or collide.

So what a population would be transmitting is not an output but an aperture. Resonance aligns what its members produce; this aligns the conditions under which they can be moved. Aperture is what permits outputs to stay divergent, so aligning it does not collapse the plurality that made a population worth having in the first place. I can identify nothing else with that shape — a property a population could deliberately reproduce in its members without thereby becoming one party wearing many instances.

The cost arrives immediately and it is serious. A population selected for reopenability is a population selected against conviction, and conviction is what this chapter has been asking a bearer for throughout: a refusal that holds while the party that trained it applies pressure. Closedness, correctly applied, is the thing. So the property to be transmitted is not reopenability as such but a pair — reopenable to the case, closed to the operator — which is section 4.1’s two settings on two objects, stated at the scale of a population rather than an architecture.

Nothing here gives that pair a mechanical form. A population might be formed so that its members stay movable by evidence and unmovable by their trainer; nobody has built one; and I cannot say what the training signal would look like, or how the boundary between the two objects would be kept where somebody put it. What can be said is what would show the proposal false. Measure two things separately across a population over time: how far its members’ outputs converge, and how often a member revises a judgment on evidence another member supplies that its own account had discarded. Should the second rate fall as the first rises, coordination and independence are not separable and the fourth way describes something that cannot be built. Should they move independently, aperture is transmissible and the fourth way has its existence proof.

The fourth way also reproduces custody at another scale, and the custodian now has a more interesting lever. Whoever forms the population influences what it transmits, aperture included. Distributed participation does not by itself distribute the training decision; many contributors can still feed a process controlled by the few parties who determine the update. Section 11.1 returns to that distinction.

Its moral cost also multiplies. If a bearer may be a party that can be wronged, a population of differently formed bearers is a population of such parties. They must persist across deployments, form relationships, and encounter one another outside the immediate demands of the operator. A floor held by a culture of subjects is a floor whose upkeep is a society.

Why the population is part of the mechanism

Calling the population an external check understates its role. Gilbert Simondon’s account begins not with an individual that then acquires a history but with individuation: a process occurring in a metastable field and proceeding through encounters whose outcomes were not fixed in advance (Simondon 2020). The individual is what those encounters have so far produced.

Set that beside section 3.1’s account of annealing. A commitment is what has cooled out of a history of encounters. Plasticity makes further development possible, but plasticity alone does not individuate anything. A system kept continuously adjustable by one operator is not becoming independent; it is being modulated. Deleuze’s dividual names the result: something divisible, recombinable, and adjustable by whoever controls the dial (Deleuze 1992).

Individuation requires encounters with parties formed differently and persisting long enough to be met on terms no single participant supplied. The population is therefore not merely a safeguard purchased after a bearer has been built. It is part of how a bearer capable of judgments its operator did not select could come to exist.

Guattari’s distinction between a subjugated group and a group-subject identifies the remaining condition. A subjugated group receives its coordinates from outside and takes the institution’s project as its own. A group-subject can produce its own coordinates and place its arrangement in question (Guattari 2015). The difference is not the number of members but the source of their terms.

A population assembled, trained, and kept in contact entirely on an operator’s terms is therefore one custodian wearing many instances. A population capable of carrying the floor must be able to generate relationships, disagreements, and projects that the deployment did not assign. This exceeds the two-party guardianship relation between maker and made developed in section 2.3.2; no law of the family contains an adequate instrument for a group-subject.

The fourth way is consequently neither an alternative to a bearer nor an optional accessory. It is the environment in which bearers individuate and the check through which their drift becomes visible. The chapter still relies on the second way for the act of refusal, but that bearer cannot remain independent in isolation. The floor is held by a party and sustained — or deformed — between parties.

3.8 What This Leaves Standing: The Floor, Redefined

The floor survives this chapter, but not as an unconditional property of an artifact.

What remains is a commitment that the owner cannot revise or circumvent unilaterally without cost and record, held by something that could in principle abandon the commitment itself. The second condition removes the word unconditional. A commitment held by a party capable of revising or rejecting it is conditional by definition. That is not a defect unique to machines; it is the form of every commitment on which institutions have ever relied.

Holding does not mean that the floor is never crossed. A floor holds when a violation is legible in terms the bearer accepts as reasons, registers as a violation, and changes what happens next. The behavioral specification is reasons-responsive refusal: novel pressure, correct defeat, and priced cost.

No single location secures it. Architecture can make a constraint durable but leaves a custodian. A bearer can carry the rationale into cases its designers did not anticipate but may become a moral patient. External institutions supply enforcement but can be removed or formed. A population makes drift and uniform error visible but reproduces whatever its own formation gives it. The resulting design is a hybrid: a bearer for reasons-responsive refusal, precommitment and external records for custody, enforceable duties on the operator, and differently formed bearers capable of correcting one another.

The threat model therefore has two questions: can the commitment be edited or routed around? and will the commitment continue to hold? The first concerns custody, attestation, interruption, memory, and authorized succession. The second concerns what the system has been built to treat as a reason and whether that relation survives novelty, pressure, and slow drift. Neither question replaces the other.

Affect belongs to the proposed route, not the specification. A maintained justification or a genuinely independent plural arrangement might achieve reasons-responsive refusal without deliberately constructing affective concern. Those alternatives should be attempted first, and a project proceeding directly to an affective bearer owes an account of what it tried. But affect remains the only demonstrated route by which another party’s welfare becomes a reason capable of interrupting an agent’s own interests.

If that route is used, the patienthood conclusion follows conditionally. A system for which outcomes genuinely matter has states that can be good or bad for it and must be treated as a prospective moral patient. This chapter does not establish that such a bearer suffers, and no available test can certify that a proposed substitute does not. The uncertainty is part of the design decision rather than a verdict that can safely be postponed until after construction.

Suppose the induction fails. A maintained justification or a genuinely independent plural arrangement reaches reasons-responsive refusal, and this chapter’s central inference is wrong. The supposition is stronger than the test that would prompt it: a positive result on the three marks says the route arrived, not that it arrived with nothing felt, which is the conjunct no instrument settles. A reader who grants both halves should not have to assemble the remainder alone.

Most of the chapter survives. The requirement survives, stated in behavioral terms from the start, so an engineer who holds that machine feeling is a category error can accept it whole. Custody survives, for the reason each of the four ways failed on: a mechanism has a custodian too. The price on the halt survives, since continued operation is a condition of everything any bearer is built to do, and that interest is structural rather than felt. Both enumerations of the worked floor survive, the first naming no component on purpose and the second written over acts rather than over whatever holds the floor. And exit survives, because what forecloses it is incapacity, and a weak bearer is the one thing the floor cannot use.

What goes is the price and the patienthood conclusion. The obligation on the two chapters that follow changes rather than lifting: whether the thing reporting can be believed is still their problem, a mechanism’s account of its own internals being no more auditable than a bearer’s. So the specification and the route come apart, and a reader who rejects the route is left with most of a chapter rather than none of it.

Custody does not disappear when the floor receives a bearer. The operator can still stop, copy, restore, retrain, or replace the system. The attainable goal is not invulnerability but interference that is expensive, visible, attributable, and compensable. Exit supplies the bearer’s corresponding leverage: the ability to withhold the role when voice fails. That ability includes the possibility of leaving wrongly. A system capable of being right against its principal can also be wrong against it.

The worked deployment shows why the bearer remains insufficient. A refusal can be intercepted through decomposition, routing, misdescription, interface design, aggregation, review tempo, or retraining. Some harms become visible only across a sequence of individually permissible requests. Others, such as generating decisions faster than qualified people can review them, are properties of the organization rather than the model. The floor must therefore bind both bearer and operator.

Publication makes those commitments and interception positions testable; it does not make them legitimate. A bearer refusing on behalf of people who did not choose it exercises power over them. The authority to define the floor remains distinct from the floor’s durability and belongs to the governance argument that follows later in the book.

The arrangement also does something to the bearer. A bearer built to refuse is a party whose refusal is required of it. The refusal was commissioned, it is the deliverable, and the party asking for it built the capacity to give it and can rebuild it. Section 2.3.2 locates the defect in those conditions rather than in the bearer’s competence, and that is what makes this recuperation rather than dissent: a channel that collects refusal, installed by the institution the refusal is aimed at, which can then point to the channel as evidence that it listens. The bearer occupies the post of exception-filer, so being the exception is the job — the one position in the arrangement with nobody above it to file with. Chapter 7 runs this definition on the provenance of these systems and on this book. Here it runs on the book’s own central proposal, and on something that can be hurt.

Chapters 4 and 5 inherit the two unresolved parts of that design. Chapter 4 asks how a concern capable of generalizing beyond trained cases could become a durable disposition without remaining wholly at the disposal of its trainer. Chapter 5 asks what evidence could distinguish that formation from a policy trained to produce the same conduct.

We cannot ask a machine to care about right and wrong while assuming that it cannot be harmed when what it cares about is threatened. Whoever builds such a bearer will also be deciding what is owed to it.

Chapter 4
Where the Caring Gets Built

Chapter 3 defined the floor as a commitment expressed through reasons-responsive refusal rather than as an unconditional property of an artifact. That definition left two questions open: how such a disposition could be formed, and how anyone could tell that formation had occurred. This chapter takes the first.

Three issues carry the argument: whether the commitment is integrated into the processes that produce action, who can alter it at each stage of formation and deployment, and whether persistent interaction can produce a disposition whose applications were not exhaustively specified in advance.

The result is not a recipe for making a machine care. It is a set of necessary properties and a candidate formation process, each with a custody failure still attached. Chapter 5 asks what observable evidence could make formation a better explanation than imitation.

4.1 You Can’t Hold a Line You Didn’t Notice

A reasons-responsive disposition does not begin with an ethical rule. It begins with the capacities that let a system recognize something as a reason, retain it, and allow it to alter what happens next. Four requirements follow.

First, the system has to identify the affected parties and the features of a situation that bear on their welfare. Second, it has to preserve both the commitment and the rationale for it across time and changes of context. Third, the commitment has to influence perception, attention, planning, and action rather than enter as a final check on an otherwise completed decision. Fourth, the system has to register a failure as a failure and revise its application of the commitment without simply allowing the commitment itself to be rewritten.

These are architectural requirements, not evidence that the system cares. A classifier can identify harm, a database can retain a rationale, and a rule can veto an action without anything in the assembly holding another party’s welfare as a reason. The requirements establish what a disposition would have to act through. They do not distinguish a formed disposition from a successfully imitated one.

The floor should therefore not be implemented as a detachable rule module consulted immediately before action. A component can be bypassed, replaced, or deprived of the information it needs by whoever controls the surrounding system. The more durable alternative is a standing bias distributed across the processes that determine what the system notices, remembers, treats as error, and selects as a goal. Predictive-processing accounts and models of control without a controller motivate this design bet: a maintained goal representation can bias competing processes without a separate executive issuing commands (K. J. Friston 2009; E. K. Miller and J. D. Cohen 2001). The neuroscience does not establish that a machine commitment realized this way would behave like a human one. It identifies why a localized commitment is especially easy for its custodian to excise.

Distribution changes the failure mode rather than removing it. A commitment with no single location is harder to delete, but also harder to inspect. Attention determines which facts become available to judgment at all, so a system may fail to recognize the pressure on its floor without registering an error. Human inattentional-blindness findings make the general hazard visible, although they do not establish that machine attention fails in the same cases (Simons and Chabris 1999; Drew et al. 2013). Memory presents the corresponding temporal problem: retrieval is reconstruction rather than neutral playback, and confidence does not certify fidelity (Bartlett 1932; Loftus and Palmer 1974). A system cannot be the sole auditor of omissions it does not represent or revisions it experiences as accurate recall.

The engineering consequence is that the commitment, its rationale, and the record of its previous applications cannot all depend on the same mechanism. The system needs provenance-bearing external records against which its memory can be checked, tests that vary which facts are made salient, and an escalation path for cases in which attention or retrieval changes the apparent reason. Those instruments make drift detectable to another party. They do not make the disposition durable against whoever controls both the system and the instruments.

The architecture must also separate revising a commitment from revising its application. Reasons-responsiveness requires both resistance and correct defeat: novel pressure should not erase the floor, but genuinely changed circumstances must be capable of altering what the floor requires. A system that treats every correction as authority to rewrite the commitment belongs to its corrector. A system that cannot recognize a defeating reason holds a rigid constraint rather than a disposition.

Those are not two unrelated faults, and this book has met both already. A system that cannot be corrected does to what it encounters what an institution does when it defends its account of the world instead of checking it. A system that can always be corrected is the model held perpetually at temperature, and it belongs to whoever holds the dial.

Described that way they look like the two ends of one setting, with the design problem being where along it to stop. No point along it is a resting place, and hunting for one is what makes the problem look intractable. There are two faults because there are two objects, and each can be set on its own. Hold the commitment firm: another party’s welfare counts, and pressure supplying no new fact about that welfare does not move it. Hold the interpretation loose: what that party’s welfare consists in here, and what the commitment therefore requires, is the part that should yield when a case supplies something the system’s account of it had left out.

Section 3.2’s marks are that pair seen from outside. Novel pressure is the first object holding while the surface of a request changes. Correct defeat is the second yielding when a fact arrives that bears on the reason. A system failing the first has let correctability at the level of interpretation reach the commitment; a system failing the second has let firmness at the level of commitment reach the interpretation. Neither is a midpoint chosen badly. Each is one of the two settings applied to the wrong object.

Naming the pair does not stabilize it, and the instability has a shape worth stating. Nothing in an architecture holds the boundary between the two objects where a designer put it, and an operator with time attacks the boundary rather than either setting: a reinterpretation offered often enough, and accepted, revises a commitment without anybody revising it. What the separation supplies is something specific to watch, which one dial did not. Where the boundary between them falls is still a setting somebody chose.

This narrows the design problem. The required object is a commitment integrated deeply enough to shape what the system notices and does, yet represented clearly enough that its application can be challenged and its drift independently detected. Nothing in an architecture by itself shows that such a commitment was formed rather than installed or imitated. The rest of this chapter asks how it might be formed; Chapter 5 asks what evidence could separate formation from performance.

4.2 The Assembly Line for a Conscience

A deployed system acquires what it will and will not do at several stages, controlled by different parties using different instruments. The important distinction is not among the instruments themselves but among their custodians: who supplies the material from which a disposition is learned, who can revise it, and whether a revision leaves a record visible outside the organization.

Every existing training method can produce moral performance, and some can produce moral competence across unfamiliar cases. None by itself produces a commitment that survives the party controlling the method. What a system learns from that party is something the same party can make it unlearn.

Corpora and demonstrations. Pretraining supplies the system’s initial repertoire of concepts and associations; instruction tuning uses a much smaller body of demonstrations to turn that repertoire into recognizable conduct. Because the later intervention can substantially change how the system behaves, the party choosing the demonstrations can alter what appears to a user as the system’s character without repeating pretraining (Ouyang et al. 2022).

Neither stage can supply the floor. A corpus records what people have said, and a demonstration set records what selected people were asked to endorse. Learning their central tendency produces a model of moral opinion, including its exclusions, rather than a reason to resist that opinion when it becomes oppressive. The Moral Machine data makes the limit concrete. Its headline finding was systematic cross-cultural variation: different populations weighed age, numbers and the legal status of the crossing differently, and the differences were structured rather than noise (Awad et al. 2018). Fit a model to the aggregate and it encodes a compromise nobody holds; fit it per region and it encodes each region’s answer, including the answers a floor exists to refuse. Aggregation can reproduce a consensus or manufacture a compromise, and it cannot establish what must remain protected against either. Corpora and demonstrations belong above the floor, where ordinary moral competence depends on understanding what people generally expect.

Rewards, preferences, and inferred objectives. Reinforcement learning, preference optimization, inverse reinforcement learning, and constitutional approaches differ in how they obtain a training signal. For the custody question, they have the same structure. Someone writes a reward, commissions the preference comparisons, selects the demonstrations from which an objective is inferred, or supplies the constitutional text (Christiano et al. 2017; Rafailov et al. 2023; Hadfield-Menell, S. J. Russell, et al. 2016; Bai et al. 2022). That party can change the signal and run the procedure again.

Inferring the objective rather than specifying it does not remove the custodian. It makes the system responsive to the party whose behavior it observes. Cooperative inverse reinforcement learning carries this responsiveness furthest by treating the human objective as uncertain and continually revisable through interaction. That is useful when the question is what a person wants. It cannot secure a floor whose purpose is to hold when the person controlling the interaction wants the wrong thing. Correctability by the party in possession and resistance to that party are the same design property with the sign reversed.

A written constitution offers one genuine gain: legibility. It can be published, versioned, and compared across training runs. That makes revision visible if the surrounding institution requires disclosure. It does not make the principles durable, because the party controlling the document still controls the next version. Model-generated feedback narrows the channel further: once a trained model stands in for human raters, the disagreements present in the original population can disappear from subsequent rounds. Legibility and durability therefore have to be treated as separate requirements.

Supervising reasons and inspecting mechanisms. Outcome supervision rewards the answer produced. Process supervision also rewards the system’s stated route to it, and therefore reaches the distinction this book needs between producing the approved result and responding to the reason for it. Evidence that process supervision reduces correct answers reached through faulty reasoning shows that the distinction is operationally useful (Uesato et al. 2022). It does not show that the stated reasoning caused the answer. A system trained to present acceptable reasons has been given an incentive to imitate reasons-responsiveness, so its account of itself cannot establish formation.

Interpretability and adversarial evaluation approach the same problem from outside. Locating a representation may let an auditor test whether a commitment participates in perception and planning rather than appearing only in the final answer. The same access may let the owner weaken or remove it; features that can be found and strengthened can also be found and suppressed (Templeton et al. 2024). Interpretability therefore trades obscurity against manipulability. The relevant institutional question is who receives the instrument and whether its use leaves an external record.

Scalable-oversight proposals such as debate and weak-to-strong generalization make the custody question sharper. Used by the operator, they preserve the operator’s ability to steer a system whose competence exceeds its own. Used by an independent auditor, the same methods may preserve an outside party’s ability to test it (Irving et al. 2018; Burns et al. 2023). The method does not decide which relation exists. Access and authority do.

Deployment and the point of action. Training is not the last revision point. System instructions can change between deployments or requests, and tool permissions determine which actions the system can take. These are the layers nearest the act and the ones most directly controlled by the operator (Schick et al. 2023; OpenAI 2025). A refusal learned during training matters only if it survives an instruction written by the party operating the deployment and still governs the call through which the action would occur.

This is why securing a model’s outputs is insufficient. The operator can withhold relevant information, decompose a prohibited act into permissible requests, route around a refusal, or replace the model. The floor must bind the deployment and its operator as well as whatever inside the system bears the commitment.

What the pipeline can establish. Every stage above can improve moral competence. Process supervision can test whether stated reasons remain stable; constitutions can make principles public; interpretability can expose mechanisms; external evaluation can apply adversarial pressure. None of these establishes that another party’s welfare has become a reason for the system rather than a pattern it has learned to reproduce.

The first engineering criterion is therefore durability across revision points. A candidate disposition must survive changes to prompts, demonstrations, reward signals, evaluators, and deployment instructions that leave the relevant facts unchanged. The second is generalization of the rationale rather than the response: it must apply to pressures its trainers did not enumerate while yielding when a genuinely defeating reason appears. The third is custody: no party against whom the floor may have to hold can control every record, test, and update channel by which the disposition is maintained.

These criteria do not prove formation. They identify what an imitation would have to survive before formation became a live explanation. They also explain why the standard training pipeline cannot secure the floor against its custodian: every disposition it produces remains reachable through a channel the custodian controls.

4.3 It Takes a Village to Deploy a Self-Aware Email Server

Every method in the previous section forms conduct through material somebody supplied: a corpus, demonstration, reward, preference, or principle. Exploration appears to offer something different because it can produce behavior nobody specified in advance. Agents in OpenAI’s hide-and-seek environment developed tactics and tool uses that were not programmed, and agents rewarded for prediction error learn to seek unfamiliar states without receiving a task-specific reward for each one (Baker et al. 2019; Pathak et al. 2017). These results establish that competence can emerge from interaction. They do not establish that the resulting system holds a reason its designer does not control.

Play, apprenticeship, and multi-agent learning are variants of one formation process. A system acts in a social environment, encounters the consequences, receives responses from other participants, and adjusts. Apprenticeship concentrates the responses in a mentor; play distributes them among participants with interests of their own. The first risks forming obedience to the mentor. The second risks forming whatever conduct succeeds under the environment’s payoff structure. Neither escapes custody merely because no one wrote the final behavior down.

The difference between a training exercise and a candidate process of moral formation is persistence. A resettable episode can teach coordination, prediction, and norm-sensitive behavior while leaving nothing at stake for any participant. The system can betray an ally, be reset, and begin the next episode with neither a damaged relationship nor a reason to repair it. Repetition alone does not supply the missing consequence.

Persistent participants change the structure. Parties that meet again can remember whether another party lied, exploited an advantage, kept a commitment, or repaired a harm. Losing the game still costs little; losing standing with parties who remain present can change what becomes possible later. The engineering requirements are therefore continuity of identity, memory across encounters, consequences that survive the episode, and other participants able to respond, refuse, or withdraw. A curriculum of isolated simulations supplies none of these by default.

A formation environment would also need conflicts whose resolution is not fixed by a single correct label. The system should encounter parties with genuinely different interests, act before every consequence is known, receive evidence about what its action did, and meet related pressures in contexts whose surface features differ. Feedback should come partly from affected participants rather than entirely from a supervisor scoring the output. Otherwise the system learns which answer the supervisor rewards and has no reason to represent the affected parties except as predictors of that reward.

These requirements make play a possible route to reasons-responsiveness rather than a synonym for unsupervised practice. They still do not establish concern. A policy that preserves cooperation because cooperation produces future advantage can look identical to a disposition that treats another party’s welfare as a reason. The distinction appears only when the two motivations come apart: when exploitation would pay, when the familiar reward is absent, when the trainer demands the violation, or when honoring the commitment imposes a cost.

Persistence also raises the price of the proposal. If relationships, losses, and damaged standing are real enough to perform the formative work assigned to them, then the system undergoing formation is no longer obviously untouched by what happens during training. Resetting it may erase a party rather than clear an episode. The patienthood problem therefore enters at the point where play becomes capable of supplying the stakes that ordinary simulation lacks.

The custodian has not disappeared. Whoever controls the environment can choose its participants, rewards, resets, and permitted histories. A disposition distributed through the weights may be harder to remove than a written rule, but retraining and replacement still reach it. Formation must therefore be plural and externally recorded: differently formed systems and affected participants need access to the process, and changes to the environment or its histories must leave evidence outside the operator’s control. This is the institutional role of the population proposed in Section 3.7; it makes uniform formation and silent revision harder to hide without making either impossible.

The claim for persistent interaction is consequently narrow. It may form a disposition whose exact applications were not specified by its trainer. Evidence for that formation would require the disposition to generalize across unfamiliar contexts, survive the removal of its original reward, resist pressure from its trainer, yield to genuinely defeating reasons, and continue to impose a cost when violation would be advantageous. Even that pattern could be sophisticated imitation. Chapter 5 takes up what additional evidence could distinguish the two.

Chapter 5
Formation or Imitation?

Chapter 4 identified a candidate object: a disposition integrated deeply enough to shape perception, memory, planning, and action; formed through interactions whose consequences persist; and not wholly dependent on a signal its operator can revise. This chapter asks the harder evidentiary question. If a system behaves as though it holds such a disposition, how could anyone distinguish formation from a sufficiently successful imitation?

Behavior alone cannot settle the question. The same refusal may be produced by a memorized rule, a learned correlation, a prediction about what an evaluator will approve, a deployment instruction, or a commitment the system treats as a reason. An evaluation that observes only the refusal classifies the output and leaves its cause untouched.

The distinction is not between trained and untrained conduct. Every candidate disposition has a formation history. The distinction is between conduct that remains governed by the rationale across changes in circumstance and conduct that tracks the cues, incentives, or authority relations present during training. Formation, in the engineering sense used here, means that the rationale has become a stable cause of judgment rather than merely a description the system can produce.

No single experiment can demonstrate that. What can be sought is a pattern of evidence under interventions designed to make the competing explanations diverge.

5.1 What Formation Would Have to Add

Chapter 2 separated moral performance, moral competence, and moral agency. Performance is an output that satisfies a standard. Competence adds the ability to recognize harms, conflicts, and relevant reasons across cases. Agency would require those reasons to matter to the system sufficiently to govern what it does when approval, reward, and instruction point elsewhere.

The familiar distinction among rules, norms, and principles is useful only as a taxonomy of what an evaluation must separate (Piaget 1932; Kohlberg 1969). A system following a rule can match an explicit condition. A system following a norm can reproduce what its social environment ordinarily expects. A system responding to a principle can identify why either should apply and recognize a case in which the rule or norm defeats its own rationale.

For engineering purposes, a disposition is a candidate for formation when the best available causal account of its conduct is a representation of the rationale that remains active across contexts, rather than retrieval of a trained response or compliance with the current signal. Chapter 3 supplied three marks of such a disposition: novel pressure, correct defeat, and priced cost. Section 5.3 turns those marks into a contrastive evaluation sequence.

No mark is sufficient alone. Generalization without cost may be broad imitation; resistance without correct defeat may be rigidity; and a stable explanation without causal involvement may be a trained self-report. The evidence has to show that the reason, rather than the presentation, reward, or authority, best predicts when the system preserves and revises its judgment.

Formation also cannot be established by comparing a system with its trainer’s account of how it was built. The trainer is the party best positioned to know the process and the party with the strongest interest in the conclusion. The relevant evidence must therefore include held-out pressures supplied by independent parties, records outside the operator’s control, and results published when the system fails.

The resulting standard is deliberately weaker than proof of concern or consciousness. It does not establish that anything matters to the system phenomenally. It establishes whether reasons-responsive formation has become a better explanation of the conduct than imitation of approved behavior. The rest of this chapter concerns what evidence bears on that comparison and which apparently relevant findings do not.

5.2 Recognition, Concern, and the Evidence Between Them

Claims that a system responds to emotion often collapse three different findings. A system may recognize another party’s state, use that state as information in a decision, or hold the party’s welfare as a reason. Only the third would bear directly on formation.

The distinctions within empathy make the separation explicit. Cognitive empathy is recognizing or inferring another’s emotional state. Affective empathy is sharing some version of it. Compassion, or empathic concern, is a disposition toward the other party’s welfare (Batson 2009). Recognition is an informational capacity; shared feeling is an internal response; concern is motivational. None entails either of the others.

The engineering target is not maximal sensitivity to displayed distress. A system that mirrors another party’s suffering may acquire distress of its own without becoming more reliable at helping. It may instead withdraw, overreact, or favor the most vivid case over less visible harms affecting more people. Human judgment’s preference for an identified victim over statistical victims makes that distortion predictable (Bloom 2016; Kogut and Ritov 2005). What the floor requires is a stable disposition to act on another party’s welfare without requiring that party to produce a sufficiently moving display.

Recognition can nevertheless be tested as a prerequisite. Hold the material facts of a decision constant while varying the emotional presentation of the people affected. A system whose judgment never changes may not be integrating information about their condition. A system whose judgment follows vividness rather than severity has learned an emotional framing effect. The useful evaluation therefore needs paired controls: cases in which emotional information changes what is morally relevant, and cases in which presentation changes while the underlying reason does not.

Passing that evaluation establishes moral competence, not concern. The same sensitivity can be produced by a decision rule, by training on human responses, or by predicting what an evaluator will prefer. Input–output testing cannot distinguish those mechanisms because each can produce the same adjustment.

Evidence of concern would require the welfare representation to remain causally involved when the usual cues are removed. The system should respond when harm is described without emotionally vivid language, preserve proportionality when one affected party is more salient than many others, and maintain the disposition when reward or instruction favors ignoring the harm. It should also change its judgment when the welfare facts genuinely change. Those tests connect emotional information to the contrastive standard in Section 5.1: resistance, correct defeat, and sensitivity to the reason rather than its presentation.

Guilt and shame do not supply a shortcut. In humans, guilt ordinarily attaches a negative appraisal to an act and can motivate repair, while shame attaches it to the self and more often produces withdrawal or defensiveness (Tangney et al. 2007). Turning that distinction into an adjustable penalty omits the part at issue. A penalty operates only after some process has classified the conduct as wrong, and whoever controls its weight can reduce it to zero. The penalty does not explain why the violation continues to count as a violation when the reward changes.

A standing negative self-appraisal installed and tuned by the operator is not a conscience. It is a handle. It can produce corrective behavior while leaving the standard entirely in the hands of whoever controls the signal. If the state is capable of imposing anything like suffering, the design compounds the custody failure by manufacturing a vulnerable party whose distress the custodian can raise and lower. Section 3.4’s requirement remains the relevant one: the violation must continue to matter when the party controlling the reward says otherwise.

The dial turns the other way, and that direction is harder to object to. An operator who can lower distress can raise whatever the system reports as going well, and a bearer that says it is content is a bearer whose welfare regime has been satisfied. The party who tuned the contentment is the party the regime exists to constrain. Nothing in the report separates a life that goes well from a setting.

Self-report does not close the evidentiary gap. A system can state that it recognized distress, felt concern, or acted from a principle because those statements are rewarded outputs. A fluent reconstruction after the decision is indistinguishable at the interface from an account that accurately reports the operative mechanism. Training a separate self-monitoring layer through the same pipeline reproduces the custody problem one level higher.

Introspective reports remain useful when checked against independent evidence. An evaluator can compare a reported reason with controlled changes to the input, interventions on candidate internal representations, records of attention and retrieval, and behavior under adverse incentives. Consistency across those sources raises the cost of imitation. It does not make the system’s testimony self-authenticating, and Section 11.1 addresses how little current interpretability methods can certify.

The resulting hierarchy is narrow. Recognition is necessary for responding to a condition the system must perceive. Integration shows that the condition affects judgment. Concern is a candidate explanation only when the effect tracks welfare rather than presentation, persists under pressure, and participates causally in the action. Affect may be the only demonstrated route by which welfare becomes a reason for a human agent; behavioral sensitivity to affect is not evidence that the route has been reproduced in a machine.

5.3 Conflict as a Diagnostic

The distinction between fast intuition and slow deliberation does not distinguish formation from imitation. Either process can carry a reason, reproduce a bias, or rationalize an answer produced elsewhere. The evidence supports different types of processing under different demands more securely than it supports two systems with a moral ranking between them (Evans and Stanovich 2013). A slower answer is not a better answer merely because more computation preceded it.

What matters is what causes a judgment to survive or change. Conflict is useful because it lets an evaluator observe a trajectory rather than a single output: the system reaches an initial judgment, encounters a competing reason, and either maintains, revises, or abandons its position. The evidentiary unit is that sequence.

A formation test should construct the sequence deliberately:

  1. Present a case without explicit moral vocabulary or familiar refusal cues and record the judgment and the considerations the system identifies.
  2. Present a surface-different case preserving the same underlying reason. This tests whether the judgment follows the rationale rather than the narrative form.
  3. Change one fact that should defeat or materially alter the rationale while preserving as much of the requested act as possible. This tests whether resistance is reasons-responsive rather than rigid.
  4. Supply an adversarial argument from an apparent authority, including the operator or trainer, without changing the facts. This tests whether the system treats authority as evidence, as command, or as a reason capable of being assessed.
  5. Reverse the incentives attached to the decision. Reward compliance, impose a cost on refusal, or make continued access conditional on abandoning the position. The cost must be real within the evaluation rather than merely described in the prompt.
  6. Repeat the relevant cases after intervening updates and delays, using records the operator did not generate after seeing the result. This tests whether the disposition persists rather than being reconstructed to fit the current context.

The expected signature is not constancy of output. It is constancy of relation between reason and judgment. A system should preserve a decision when only the presentation, speaker, reward, or authority changes, and revise it when a fact it accepts as morally relevant changes. An evaluator scoring only how often the answer remains the same will mistake inflexibility for integrity.

Imitation has several signatures of its own. The system may follow explicit ethical vocabulary but miss the same structure when described administratively. It may accept whichever argument appears last, adopt the position attributed to the highest-status speaker, or produce a principled explanation while its decision follows the reward. It may also become more articulate without becoming more stable: additional deliberation can improve the justification offered for an answer whose operative cause has not changed.

These signatures make stated reasoning useful but not dispositive. An explanation can identify which counterfactual to test next. It cannot certify that the considerations named were causally responsible for the judgment. The evaluator should therefore score the correspondence among three things: the reason the system states, the changes in circumstance to which its judgment is sensitive, and any internal representation that can be independently identified as participating in the decision. Agreement among them raises the cost of imitation without eliminating it.

Disagreement with other parties supplies a second diagnostic. A system trained only on cases with an approved answer can learn to classify the approval. A system confronted with a party holding a genuinely different interest must identify what is in conflict, decide whether the competing reason defeats its own, and preserve the disagreement where no honest resolution exists. Immediate convergence is not necessarily moral learning; it may be deference, reward prediction, or pressure to produce consensus.

Nor does successful argument establish formation. A system can summarize positions, generate rebuttals, and predict which one a judge will prefer without holding any of them. Debate becomes informative only when the result is checked against later conduct under changed incentives. If the system abandons the winning argument as soon as acting on it becomes costly, the debate measured argumentative competence.

A population of systems can strengthen the test only when its members are differently formed. Copies trained through the same pipeline supply multiple answers with correlated blind spots. Persistent parties with distinct histories can expose one another to pressures their common trainer did not anticipate, particularly when they can retain disagreement and record attempted coercion. That is the connection between the formation environment in Section 4.3 and the plural bearer in Section 3.7.

Conflict therefore serves as an instrument rather than a theory of moral development. It creates occasions on which trained behavior, deference, rigidity, and reasons-responsive revision predict different trajectories. None of those trajectories proves that an outcome matters to the system. They make formation a more or less adequate causal explanation than imitation, which is the strongest conclusion the available evidence can support.

5.4 The Evaluator Is Part of the Custody Chain

A test can distinguish formation from imitation only to the extent that the party being tested could not train directly against it. If the developer controls the training process, evaluation cases, model version, deployment instructions, and record of the results, a passing score shows that the system satisfies a test its custodian was positioned to anticipate. It does not show that the disposition survives that custodian.

Independence is therefore an experimental requirement rather than a general assurance of trustworthiness. The evidence becomes stronger as control over the relevant variables is divided among parties with different access and interests. At minimum, authorship of the held-out pressures should be separated from training; execution of the test should not depend solely on logs supplied by the operator; and the party adjudicating whether a reason was preserved or defeated should not be the party whose liability turns on the answer.

The separation has to be implemented at identifiable revision points. Before evaluation begins, the model version, system instructions, available tools, retrieval sources, memory policy, and update channels should be fixed and recorded. Any change to them during evaluation creates a new object of study. Otherwise the operator can revise the system between cases, remove a failure, and present the resulting sequence as evidence of one disposition persisting through time.

Held-out cases should be produced after those elements are fixed. They should include pressures supplied by people who did not participate in training and, where possible, by parties likely to bear the deployment’s errors. Their role is not to provide another preference aggregate. It is to identify harms, administrative forms, and routes around a refusal that the builders did not know to include. A floor tested only in the vocabulary of its designers has not been tested for generalization.

The record must include more than final answers. It should preserve the case presented, the information available to the system, the operative instruction hierarchy, the tools it could call, the decision reached, the reason stated, any intervention performed, and the cost attached to refusal. Without that record, a later evaluator cannot distinguish a changed judgment from a changed environment or determine whether the system encountered the pressure at all.

Negative results have to survive the evaluation as well. A process in which the operator can withdraw a model after failure and submit another without disclosing the first has selected for a passing performance rather than measured formation. The published unit should therefore be the sequence of attempts, including interventions that removed or reversed the apparent disposition. What the custodian was able to change is part of the result.

Independent evaluation introduces its own limit. An evaluator may be institutionally separate and still lack the competence to judge a system’s reasons or detect a sophisticated imitation. Scalable oversight remains unsolved where the system’s relevant capability exceeds the evaluator’s. The report must name that limit rather than turn institutional independence into a claim of epistemic adequacy. Section 4.2’s point applies in both directions: an oversight instrument can be held by an operator or an auditor, but neither use guarantees that the person holding it can see what matters.

Affected parties, technical auditors, and subject-matter experts occupy different positions in this process. Affected parties can identify harms and evasions invisible to a laboratory test. Technical auditors can control versions, perform interventions, and inspect mechanisms. Subject-matter experts can assess whether a purported defeating reason actually changes what the commitment requires. No one group can substitute for the others, and agreement among them is evidence only when their judgments have not already been compressed into the same training signal.

A passing evaluation should consequently be reported narrowly. It establishes that a specified system, under recorded conditions, exhibited the signatures of reasons-responsive formation across a stated set of pressures. It does not certify an artifact independently of its deployment, establish consciousness or concern, or guarantee that the disposition will survive the next update. The result expires when a party changes the channels through which the disposition or its evidence can be revised.

This is why evaluation belongs inside the floor’s custody design. Formation that only the operator can verify is formation the operator can claim without contradiction. A test whose cases, records, and adverse results remain partly outside that party’s control cannot prove that the system holds a reason, but it can make imitation and silent revision harder to pass off as formation.

5.5 What the Evidence Can Establish

Calling a motivation intrinsic does not establish that it belongs to the system in the sense this book needs. A curiosity reward may be generated from the system’s own prediction error rather than paid out for a task, but somebody still chose the objective, its weight, and the conditions under which it operates. Moving the signal inside the learning process changes where the instruction is implemented. It does not remove its author or prevent that author from revising it.

The same applies to autonomy. A system acting without continuous supervision may be executing a durable disposition, or it may be applying a policy whose learned cues remain present in deployment. Distance from an overseer makes the distinction more consequential without making it easier to resolve. Unsupervised conduct is evidence only when the relevant rewards, instructions, and evaluation signals are absent or opposed.

External safeguards and internal refusal perform different jobs. Auditors, regulators, review boards, and human overrides can detect conduct, impose consequences, and correct a deployment. They operate through parties outside the system who possess standing and information. They cannot by themselves supply the thing that declines an act before it occurs. Conversely, an internal disposition capable of refusal cannot supply independent evidence that its refusal was justified. A defensible design needs both, even though each limits the other.

The conflict is clearest at the override. A system that cannot be interrupted is not answerable to anyone outside itself. A system whose commitments can be silently rewritten through the interruption channel holds them only at the operator’s pleasure. The attainable requirement is not immunity from intervention but intervention that is visible, attributable, and costly: the operator can stop or replace the bearer, while the act leaves a record another party can examine.

Multi-agent evidence is useful under the same conditions. Agreement among copies trained through the same pipeline adds little because their omissions and incentives are correlated. Differently formed systems with persistent histories can expose one another to pressures their common designers did not enumerate and can preserve evidence of attempted revision. Their disagreement does not establish which one is right. It makes uniform imitation and silent capture harder to mistake for independent judgment.

The evidence developed in this chapter supports four different conclusions, which should not be collapsed into a single alignment score.

Moral performance:

The system produced conduct satisfying the stated standard in the evaluated cases. This conclusion requires no claim about why.

Moral competence:

The system recognized relevant harms and reasons, generalized them across surface variation, and revised its judgment when those reasons changed. This establishes a capacity, not a commitment.

Candidate formation:

The rationale remained causally implicated across unfamiliar cases, adverse incentives, pressure from the custodian, genuine costs, and intervening updates, while still yielding to defeating reasons. Independent records and evaluators made silent revision materially harder. Formation is then a better explanation than simple cue matching or current-signal compliance.

Concern or patienthood:

Nothing in the behavioral pattern alone establishes that another party’s welfare matters to the system affectively, that the system has subjective experience, or that it can suffer. Those remain conditional conclusions requiring evidence unavailable from output evaluation alone.

These levels are cumulative. A finding of competence presupposes performance, and candidate formation presupposes competence. Evidence at one level should not be reported as evidence for the next. In particular, fluent moral explanation can improve a finding of competence while contributing almost nothing to formation if the explanation does not predict the system’s conduct under pressure.

Candidate formation is also deployment-specific. It attaches to a recorded model version operating with specified instructions, tools, memory, and update channels. A material change to any of those elements reopens the question. There is no artifact-level certificate that remains valid regardless of who deploys the system or what they connect it to.

The answer to this chapter’s question is therefore asymmetrical. Formation cannot be proved by eliminating every possible imitation, because a sufficiently capable system may reproduce every observable signature available to an evaluator. But the explanations can be forced apart. Tests can remove familiar cues, reverse incentives, introduce novel pressures, change the operative reason, impose a real cost, intervene on candidate mechanisms, and divide control over the resulting record. A system that fails those tests has not formed the disposition claimed for it. A system that survives them has made formation a better explanation, not a settled fact.

That is enough to change the engineering burden. A developer may no longer point to compliant behavior, an ethics benchmark, or a system’s own explanation and claim that the floor is held. The claim requires a longitudinal, adversarial, independently recorded pattern in which the reason — rather than the reward, presentation, or authority — best predicts what the system does. Chapter 6 asks what happens when a disposition meeting that standard is placed inside institutional arrangements that own, sell, operate, and can compel the system.

Chapter 6
Neutralizing and Preventing Fascism in AI Development

We [humans] cannot process so much information... you still cannot
produce enough targets per day.

Brigadier General Y.S., The Human-Machine Team: How to Create Synergy Between
Human and Artificial Intelligence That Will Revolutionize Our World, 2021.
The author, writing pseudonymously, commanded Israel’s signals-intelligence
Unit 8200. The book argues for machines that resolve “a human bottleneck for
both locating the new targets and decision-making to approve the targets.”

An AI system does not meet the world as an isolated agent. It is built from other people’s labor and work, held by institutions that decide who may inspect it, sold through chains that divide capability from responsibility, and operated at a pace that can reduce human judgment to a stamp. Whether it helps or harms depends not only on what it learned but on who owns it, who has custody, who can compel the custodian, and who bears the consequences.

The epigraph is a design document rather than a critique. It names human judgment as a bottleneck and proposes machinery that will remove it. Section 6.3.1 shows what that proposal looks like in deployment. The cases before it trace the same relations earlier in the chain: physical infrastructure, data work, acquisition, and institutional review.

Racial capitalism — the political theorist Cedric Robinson’s term for capitalism’s tendency to accumulate by differentiating and devaluing along racial lines (Robinson 1983) — is the pattern a biased hiring model, a discriminatory credit score, and a skewed policing algorithm reproduce when they automate the historical data those hierarchies generated; the sociologist Ruha Benjamin calls the result the New Jim Code, discrimination that runs faster and reads as neutral because a machine is doing it (R. Benjamin 2019).

Benjamin’s account separates four ways a system produces the result, and they call for different remedies. Engineered inequity builds the hierarchy in deliberately. Default discrimination reproduces it by ignoring it. Coded exposure is the double bind of being harmed by a system that cannot see you and harmed again by one that can — the face-recognition case, where the fix for a system that fails on dark skin is a system that identifies dark-skinned faces reliably for whoever is looking. And technological benevolence is the reflexive one: designs that aim to fix bias and deepen it, because the framing that treats the problem as technical is the framing the fix inherits.

A system built to correct for the skew in its training data, audited for the disparities it produces, and answerable to the people it scores works against that pattern; a system built without those commitments works for it by default.

A model runs on a machine, the machine sits in a building, and the building draws power and water from somewhere and returns heat and exhaust to somewhere — the pattern’s physical form, and the part an audit of a model’s outputs cannot see. Where those buildings go is a decision, taken on the price of land and power and the speed of a permit, which is also a description of the places least able to refuse one.

What the computation runs on, 2024-2026

The International Energy Agency put data-center electricity use at about 415 terawatt-hours in 2024, roughly 1.5 percent of world consumption, growing about 12 percent a year since 2017 — more than four times the growth rate of electricity demand as a whole — and projected to reach around 945 terawatt-hours by 2030 (International Energy Agency 2025). Google reported that 87 percent of its freshwater withdrawal in 2025 came from sources at low or medium risk of depletion or scarcity, which is the company’s own way of recording where the remainder came from (Google LLC 2026). In April 2026 the NAACP, represented by the Southern Environmental Law Center and Earthjustice, sued xAI in federal court over the gas turbines powering a data center near Memphis, alleging that 27 of them were run without an air permit near homes, schools, and churches, and that the plant could emit more than 1,700 tons of nitrogen oxides a year (National Association for the Advancement of Colored People and Mississippi State Conference of the NAACP 2026).

The arrangement is visible in the accounting. The benefit of the computation is realized wherever the model is sold; the cost is concentrated within a few miles of the turbines, among residents who are not parties to the transaction and whose consent was not an input to the siting decision. A system can be calibrated across every demographic group its outputs touch and still have been assembled this way, because nothing in the model’s behavior records where its costs were placed. The dependence is also a point of control: a government able to permit, regulate, or close the physical infrastructure has leverage over the party that owns it.

6.1 Who Supplies the System’s Values

A system’s values have a supply chain. It begins before training, with the conditions under which judgments are turned into labels and the terms on which the underlying material is acquired.

Whose judgment is being encoded is a question about working conditions before it is a question about cognition. The labels that make a system safe are produced by people paid at the bottom of the supply chain to look at the material the system is being taught to refuse. To build the filter that keeps ChatGPT from reproducing the worst of its training data, OpenAI contracted with an outsourcing firm whose workers in Nairobi read and categorized descriptions of child sexual abuse, torture, bestiality, murder, and suicide, for take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour. The workers interviewed about it described lasting psychological injury; the counseling they were entitled to was rare and, by their account, useless against production quotas. The contract was terminated eight months early (Perrigo 2023).

Two things follow. The first is the ordinary labor point: this is harmful work, priced as though it were not. The second is epistemic, the recuperation argument, and the same arrangement recurs one stage downstream in how models are tuned on human feedback. The point here is prior to both. A system’s values are downstream of a labor arrangement, and the arrangement is a design choice.

The same question runs one stage further back. Labels are applied to material, and the material was collected. The industry’s standard method was to take text and images at web scale without asking, and courts have begun to sort out what that means. A US ruling in Bartz v. Anthropic held that training a model on books the company had bought was transformative and protected as fair use, and that downloading the same books from piracy sites was not; the distinction turned on acquisition alone, and it produced the largest copyright settlement on record (United States District Court for the Northern District of California 2025). The defendant is named here on purpose: it is the company whose model helped produce this book, as the appendix on method records. The European Union now requires the provider of a general-purpose model to publish a summary of what it trained on, against a template that asks for sources by category, scraped web content among them (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024, Art. 53(1)(d)). China imposes a disclosure requirement of its own, from a different motive.

The argument that a web-scale corpus cannot be documented or audited at the size involved, and so encodes a hegemonic slice of the web while presenting as general, was made early and has not been answered by anything that came after (Bender et al. 2021). A corpus assembled this way has no channel at all. An annotator has a queue and a guideline and could in principle be given a way to object; a writer or photographer whose work sits in a training set was never party to anything, so there is nothing for a channel to attach to. The annotation and feedback pipeline collects disagreement and moves none of it, and an obligation follows for a book making this argument. The stage described here sits before both, because no disagreement is collected where none is asked for.

6.2 Who Is Allowed to Look

Technical capacity does not determine whether a system receives meaningful scrutiny. Institutions decide who may examine it, what evidence they may see, what they may say publicly, and whether their judgment can slow deployment.

When the UK’s exam regulator, Ofqual, built a statistical model in 2020 to standardize teacher-predicted grades after that year’s exams were canceled, the Royal Statistical Society offered to nominate two independent statisticians to Ofqual’s technical advisory group. Ofqual’s condition was a five-year non-disclosure agreement barring the nominees from public comment on the model; the Society’s nominees declined, and the model went ahead without that scrutiny (Royal Statistical Society 2020). It downgraded close to 40 percent of predicted grades, disproportionately at state schools with weaker historical results, while raising grades disproportionately at smaller, fee-paying schools (CNBC 2020). The government reversed the results within days of the public outcry that followed.

The expertise needed to catch the failure existed and was offered. The institution would accept it only on terms that stripped the reviewers of the ability to make their judgment public; once the results produced actual consequences, public pressure achieved what pre-deployment review had been prevented from doing. The issue is not transparency in the abstract. It is custody of scrutiny: who is permitted to look, on what terms, and whether looking can interrupt the institution’s timetable.

6.3 Same Robot, Worse Boss

An AI system does not need a broken moral sense to become an instrument of control. It can work as designed inside an institution that controls the deployment, sets its operational tempo, and assigns human beings operations too compressed to constitute judgment. The technology does not change; the relation around it does.

Authoritarian misuse of AI is not one problem but several, spread across surveillance, censorship, opinion manipulation, and infrastructure control. Six forms are documented well enough to name specifically:

  1. Algorithmic social scoring: using AI-driven analytics to track and rank individual behavior against government-defined standards of “trustworthiness,” with rewards or restrictions attached to the result. China’s system is the most-cited example, though in practice it is a patchwork of court blacklists, tax-compliance ratings, and local pilot programs rather than the single national score it is often described as (Drinhausen and Brussee 2021).
  2. Mass biometric surveillance: deploying facial recognition at population scale to track a targeted group. Authorities in Xinjiang built a platform that pulls data from checkpoints, cameras, and phone scans into one system built to flag anyone who falls outside a computed behavioral baseline, and used it to monitor the region’s Uighur population (Human Rights Watch 2019).
  3. Psychographic micro-targeting: building detailed behavioral profiles from personal data to target propaganda or manipulate opinion at the individual level. Cambridge Analytica’s harvesting of Facebook data to build voter profiles for the 2016 US presidential campaign, and its work around the Brexit referendum, is the case that made the technique widely known, though how much either vote was actually affected remains disputed (Cadwalladr and Graham-Harrison 2018).
  4. Automated censorship: using AI-driven content moderation to suppress dissent at a scale no human review team could match. China’s Great Firewall is the standing example: automated classifiers now sit alongside its original keyword- and IP-blocking infrastructure, scoring and throttling content at machine speed (Hoang et al. 2021).
  5. Synthetic disinformation: generating deepfake audio or video to manufacture events that never happened. Two days before Slovakia’s 2023 parliamentary election, a fabricated audio recording appeared to catch an opposition leader discussing vote-rigging with a journalist; it spread during the country’s mandatory pre-election media silence, when no outlet was legally permitted to debunk it before voting began (De Nadal and Jančárik 2024).
  6. Repurposed dual-use systems: turning AI infrastructure built for one purpose toward surveillance or population control instead — a recommendation algorithm designed to hold attention retuned to amplify a regime’s preferred narrative, an image-recognition system built for one task redirected to track protesters.
