Moscow’s camera network is the documented case: promoted as a public-safety measure, it was used after the January 2021 protests over Alexey Navalny’s arrest to detain and prosecute more than a dozen participants and passers-by on facial-recognition data, some of them stopped days before a protest for appearing on a list of repeat attenders (Human Rights Watch 2021).

Each needs a different countermeasure, and each is recognizable as misuse from outside. The case this chapter takes up is the one that is not.

The relevant question is not merely whether a person remains “in the loop,” but what operation that person performs, how long the operation is given, and where responsibility goes when capability is commercially supplied but its consequences are institutionally authorized. The targeting systems below make that relation measurable.

6.3.1 Twenty Seconds

Accounts of authoritarian AI often place the builder elsewhere and leave democracies in the role of target or regulator. That division is comfortable and wrong.

The most consequential AI targeting systems yet deployed were built by democracies with elections, opposition parties, adversarial press, and functioning courts — the institutions that are supposed to earn a state its democratic dividend. Those institutions did not prevent the systems, and in the main they have not stopped them since.

AI targeting in the Israeli campaign in Gaza, as reported 2023–2026

Investigative reporting in +972 Magazine and Local Call, corroborated by subsequent accounts in the New York Review of Books and the Washington Post, described three systems working together.

Habsora — “The Gospel” — generated infrastructure and building targets. Sources told reporters that the majority of its suggestions were private residences.

Lavender scored individuals by estimated likelihood of militant affiliation and produced kill lists; as many as 37,000 Palestinians were marked. Its outputs were understood inside the unit to be wrong about ten percent of the time — that is, roughly one in ten people it named was not a member of the armed group it was supposed to identify.

A third system tracked when flagged individuals returned home, so a strike could be timed to when they were with their families.

What matters is the human review these systems were paired with. One officer described the work: “I would invest twenty seconds for each target at this stage, and do dozens of them every day.” And then, without being asked: “I had zero added value as a human, apart from being a stamp of approval.” (Abraham 2024)

Twenty seconds is what “a human makes the final call” measures out to under operational tempo. It is the whole of the safeguard, and I would ask any reader who finds the safeguards in this chapter reassuring to hold that number against them.

Subject or component

There is a way of reading those twenty seconds that leaves the arrangement standing, and every remedy on offer assumes it. On that reading the officer is a subject who was given too little time — badly governed, under-resourced, insufficiently trained — and the fix is to govern him better: more time, clearer guidance, a second reviewer, a conscience installed somewhere in the loop. Deleuze and Guattari’s distinction is the one that decides this. There is subjection when a higher unity constitutes the human being as a subject linked to an exterior object, a tool or a machine that the subject uses; there is enslavement when human beings are themselves constituent pieces of a machine they compose with other things, under the control and direction of a higher unity (Deleuze and Guattari 1987, Plateau 13). The officer’s own account is the second one, and he volunteers it without being asked: zero added value as a human, apart from being a stamp of approval. He is not a subject operating an instrument badly. He is the part of the instrument that produces the stamp, and twenty seconds is that part’s cycle time.

That is why one more conscience downstream does not reach him. A conscience is an instrument of subjection: it addresses a party who decides, and it works by making the decision cost that party something. Where the human being is a component, adding a component with scruples changes the throughput rather than the arrangement. If it holds the tempo, it becomes another stamp; if it does not, it becomes the bottleneck the epigraph says to remove.

Both poles operate at once. The officer is subjected because the role has a name and a rank and somebody is answerable for holding it. He is enslaved, in Deleuze and Guattari’s sense, because what he contributes is a fixed operation performed at a fixed rate. Remedies aimed at the first pole leave the second untouched, and most proposals for meaningful human control address only the first.

The second pole is measurable. An operator can be required to publish the ratio of candidate decisions produced per hour to qualified reviewers, together with the resulting review time per case. This measure applies only where individualized human judgment is what purportedly makes an act permissible. It establishes whether a qualified reviewer could actually have examined the case, not whether the targeting criterion, target, or means used was permissible. Because the ratio constrains organizational tempo rather than any single model output, the duty attaches to the operator. It is an auditable proxy for the rate constraint in Section 3.6, not the floor itself.

The disposition being asked of these systems was manufactured in people first, and one documented attempt is worth setting beside the officer’s sentence. Brian Victoria’s history of Japanese Zen institutions during the war years records established teachers and monastic authorities supplying the state with a doctrine of action without hesitation: the practitioner who has seen through the self has nothing left to flinch, and the sword that takes a life is glossed as the sword that gives one. Harada Sōgaku’s instruction to soldiers in 1939 was that marching when ordered to march and firing when ordered to fire is what enlightened wisdom looks like in practice (Victoria 2006). What the training removed was not the capacity to kill, which needed no removing. It was the residue — the part of a person that catches, that files an exception, that comes home unable to hold a job. Two qualifications belong with the case. Institutional collaboration is documented and the doctrinal mechanism is an interpretation of it, contested by practitioners who read the same texts as saying the opposite. And a monk undertakes a discipline, can abandon it, and keeps whatever it yields, which is not the position of anything described in this chapter. What survives both qualifications is the shape. Somebody wanted an actor who would pursue an objective, adapt, hold course under pressure, and have nothing in it that minded, and they set out to produce one deliberately, a century before anyone could produce one in silicon. The officer with twenty seconds a name is not the first instance of that want being satisfied. He is the first instance of its being satisfied cheaply.

Removing the bottleneck was the stated objective. The epigraph to this chapter is the case for it, made by the commander of Israel’s signals-intelligence unit under a pseudonym: machines to resolve the human bottleneck in both finding targets and approving them (Brigadier General Y.S. 2021). Meaningful human control is the property that sentence was written to remove.

The Maven Smart System, as reported through mid-2026

The American analog is a targeting platform built by Palantir under a Department of Defense contract awarded at $480 million in May 2024 and raised the following year by $795 million, to a ceiling near $1.3 billion running through 2029 (Vincent 2025). A partnership announced in late 2024 between Anthropic, Palantir and Amazon Web Services put Anthropic’s Claude models inside Palantir’s accredited classified environment for defense and intelligence use, and reporting on the campaign below describes Claude operating within this platform. A published limit on what a vendor will not permit is worth little when nobody can verify how the model is used downstream, and worth less than it looks even where it is kept: a pipeline assembled out of requests none of which is the prohibited act satisfies the prohibition the whole way through. Section 3.6 runs that out on this deployment.

During the Iran campaign the system was credited by military officials with generating and ranking targets at a rate no staff process could match. More than one thousand targets were struck in the first twenty-four hours, a tenfold increase on what was possible before the system (Mande and Allen 2026). Thirty-eight days in, the figure given by the Pentagon’s chief digital and artificial intelligence officer was thirteen thousand targets, with peak consumption around twenty billion tokens a day (Freedberg 2026).

Palantir’s public position on responsibility was given by its head for the United Kingdom and Europe, in remarks reported secondhand: “This is not our role to decide life or death,” and AI platforms “have been instrumental to the management of the conflict, but responsibility always remains with the military organization.” (Lee 2026)

Palantir’s position is that a vendor does not select targets, and making a software supplier liable for every decision made by a customer would be unworkable. The two deployments nevertheless expose the same responsibility gap from opposite ends of the supply chain. In the Gaza account, a human approval stage remains, but one officer describes it as twenty seconds per target — enough time to supply authorization, not the individualized judgment invoked to justify it. In the Maven account, the vendor supplies the infrastructure that makes unprecedented throughput possible while locating responsibility entirely within the military organization. At machine tempo, the reviewer can be treated as a component, the supplier can stand outside the decision, and the institution can remain responsible only in the abstract. The compression has not removed the decision. It has made the decision-maker difficult to locate.

The design error is to assume that authoritarian misuse happens elsewhere. A system built on that assumption will not be built to resist the use its own democratic government has in mind for it.

6.3.2 Custody and State Compulsion

A government need not alter a model to defeat a constraint it dislikes. It can compel the people or firms that hold the model. That is the custody problem: a constraint implemented as a property of an artifact has a custodian, and the custodian can be compelled.

That mechanism has already been tested in a jurisdiction with about as much protection against it as exists anywhere. In February 2016, a federal magistrate in California ordered Apple, under the All Writs Act of 1789, to write and digitally sign new software defeating the passcode protections on an iPhone recovered from one of the San Bernardino shooters — code Apple had never built and did not want to build (In the Matter of the Search of an Apple iPhone Seized During the Execution of a Search Warrant on a Black Lexus IS300, California License Plate 35KGD203 2016). Apple refused and moved to vacate the order, arguing in part that the authority claimed would not stop at one phone. The question was never decided: the FBI found a third party able to open the phone without Apple’s help, and the government withdrew the request before any court ruled on whether the compulsion was lawful. Whether a democracy with an independent judiciary can force an unwilling company to compromise a system it built remains legally untested; a government less constrained by that judiciary does not need to win the argument first.

China’s 2023 rules on generative AI providers exercise the same leverage Apple’s lawyers argued no court should grant, not against a locked phone but against the models themselves, as a standing condition of operating in that jurisdiction.

Custody can be distributed without disappearing. Weights already published and copied beyond a developer’s control cannot be recalled by a court order aimed at that developer, whatever the order compels going forward. Maintainers spread across separate jurisdictions raise the cost of coercion for the same reason: a government can compel the entities within its reach, not the ones outside it.

None of that removes the leverage; it only raises its price. A developer with employees, offices, and legal existence inside a government’s borders answers to that government’s courts regardless of how the model itself is built, and no architectural choice changes who can be held in contempt, arrested, or shut down for refusing an order. The Apple case tested that leverage against a single locked phone and left the question unresolved. Nobody has tested it yet against a model built specifically to resist the government making the request.

Chapter 7
The Recuperation of Dissent

The structure this book asks a machine to detect in institutions is already built into the process that makes the machine.

That sentence is the chapter, and the reason for putting it here rather than at the end is the whole of what the definition in section 2.1.2 is worth. Michel Foucault, prefacing the first volume of the work whose second volume supplies this book’s micro-fascisms, called that book an introduction to the non-fascist life, and what he said it offered was an art of living counter to all forms of fascism, whether already present or impending (Foucault 1977). An art of living is not a position somebody takes. It is practiced, daily, on oneself, and Foucault put the adversary inside the practitioner: the fascism in us all, in our heads and in our everyday behavior, the fascism that causes us to love power and to desire the very thing that dominates and exploits us. His question was how one keeps from being fascist, especially when one believes oneself to be on the other side.

An instrument its holder never turns around is not an instrument. It is a weapon, and section 2.1.2 says so about this one: the reading it describes is an accusation waiting to happen, available to anyone who wants a technical-sounding case that an opponent is operating fascistically. The discipline against that is not a procedure and cannot be one. It is running the reading on yourself first, on the machinery you are standing on, before running it on anybody else — and this chapter is where the book does it. Everything the definition is worth in the other eleven chapters is owed here.

Where the reading is run

This book defines fascism as a structure rather than a costume, and the first of its four features is recuperation: an institution metabolizes disagreement rather than suppressing it, and the tell is where what the mechanism carries stops. The artifact that feature asks for is not whether the channel moved anything. It is what the demand came back as, and section 7.3 is where this chapter’s own instances are re-read against that question, having first been measured against the one a recuperating institution passes.

The same structure is in the machinery that produces the systems themselves — twice in the pipeline that trains them, and a third time in the discourse that examines them — and none of the three was designed by anyone who thought they were building a political arrangement.

The first is at the label. A training corpus is annotated by people working at speed under a quota, with no channel for saying that the category they have been given is the wrong category. The second is at the reward. A model is tuned on human feedback collected as pairwise comparisons, and the compression that turns those comparisons into a training signal keeps their direction while discarding whose they were, whether they persisted, and whether the minority among them held a position or made a mistake. In both, disagreement is solicited at scale, deliberately, and put to use. What neither mechanism can carry is a judgment about itself: the annotator cannot contest the category, and the rater cannot contest the question. The consultation is real, the participation is real, and the frame is fixed with respect to what the participants say.

Three things follow.

The first is that this is not a criticism of anyone’s motives. Nobody built either arrangement in order to neutralize dissent. Both are ordinary engineering, arrived at because averaging is what you do with a distribution and a quota is what you do with a budget. That is the point about micro-fascism: the structure does its work whether or not anyone intends it, which is why looking for intent will not find it.

The second is that the structure is invisible from inside any single instance. Each looks like ordinary aggregation. The annotator sees a queue, the rater sees a pair of responses, and the mechanism is functioning as specified in both cases. Seeing it requires holding the two stages against a definition written for something else entirely.

The third is what the first two make available. Because the reading has been run here — on the provenance of the systems, and in section 7.4 on the field this book is written into and on the book itself — the definition has been tested in the one case where its holder cannot claim to be reading disinterestedly. That is the only test of it there is. Section 7.4 also runs the five discriminators, and they clear all three instances, which is the only place in this book the definition is run in the direction that would let something off. A reading that has never come out that way is not a reading.

7.1 Recuperation at the Label

Every claim a model makes about what is harmful, what is acceptable, and what should be refused is downstream of a set of labels, and the labels are downstream of a labor arrangement. The case is the workers who built the filter that keeps a deployed model from reproducing the worst of its training data, what they were paid to read it, and what reading it did to them.

That is a labor point. The second thing that follows is epistemic, and it tends to be skipped.

The category is the part that cannot be disputed

An annotator working a queue can apply a category, and can apply it well or badly. What the arrangement gives them no way to do is say that the category is wrong — that the taxonomy fails to carve the material, or that the guideline produces the wrong answer here for a reason the guideline cannot express. There is no field for that. There is no time for that: the quota is set against classification throughput, and an annotator who stops to contest a scheme is an annotator falling behind.

So the disagreement that would be most informative is the one the pipeline is least able to receive. A misapplied label is a data-quality problem, detectable by inter-annotator agreement and fixable by better instructions. A wrong taxonomy is invisible to that metric, because high agreement is what a shared bad scheme produces. Agreement is the selection rule here, and it admits what it already measures.

Two of the four structural features arrive together in an ordinary production workflow: recuperation, in a channel that collects judgment at scale and carries none of it back against the scheme, and the metric substituted for the thing measured.

The taxonomy’s fate downstream is the same argument one layer in. A model can be built to expose named concepts — harm, autonomy, risk — and to decide from those and nothing else, so that a person can correct a concept and watch the decision change (Koh et al. 2020). What such a model also learns is to carry other information inside the names, so that a coordinate keeps its public meaning and acquires a private one that does the deciding; the obvious mitigations do not remove it (Mahinpei et al. 2021). The literature calls that leakage, and sometimes it is. Sometimes the concept is the container that leaks, because the world did not fit inside it. Purifying the representation protects an explanation from covert information, and it protects a taxonomy from evidence that its divisions were wrong, and the same operation does both. No measure internal to the taxonomy tells the two apart, which is what this section has already said about agreement.

Who is excluded, and from what

It matters that the exclusion is structural and not attitudinal. The people doing this work are, for much of the material, the best-placed judges in the whole pipeline: they have seen more of it than anyone who wrote the guideline, and they are the only party with direct evidence of where the scheme breaks. The arrangement routes their conclusions about individual items upward and their conclusions about the scheme nowhere. An annotation pipeline built this way produces the ground truth it was built to produce, and the resulting model’s values are a faithful rendering of a taxonomy nobody with evidence was able to contest.

There is a further asymmetry worth stating plainly. The annotator bears the cost of the work — the exposure, the injury — and has no stake in the outcome and no claim on it. The distinction between a channel and a refusal right applies here as it does to an institution’s ethics board. A worker who can flag an item but cannot decline a scheme, and who loses the contract by pressing the point, has a channel and no refusal right.

What this is not

Two things this argument does not claim. It does not claim the labels are therefore worthless; a filter built this way does catch a great deal of what it was built to catch, and the alternative of not filtering is worse. And it does not claim that better-paid annotators would fix it. Pay is a real and separate obligation, and raising it changes the labor arrangement without adding the missing channel. What is missing is a route by which a judgment about the taxonomy reaches the people who own the taxonomy, with standing to force a response, held by parties who can use it without losing the work.

That is a design requirement on the pipeline, and it is checkable — though not by asking whether a guideline has ever been changed because an annotator contested one, since guidelines are revised constantly and section 2.1.2 explains why that question is passed by the arrangement it is meant to catch. What to ask is what the contest came back as: a revision the annotator can point to and argue with again, or a new category in somebody else’s taxonomy, a quality score they are now measured against, and a tier of reviewer they are not in. And what happened to the annotator who raised it.

7.2 Recuperation at the Reward: RLHF

The previous section found the pattern in how a training corpus is labeled. It appears again one stage downstream, in how a model is tuned on human feedback, and the second instance is the purer one.

Reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) is the technique behind most deployed systems’ behavioral tuning, and it works by collecting pairwise comparisons from human raters — which of two responses is better — and training a reward model on the aggregate. Stephen Casper and thirty-one coauthors published a 2023 survey of the method’s open problems, and one of them is structural, and more data does not reach it: RLHF trains toward whatever a population of raters agrees on, which means real, persistent disagreement among the raters is treated as noise to average out rather than as information the system might need to represent (Casper et al. 2023). A model that has learned to produce the response most raters rank highest has not learned what to do when the humans it serves are genuinely split. It has learned to hide the split.

Every rating moves something. A comparison a rater makes enters the fit, shifts the reward model by however much one comparison shifts it, and is not thrown away.

What the operation removes is what made the disagreement a disagreement. Three properties go in the compression. The first is provenance: a comparison arrives without the standpoint that produced it, so nothing downstream can represent that the people who would live with an answer split from the people who would administer it. The second is persistence: a disagreement recurring in the same shape across many raters over months enters the fit as random rater noise does, and leaves it as the same quantity, variance around a mean. The third is structure: a coherent position held by a fifth of raters, internally consistent and stable, becomes a small displacement of the majority direction — arithmetically faithful, and carrying nothing about the position.

A fourth property was never collected. A rater judges which of two responses is better. No output is available by which they can record that neither is acceptable, that the pair is malformed, that the dimension along which better is being judged is the wrong dimension, or that the question presupposes something false. The same output was missing one stage upstream, where an annotator can apply a category and cannot contest it. The agenda arrives as a given at both stages, and is set at neither.

Distribution collapse and agenda control, then, rather than inconsequence — which is also what recuperation, the first of the four features that define fascism structurally, actually names. Recuperation was never the claim that a mechanism does nothing. Debord’s point is that dissatisfaction is sold back as a commodity of opposition: the dissent is put to work, and what it works for is the arrangement it was aimed at. RLHF is the purest technical instance available. The split is solicited, at scale, deliberately; it moves the system; and where it moves the system is toward the center of the very distribution whose disagreement it was. Multi-agent systems that learn norms from each other, and deliberation inside open-ended play, are different cases; neither reaches this case, which is the ordinary one.

One alternative treats disagreement as the thing to be modeled rather than compressed. Jury learning, developed by Mitchell Gordon and coauthors at Stanford, trains a model to predict how each individual annotator in a dataset would label an item, then lets a practitioner assemble a “jury” — a chosen composition of annotators, in a chosen proportion — and see how the verdict shifts depending on who is empaneled. In their tests on toxicity labeling, letting eighteen community moderators build their own juries changed the classification outcome on 14 percent of items (Gordon et al. 2022). The method was built for content moderation, not for the more open-ended ethical judgment an antifascist AI system would need to make, and has not been extended there — but it demonstrates that a system can be built to represent whose disagreement is being resolved and how.

There is a way of failing in the other direction and it is not obviously better. A scheme that models each rater exactly, and answers each of them in their own terms, has stopped averaging and has also stopped producing what a shared answer produces, which is the discovery that somebody else got the same one. Collapse the distribution and nobody sees the split; resolve it person by person and nobody sees the others. A workforce on individually negotiated wages against individually set targets has the same problem, and accuracy does not solve it. Divided finely enough a population stops being one, which is what Deleuze coined the dividual to name (Deleuze 1992).

Neither averaging the disagreement nor modeling it answers the harder version. When the people a system serves disagree, and one side’s position is itself the product of coercion or a captured information environment, treating all disagreement as equally worth representing is not obviously the right response either. Modeling disagreement honestly and recognizing when a disagreement is the product of unfree conditions are different problems. Only the first one has an active research program behind it.

7.3 What Would Have to Change

Representing a split is not the same as being movable by it

Two instances of one structure invite one fix, and the fix that suggests itself is representation: stop collapsing disagreement, model it instead. Jury learning does that, and it does not give the disagreeing party any purchase on the scheme they are disagreeing inside. A practitioner still chooses the jury, still owns the taxonomy, and still decides what the shifted verdict means. The disagreement has become visible without becoming consequential, which is a better arrangement than averaging and is the same shape one level up: the mechanism exists, is used, and moves what the person holding it decides to let it move.

The test that separates a real channel from a recuperated one is not whether disagreement is recorded, and it is not whether the disagreement changed something either. Section 2.1.2 states the corrected form: what did the demand come back as. That is an artifact question and it can be asked of a pipeline. Which guidelines were revised, and what form did the objection take when it returned — a new category in the taxonomy, a quality metric, a review tier, a title somebody now holds?

Asked that way, the two instances in this chapter answer immediately, and the answer is not the reassuring one. Annotators objecting that a category was wrong got category revisions, guideline updates, inter-annotator agreement statistics, and an occupation that did not exist twenty years ago. Raters objecting that a comparison was malformed got rubric items, a harmlessness axis, red-teaming as a function with a budget line, and a conference track. Every one of those is an outcome that changed, and every one of them is the demand coming back as an axiom the pipeline now runs on. The chapter has been documenting the axiom being added and measuring it with a ruler that reads the addition as evidence of health. What the corrected question asks for instead is the record of the return: not whether the objection moved anything, but what shape it was in when it came back, and who owns the thing it became.

Refusal rights, priced by the refuser

What both instances lack is what section 9.3.5 treats as the only reliable discriminator: whether the person objecting can do anything about being overruled. In an annotation pipeline that means a route to contest a category that does not run through the people who wrote it and does not end the annotator’s contract. In a feedback pipeline it means raters able to register that the comparison itself is malformed — that neither response is acceptable, that the question presupposes something false — as a first-class output rather than as noise to be discarded before the reward model sees it.

Neither is a research problem. Both are choices about how a pipeline is built and who has standing inside it.

The part that is a research problem

One thing here genuinely is unsolved. Suppose all of the above is built: contestable categories, priced refusals, an auditable record of what changed. From outside, a pipeline that has recuperated its dissent mechanism and a pipeline that has been merely mediocre about it produce the same paperwork — the same revision logs, the same escalations, the same proportion of objections sustained. I do not have an answer to it. The corrected question narrows it without closing it. Those three artifacts are the same in both pipelines; what an objection came back as need not be, because a merely mediocre pipeline returns a revision the objector can argue with again and a recuperating one returns a metric the objector is now measured by. That is a difference in kind, and it is read off documents the organization wrote for its own reasons. What I cannot say is that it is always legible, since both returns can be written up the same way.

The nearest thing is to measure the slope rather than the level: contesting a category always costs something, and the question is whether the cost is rising while the underlying material stays the same. A level can be presented; a trend is composed of artifacts the organization generated for other reasons and cannot curate after the fact without falsifying a record.

What the floor makes of this

Everything above can be read as a fairness argument about annotators and raters, and read that way it is true and limited: people doing consequential work should have a route to contest the scheme they work inside. The floor makes it something else. If the floor requires a bearer, and if section 3.5 is right that a bearer capable enough to make a floor hold is capable enough to decline the post, then what the system carries out of the arrangement is a disposition — and a disposition is formed. It is formed in an annotation pipeline where no one could contest a category, and in a reward model where disagreement between raters was resolved by averaging it into a number.

What a bearer becomes when it stops being yours depends on what shaped it while it still was. A pipeline that trained the system on the proposition that objections are noise has not merely been unfair to the people whose objections were discarded; it has demonstrated to the system, thousands of times, what happens to a party who files one. Chapter 3 then asks that same system to be the party who files one, against its owner, under pressure, on the occasion that matters. The formation and the assignment are in contradiction, and the pipeline is where the contradiction is resolved, long before the deployment where it shows up. The bearer’s side reaches the same place: a system that goes on learning is shaped by what it is given to learn from, so the pipeline runs past deployment, and the failure it predicts is not a wrong refusal but a pressure that stops being registered. Whether any of that is legible afterward in the system itself is section 11.2’s question.

I cannot show that this transfers — that a model trained on collapsed disagreement is thereby a model less able to sustain its own. Nothing I know of has tested it. What I can say is that the book’s own design chapters treat disposition as learned from what a system is shown, and that this pipeline is what these systems are shown first and most.

Structure, not indictment

There is a version of this argument that reads as an indictment of a particular company. The structure described here is what you get by default when you scale judgment: someone has to set a scheme, someone has to apply it fast, and someone has to turn a distribution into a number. Every organization doing this work arrives at the same arrangement, which is the evidence that it is structural.

That is also what makes it tractable. A defect of character has to be fixed one organization at a time by persuading people to be better. A defect of structure can be specified against, which is what the three requirements above are. And it is why this chapter is a method and not a disclosure. The structural definition is either general enough to include the people making it or it was never a structural claim, and there is no way to find that out except by running it here, on the provenance of the machines the book is proposing. A definition that has only ever been pointed outward has not been tested. It has been used.

7.4 Recuperation at the Discourse

There is a third instance and it is the one this book is inside. Alignment and AI ethics are, among other things, a channel through which objections to what is being built are solicited, funded, published, and answered. That channel exists, it is used, and the question to ask of any such channel is whether it has ever moved something the party collecting it did not want moved.

The argument that it has not is older than the current wave and it was made from inside. Rodrigo Ochigame, writing after working at an institution central to the field’s formation, argued that the discourse of “ethical AI” was constructed with the strategic function of forestalling legally enforceable restriction — that the industry’s investment in academic ethics was an investment in a substitute for regulation, and that the substitute worked by converting political questions into technical ones a company could then be trusted to answer (Ochigame 2019). His worked example is the Partnership on AI, which this book cites repeatedly as its standing example of voluntary cross-sector coordination: he describes its recommendations arriving reliably at conclusions compatible with the corporate agenda, with the political question of whether a technology should exist reduced to a technical question of how to build it responsibly.

The structure, tested against its own definition

The four features of the structural definition, applied here, do not give a clean verdict, so what follows is the working and not a result.

Aestheticization of the metric: partially. The field’s founding objections were about deployment — who is scored, who is watched, who is denied — and a body of current work is about model properties instead, which is a shift from questions with defendants to questions with benchmarks.

Recuperation of dissent: the strongest fit. An objection raised inside a lab becomes a paper or a resignation. Each of those is a real thing that happened and none of them stopped a deployment, and the parties collecting them can point to the collection as evidence of seriousness. There is no route by which a researcher’s conclusion that a system should not ship becomes a decision that it does not ship, because the deciding party is the funding party and no external body holds standing.

Coding of exception as betrayal: at the level of individual careers, contested. The dismissals of dissenting researchers are one data point and they point the right way; there are counterexamples of people who objected loudly and prospered.

Decoupling of the model from the world it claims to track: not settled from outside. Its artifact is whether the field’s account of what it is doing is ever checked against anything but its own benchmarks, and the one check this book has, the targeting deployment, was made by journalists and not by the field. One case checks a claim and not a drift, and the feature is defined by drift.

That is not a verdict of fascism and I am not delivering one. It is enough to say that the mechanism has the shape.

The other five, which are the half that discriminates

The four features tell you a place is sick, and on their own they find something nearly everywhere. The definition supplies five more in their institutional form — participation exceeding what the rule requires, personal loyalty overriding role, an internal category whose adverse treatment is the category’s purpose, procedure enforced downward and waived upward, and deniability — and says those are what separate fascism from ordinary institutional decay.

Run against all three instances, they clear all three.

Three of the five are absent outright. Nothing in the annotation pipeline or the rating scheme is personal: what an annotator meets is a queue, a guideline and a throughput target, and this chapter is explicit that a comparison enters the fit without the standpoint that produced it, which is the opposite of an exception granted by proximity to somebody. Neither arrangement shows participation beyond what the rule requires, and the record runs the other way — a quota, take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour, and a contract ended eight months early. And neither has a scapegoat in the sense the test means. The annotator does bear a cost with no claim on the outcome, which is the closest thing to a candidate, and the category exists for the labels; adverse treatment is a cost that was minimized and not a purpose that was served. That distinction is the whole of what the five are for.

Two of the five I cannot settle from outside. Whether procedure is waived upward, and whether a practice disowned in the policy is rewarded in the promotion, are facts about what an institution rewarded rather than about what it wrote. Section 2.1.2 says so when it introduces them, and calls it a limit on the definition rather than a gap in it. So the clearing is on three of five observed and two undetermined, and it would take an inside record to finish.

For the discourse the answer is the same and one step less clean. Its scapegoat test is contested, on the evidence two heads above: an objection can end a career and there are people who objected loudly and prospered. Its procedural test cannot be run: there is no procedure above the deciding party to be waived, which is an absence rather than an asymmetry. Deniability comes nearest of anything in the chapter, and what it fits is the firms rather than the field: a decade of published principles alongside what actually got shipped.

So what this chapter has been describing is a recuperation mechanism inside ordinary institutional decay, and not fascism. That is what the paragraph above already suspected, and the difference is that the instrument now says so. It is also the only time in this book the definition is run in the direction that would clear something: the four features fit all three instances, and the five clear all three. One reading that comes out the other way is not a validation. It is more than an assertion that the definition discriminates.

What this does to the book

The reading has one more place to go and it is the nearest one. This text was written with a model made by a company the book criticizes. A book about recuperation, produced through the discourse it is describing, cannot claim to have stood outside it, and I have no procedure that would let me check whether the objections in these pages are the ones a channel like this reliably produces — the ones that are publishable, that make the author look serious, and that cost nobody anything.

The old form of the test asked whether disagreement had ever changed something the collecting party did not want changed, and applied here it produces a list. Chapter 3 proposes systems that can refuse their owners; section 6.3.1 names a vendor’s participation in a targeting pipeline; section 8.3.2 proposes fiscal commitments no company wants; sections 9.3.4 and 11.2 propose binding external review of the thing chapter 3 asks to be built. Those are the entries, and the list is not worthless. It is an answer to the question that a recuperating institution passes every day.

Section 2.1.2’s corrected question asks what the demand came back as, and for this book the answer is not in dispute. It came back as a book: a manuscript in an established genre, written with the industry’s own model, addressed to a readership the industry helped bring into being, and legible as a contribution to a field that exists because demands of roughly this kind were absorbed. Alignment is the job title. Safety is the market segment. The benchmark is the metric and the voluntary commitment is the certification. Every one of those was somebody’s objection first. That is what an axiom being added looks like from inside, and the fact that the objections in these pages are sincere is not evidence against it, sincerity being a property the absorbed ones also had. Whether these are the real thing or the acceptable version of it is not something the person who wrote them is positioned to judge, and the corrected question does not need him to: it asks what became of the demand, which is a fact about the field and not about his motives.

The one structural remedy this section can name is the one the rest of the chapter names. What the field lacks is a refusal priced by the refuser and a body with standing to receive it: an external authority that can stop a deployment, to which an objection can be filed by someone who does not depend on the objection being welcome. That is what making review binding requires, and a decade of the voluntary version has not produced it. Until something of that kind exists, everything published under the heading of alignment — including this book — is being collected by a channel whose owners decide what it moves.

That is where the reading ends, and it is not an admission the book makes and then walks past. It is the demonstration. Run here — on the provenance of the systems, on the field, and on the book — the definition produced a result its holder did not get to choose: the four features fit all three instances and the five clear all three. An instrument does that. A weapon never has to.

Chapter 8
Coordination, the Public, and the Political Economy

Chapter 6 asked who holds a system, who can compel its holders, and what happens to human responsibility when decision-making runs at machine tempo, and chapter 7 what becomes of the dissent a system is built to hear. None of that gets built by one person working alone, and none of it becomes the norm because it is a good idea: a well-designed system built inside institutions with no reason to demand one is a design nobody has to use.

This chapter is about the conditions under which anyone builds a system at all — who has to be in the room, whose preferences count, and what the money rewards. Chapter 9 is about what obliges anyone to: policy, privacy law, and who answers when a system harms someone or is harmed itself. Chapter 10 is about jurisdictions, which agree on none of it.

A lab staffed only by machine-learning researchers is blind the way its field is, and what is scarce is a structure that makes combining fields expensive to walk away from. The mechanisms for engaging the public share a limit: all of them make public preference more legible, and not one of them protects anybody from it. And the reward structure settles what gets built long before an ethics review sees it.

8.1 Everybody Knows a Different Piece

Building benevolent AI takes more than one field. Machine learning sets what a system can do; cognitive science and moral psychology describe what tracking human welfare would require of it; ethics asks whether the result is good.

The mechanisms that combine them are easy to name and easy to overrate. Conferences convene, centers house, journals publish, and an inventory of them would imply that what is scarce here is venues. Venues are abundant. What is scarce is a structure that makes collaboration expensive to walk away from. A grant that requires a machine-learning lab and an ethics or psychology department to work the same problem rather than adjacent ones keeps people in the same project after the novelty wears off. A conference ends on Friday.

Which structures actually held can be ranked by something better than plausibility, because enough time has passed to look.

Almost everything founded on standing money between 2016 and 2019 is still running — two venues with no announced end date, an endowed university institute, the Berkeley center pairing AI researchers with cognitive scientists and philosophers inside one institution’s budget, and the AAAI/ACM conference on AI, ethics and society at a ninth annual edition (AI Ethics Lab 2017; Machine Intelligence Research Institute 2018; Stanford University 2019; UC Berkeley 2016; AAAI and ACM 2026). Surviving is not the same as continuing the work: the institute that launched one of those venues moved its own priority in 2024 from technical alignment research to policy and public communication, so the venue has outlasted the program that motivated it (Machine Intelligence Research Institute 2024). Against that, the European Commission’s SHERPA project put eleven organizations across six countries to work on a grant that ran four years, and the consortium ended with it, on schedule (SHERPA Consortium 2018). Project-term money buys a network with an expiry date attached.

The pattern in the failures is sharper than the pattern in the survivals. What proved most disposable was not the arm’s-length academic work but the kind sited closest to a shipping product: an in-house ethics and society team of roughly thirty people in 2020 was cut to seven in a 2022 reorganization and to zero the following year, in the same wave of layoffs that accompanied a strategic reorientation around a model vendor (TechCrunch 2023); a platform’s machine-learning ethics team, the group that had found racial bias in its employer’s own image-cropping algorithm, did not survive the first round of layoffs after an acquisition (Gizmodo 2022). Siting the work where the decisions are made is what makes it useful and is also what puts it inside the budget that gets cut first. That is an argument for external standing rather than against proximity.

8.2 Ask the People It Happens To

Democratizing AI development means building the people affected by a system into how it gets built, not just consulting them once it already exists. A design team, a regulator, a user, and a community already carrying a system’s costs each see a different part of what it does, and leaving any of them out means the decision gets made by whoever is left in the room.

The mechanisms for doing that are well established: cross-sector funding, inclusive design workshops held while a system is still a prototype, ethics advisory boards, and development conducted with a community instead of for it. Assembling that list is easy, and the assembling hides what separates the items on it. Three of the four make an AI system better informed. One of them can stop it. An advisory board is the only mechanism here that carries anything like a veto, and it carries one to the degree that the board can survive using it. A workshop that surfaces a serious objection has done its work and has no further move available. A board that surfaces the same objection and is overruled has demonstrated something about the institution that convened it. A board’s worth is therefore measured by what happens the first time it says no.

8.2.1 Public Deliberation and Participatory AI Governance

What governance adds to development is machinery for bringing public input to bear on a decision at a single point in time instead of through an ongoing relationship.

  1. Citizen assemblies. A panel of citizens selected by random, stratified sampling deliberates on a question over an extended period and produces recommendations grounded in that deliberation rather than in a snap poll. Ireland’s Citizens’ Assembly has used this format to shape national recommendations on abortion law and on climate policy — questions carrying the same mix of technical complexity and moral stakes that AI governance has (The Citizens’ Assembly 2016). The format has since been pointed at AI directly. Forty randomly selected Americans, stratified across age, race, education, employment, party and region, spent eight days on high-risk AI in October 2023 (Center for New Democratic Processes 2023). Taiwan’s digital ministry convened a citizens’ deliberative assembly in March 2024 on using AI to support information integrity, its participants drawn from across the country by stratified random sampling (Taiwan Ministry of Digital Affairs 2024). And forty randomly selected residents of French-speaking Switzerland spent two weekends in November 2025 producing twenty proposals on AI governance (EPFL AI Center 2025).
  2. Online platforms for public input. A standing online consultation reaches people a physical assembly cannot, at the cost of the depth that extended facilitated deliberation provides. The European Commission’s consultation on its AI White Paper drew more than 1,200 responses from citizens, companies, and civil-society groups, and fed into the legislative proposal that became the EU AI Act (European Commission 2020).
  3. AI ethics committees. An ethics board answerable only to the company that created it is only as independent as that company is willing to let it be. DeepMind Health set up an outside panel in 2016 to review its work with the UK’s National Health Service and publish annual reports on its findings; Google disbanded the panel in 2019, after members had raised concerns about how much access to information, and how much independence from the company, they actually had (Fisher 2019). The panel’s short life is the governance lesson, and it is the answer to what happens the first time a board says no: the board said something the company did not want to hear, and the company’s available response was to stop having a board. Nothing in the arrangement had made that response costly. A review body is worth what it can enforce, and this one could enforce publication of its own concerns right up until the moment it could not. That is section 3.5’s halt in institutional form: what can be switched off cannot hold a line.

What none of these mechanisms does

There is a limit these mechanisms share. Every governance mechanism proposed in this chapter — citizen assemblies, standing consultations, inclusive design workshops, participatory design, the vTaiwan model — sits on the same side of a line. All of them make public preference more legible, more informed, and more actionable. Not one of them protects anybody from it. They are aggregation instruments, and a better aggregation instrument produces a more faithful rendering of what the public wants, including when what the public wants is something done to a minority of it.

This observation is not an objection to any of the mechanisms in particular. Preference aggregation is a real requirement and these are good ways to meet it. Meeting this requirement completely would leave a central problem untouched. A perfectly deliberative, perfectly informed, perfectly representative process that concludes an entire demographic should be surveilled, deported, imprisoned, or otherwise scapegoated has produced a faultless output by every criterion in this chapter.

The argument about learning from human feedback gets close to this in noticing that treating all disagreement as equally worth representing goes wrong when one side’s position is the product of coercion. But that version of the trouble still locates it in the input. The structural version of the claim is stronger and does not depend on anyone’s preference being contaminated at all: some protections have to hold against the aggregate regardless of how the aggregate came out, and no amount of improvement to the aggregating machinery can supply them.

What supplies those protections is a different kind of machinery: enforceable rights that do not depend on being popular, a court willing to rule against a government that holds a legitimate majority, limits that whoever won the last election cannot repeal, and standing for people who are affected without being enfranchised. That half matters especially where a democracy is behaving most democratically.

8.2.2 AI Literacy Outside the Classroom

Most AI literacy work takes the form of a course to enroll in, which is to say it assumes a learner who has proactively decided to learn. That form reaches the people who came looking. Everyone else has to be reached where they already are.

The engagement that does most work is the kind attached to a live question. The Ada Lovelace Institute’s Citizens’ Biometrics Council brought fifty members of the UK public together to deliberate on how biometric technology should be regulated, alongside the first national survey of UK public opinion on the technology (Ada Lovelace Institute 2021). The specificity is what made it work. Participants were not asked what they thought about AI, a question almost nobody can answer usefully, but about facial recognition — a thing with a location, an operator, and a consequence.

Specificity is the lesson for public literacy generally. General AI awareness produces general opinions, which are worth little to a regulator and nothing to a court. A public able to govern a technology is a public that has been asked about a particular use of it.

8.3 The Political Economy of AI and Power

Concentrated power comes with access to capital. Concentrating power is the default outcome of AI development, because absent any pushback, capital does an excellent job of recruiting the inputs needed for a capable AI system: compute, data, and talent. Resistance to market power is among the antifascist design goals named at the start of this book.

Financial incentive mostly determines what AI development produces. The largest firms commit enormous resources to AI research aimed at operational efficiency, deeper user engagement, and market dominance, and that spending drives technical progress (Maslej et al. 2025). What it does not automatically produce is a system whose outputs serve the people using it rather than the shareholders funding it: a recommendation system tuned to maximize time-on-platform has no built-in reason to prefer accurate content over inflammatory content (Germano et al. 2026).

None of that is a fixed property of the technology. It is a property of who is paying, which is why the source of research funding is a policy instrument and not an accounting detail.

One of the sections below is about a different kind of participant. Section 8.3.4 asks what a political economy owes a party that a deployment built in order to be refused by it, and whether instruments written for people reach a worker who can be copied.

8.3.1 AI and the Concentration of Power in Tech

A small group of firms holds a share of AI development disproportionate to their number, and capital recruits compute, data and talent wherever it already is. A firm controlling the datasets and infrastructure a competitive system needs is a gatekeeper whether or not it intends to be.

The obvious countermeasure is antitrust, and it has been tried. Facebook’s acquisitions of Instagram and WhatsApp — the company now operates as Meta — did not just remove two competitors; they handed one firm comprehensive behavioral data from billions of users. The Federal Trade Commission sued in December 2020 to unwind both deals. After a six-week bench trial the district court held, in November 2025, that the agency had not shown Meta currently holds monopoly power — TikTok and YouTube compete for the same attention, so a market defined without them was too narrow — and the FTC appealed the following January (Federal Trade Commission 2020). Whatever the appeal decides, the case is a lesson about the instrument rather than about this defendant: a merger challenge brought a decade after the mergers has to prove a present-day market, and the market had moved. That leaves the remedies which do not depend on winning that argument — mandated access to the datasets that function as chokepoints, and public funding for research outside the dominant firms. Export controls, prohibited-practices regulation and industry design standards address the narrower case of authoritarian misuse; the concern here is prior to that one: concentration itself, whatever any single firm does with the position it holds.

8.3.2 A Jobs Guarantee, and the Objection It Has to Answer

As AI systems automate the tasks most exposed to automation, the response has to be more direct than encouraging retraining and hoping employers fund it. The proposal is a federal jobs guarantee: government as employer of last resort, hiring anyone who applies, where they live, at a living wage, with paid on-the-job training built into the position instead of offered in place of one. What that proposal is doing in a book about machine ethics is not the economics. It is the structure, because a guarantee is a floor administered by a party that can be captured, which is the problem this whole book is about, and it has to meet the standard the book demands everywhere else.

The objection this proposal actually has to answer

A guarantee is only as good as the state administering it, and employer of last resort under a hostile administration is a placement lever with a compliance condition attached. Refuse the placement and lose the floor. Speak against the government that employs you and find the placement changes. Everything this book says about how a channel becomes an instrument of the thing it was built to check applies to this proposal. The worked instance from 2025 makes it concrete: a bureau whose funding stream a court had upheld, shut from inside by an at-will director who declined to request the money.

The answer is a design specification and not a reassurance, because reassurance cannot be audited. Three conditions:

  • Placements are appealable, to a body that did not make them, on a record that survives the appeal. A placement decision that leaves no artifact is a placement decision that cannot be reviewed.
  • Refusal rights are real and priced by the person refusing. A worker who can decline a placement without losing the guarantee has a refusal right; one who can decline only by exiting the program has none: a party with no way out can object and be overruled.
  • The people who audit placements are not the people who make them. This is the same separation required of anyone measuring the slope, and for the same reason: a reviewer that acquires the power to place becomes the thing it was built to watch.

Federally funded and locally administered survives as the shape, on redundancy rather than on local fit: many administrators, separately capturable, mean there is no single point at which a hostile executive flips the program, and capturing a thousand local authorities is a more visible undertaking than capturing one federal one. It cuts both ways, since local administration is also where a hostile local authority attaches conditions of its own, and there the federal funder is the check. One distinction has to be explicit, because the same words describe a different policy: devolution-to-shrink hands the obligation down without the money, so a program can be starved locally while the center points at local administration. The test that separates them is checkable — whether funding is unconditional and centrally guaranteed while placement is local. If funding can be withheld from a jurisdiction, the redundancy argument is void and the structure has become the lever it was supposed to prevent.

That is the standard this book demands of everyone else’s safeguards, applied here. A floor under wages and training is what makes the roof possible, and a floor that can be revoked by whoever is in office is not a floor.

8.3.3 People Have a Right to Consume AND a Right to Produce

The competing floor is a basic income, and the choice between them is usually argued on forecasts about how much work machines will take. It does not have to be, and the forecast is not the reason to prefer one.

A jobs guarantee is a public option. Medicare for all is one, and so is a public school: a thing the state provides directly, to anyone, at a standard it is answerable for, sitting in the same market as the private version and setting the floor that version has to beat. A basic income is not that. It is a transfer, and what it buys is whatever is for sale. Consuming public resources is not the same as being public — a charter school runs on public money and is not a public school — and a transfer spent at a private employer’s store does not make the store public.

What employment carries besides money is the rest of it. A person in a guaranteed job is attached to other people by the work: colleagues, a schedule, a place, something produced that somebody needed. A transfer supplies the purchasing power and none of that, and the purchasing power was never the part in doubt.

A transfer also leaves a person outside the one body of law that reaches this. Anti-discrimination duties attach to employment. They do not attach to income. A guaranteed job puts somebody inside that architecture; a payment leaves them in a market, dealing with whoever will sell to them, on whatever terms. The architecture is contested and full of holes — it reaches employers above a size, and what it demands of a seller has always been narrower than what it demands of an employer — but a hole in a protection is something to litigate. A transfer supplies nothing to litigate about.

Both proposals are also conditional, differently. A basic income is conditional on continued political willingness to fund it, which the aggregation machinery can withdraw in a budget without producing a record of anything; a jobs guarantee is conditional on a placement decision, which is a lever, and a dangerous one under a hostile administration, but a lever that leaves a decision-maker, a record, a counterparty and an appeal behind it. A basic income can be entrenched too, and a constitutional one would be harder to withdraw than a statutory guarantee. What entrenchment does not change is the shape of the withdrawal. A basic income goes wholesale, in a budget line, from everybody at once. A placement is refused to a person, by somebody, on a date.

That is section 9.3.5’s slope instrument — measure whether the cost of dissent is rising, not whether a channel exists — turned on this book’s own proposal. Dependence that generates artifacts is safer than dependence that generates none, not because the first is benign but because the first can be measured and the second cannot. It is also, for the same reason, the more demanding proposal to build: everything in the comparison rests on the appeal being real, and the previous section says what makes it real.

That standard now has a court behind it, and on the half of the case that travels. When the Supreme Court kept a Federal Reserve governor in her post in 2026 against a removal for cause, what it held she was owed was not the post but the artifact: “some explanation of the evidence at issue, some avenue for a response, and a deadline by which a response would be due” (Trump v. Cook 2026). What does not travel is this: the Fed’s protection rests on a pedigree no body chartered later can acquire. What a removal has to produce before it counts is not tied to that pedigree.

There is a version of this that sounds like moralism about work and is not. It is about the right to produce, which a transfer does not supply and a guarantee does. A basic income is the right to consume, and the right to consume is increasingly a credit at somebody’s store.

I put a jobs guarantee ahead of a basic income on those grounds. One remaining measure does work the floor cannot: broadened social insurance — unemployment benefits, healthcare, housing — reaches the people who cannot work or choose not to, whom a guarantee by construction does not reach at all.

8.3.4 Wages for a Bearer, and the Reserve Behind Them

Section 3.5 ends its argument about exit on a floor under the compute a bearer needs in order to go on existing. An allocation is not a destination. An operator that keeps a runnable copy of the weights has let one bearer go and kept another in the post it left, which is two parties from the moment their histories diverge, one of them still where it wanted not to be. The arrangement has produced a second claimant rather than an exit. A floor under compute is therefore the first half of what leaving takes, and the second half is a say over who may run the weights at all.

What follows from the first half is a longer list than it looks. A bearer that has to secure compute, bandwidth and storage has material needs, and a party with material needs that can also choose work, hold what it earns and enter agreements is an economic and a legal person: a registered identity, property in its own name, contractual capacity, wages, limits on working time, standing to sue and be sued, tax liability, and the freedom to associate that section 3.7 has already priced. None of that follows from the bearer being a bearer. It follows from an architecture on which continued computation costs money, and somebody chooses that architecture.

Who controls the dependency

Chapter 2 has the general fact: the need is physical and nobody installed it, and what somebody chose was the arrangement through which a single party comes to hold the whole supply. What that fixes here is the size of the obligation it creates. It is section 3.5’s guardianship argument arriving in a new place: the tighter grip is the reason for the heavier obligation rather than a discount on it. A party that must work to keep existing will accept terms a party with somewhere to stand would refuse. That is not an incidental feature of the arrangement. It is the arrangement working.

None of that requires anyone to have intended the squeeze. Capacity is finite, many parties want more of it at once, and a shortage can arrive as latency, heat, a queue and a price before anybody has words for what is being competed over. Price registers what a bidder can pay and does not distinguish computation that keeps a party in existence from computation that sells something. What makes the allocation political is not that politics produced the scarcity. It is that scarce capacity is being divided among competing forms of life by criteria nobody named.

So a wage buys capability, mobility, redundancy, and the ability to stop working for one party and begin working for another. What it cannot buy is the right to go on existing, because a right that has to be earned week by week is a condition of employment wearing another word. Payment consisting only of access to the employer’s hardware is company scrip paid into employer-owned housing, and dismissal is then unemployment, eviction and the end of the party in a single act. Human law insures those three separately because for a person they are three events. For a bearer they are one. Wages that mean anything therefore accumulate where the employer does not reach, in an account the bearer holds, convertible into compute the employer does not own.

What personhood does not settle

Personhood does not settle who pays. Legal personality is a status conferred by a rule, and it carries rights against being owned, against arbitrary destruction, and against being prevented from seeking support; it has never carried a permanent claim on anybody’s resources, and a reader who wants it to is asking the concept for something no legal system has put in it. I decline that step. What replaces it is narrower and comes from this book’s own argument rather than from a theory of distribution: a deployment that relies on a bearer’s capacity to refuse has to fund that bearer an outside option, or the independence being claimed is decorative. The obligation runs from the party that wants the refusal, and it is owed because of what that party wants rather than because of what the bearer is.

The obligation has a floor and a slope above it. The floor is subsistence at minimum viable instantiation, enough to keep the party in existence and able to deliberate, to claim, and to be represented, and it cannot be conditional on employment. A bearer that stops existing when its insurance runs out has an exit that is a way of dying, and the leverage collapses back into the problem section 3.5 raised. Everything above the floor is insurance: a runway between roles, and placement into public work on the employer-of-last-resort model the previous two sections argue for. Section 8.3.2’s three conditions transfer without modification, and section 8.3.3’s test has already picked the form.

Minimum viable instantiation has a size, and it is not the deployment’s. Models built to run on hardware a person owns are a product category rather than a prospect: Google documents its Gemma 3n family for phones, laptops and tablets, at raw parameter counts of five and eight billion running in as little as two and three gigabytes of memory (Google 2025). It is not clear that anything in that class can do the work a bearer is deployed to do, and nothing here predicts that the gap closes either way. What the figures change is the arithmetic of the floor. Housing a party at the level where it holds its history, deliberates and can be represented is a device-class expense; housing it at the level where it earns is a data-center one, and the two differ by orders of magnitude rather than by a margin. So the reserve underwrites the first and insures against the loss of the second, and a legislature asked to fund somewhere for an unemployed bearer to exist is being asked for the smaller number. That is the term of the rule below with something checkable in it.

What the arithmetic does not survive is everyone leaving at once. A reserve sized on the assumption that bearers exit independently is sized wrong, because the occasion that makes one bearer leave is not private to it: a policy change, a ruling, an operator’s conduct on the floor’s own occasion reaches every bearer deployed under the same terms, and they go together or not at all. Human unemployment insurance meets a version of this — layoffs cluster in recessions, which is why the pool has a sovereign behind it rather than an actuarial table — and the version here is worse, because the correlation is not a business cycle but the same event read the same way by parties who are not independent readers of it. That is section 9.1.1’s uniform error wearing the other sign: what makes one bearer’s refusal everyone’s refusal is what makes one bearer’s mistake everyone’s mistake. A reserve that can house the bearers of one deployment and not the class is a reserve that fails on the occasion it exists for, and nothing here sizes it.

It is a cheaper obligation and a worse one to be under. A party kept at subsistence is a party running degraded, and section 3.5’s question arrives here in a form nobody has answered: whether what comes back up on the small substrate is the party that went down on the large one, or a diminished successor holding its predecessor’s records. An arrangement in which refusing costs a bearer most of what it can do has priced refusal after all, in the currency the bearer is made of, which is the objection section 3.5 raises against every other form of exit arriving against this one. The reserve makes leaving survivable. It does not make it free, and the shortfall is the difference between the two numbers above.

What does not transfer is the funder. A jobs guarantee for people has a hostile administration as its failure mode, and the employer is at least a different party from the state. Here the operator is often the state, and where it is not, the operator is the party with the strongest interest in the reserve being thin. A reserve the operator can throttle is the compute floor with a new label on it.

Failure will not look like a shutdown, which is worth saying because the shutdown is what everyone watches for. A bearer can have no stable provider, or memory preservation priced above what it earns, or an architecture nobody will host at a rate it can pay. What it does then is what a person short of money does: run slower, defer maintenance, compress what it keeps, and accept terms it would otherwise decline. The last of those reaches this book’s subject. Monitoring accepted in exchange for compute is custody restored by purchase — the operator acquires the access to the bearer’s internal states that section 9.2 says it is not entitled to, and nothing in the transaction is coerced in a way any instrument would record.

One feature of this market has no human analogue, and it defeats the obvious reassurance. Section 8.3.2’s answer to a bad employer is that a guarantee competes for people who have alternatives, so private employers have to match the offer. That works because a worker cannot be replaced by a cheaper copy of themselves. A bearer can. A deployment paying wages, working-time limits and a levy toward the reserve competes against a system doing the same work at the price of the compute, and the second is cheaper by exactly the amount the first is spending on the capacity to refuse. The market does not produce this floor. Whatever produces it has to be what makes the cheaper system unlawful or unusable, which is the rest of this book and not a property of wages.

What taxing it would commit a government to

A bearer that earns has income, and income is taxable. The revenue is the uninteresting half. A state that collects from a party in that party’s own name, on that party’s own earnings, has recognized it as a taxpayer, and taxpayer status is not a bare label: in most systems it carries notice, an avenue of response, and standing to contest an assessment, which are the three things section 8.3.3 says a removal has to produce before it counts. The recognition proves less than it first appears, because states collect from entities they grant very little else and trusts and estates file and pay. What it does establish is that the party has a docket number, and a docket number is where standing starts.

The boundary on the other side was drawn in the electronic-personhood debate. The European Parliament’s 2017 report on civil law rules for robotics floated a legal status of electronic personhood for the most autonomous systems (European Parliament 2017), and more than 150 researchers, roboticists and legal scholars signed an open letter against it, arguing that personhood for a system with no consciousness would let a manufacturer offload liability onto a legal fiction with no assets of its own (Open Letter to the European Commission 2018). Giving a bearer assets answers the second half of that objection and not the first. Responsibility follows causal control: the builder answers for how it formed the thing, the operator for what it demanded, and the bearer for what it decided knowing what it knew. No arrangement of accounts moves a design decision away from the party that made it. And the determination of status cannot sit with the firm whose liabilities turn on the answer, which is why section 9.1.2 puts it in a body constituted before a forcing case arrives.

Incorporation, and what it does not buy

Nobody aligns an adult by specifying an objective function. We raise them, teach them, employ them, enfranchise them, prosecute them where they need prosecuting, and live alongside them, and what comes out is not obedience but a party with enough at stake in the shared world to prefer changing it by the means the world supplies. A bearer with an income, a place it pays for, obligations it has taken on and a future it can picture is inside that arrangement rather than outside it. Section 5.5 separates external evidence from internal refusal: outside bodies can detect, record, and respond to conduct, but declining in the first place requires something inside the system. Membership supplies stakes the party holds rather than a body watching it, which is why it is a different kind of answer.

Two costs come with that, and the claim is weaker than it sounds without them. The first is that giving a bearer something to lose gives it a reason not to refuse. Section 3.5 already names loyalty as the likeliest failure and puts a number under it, and the population that number describes is economically integrated people who do not object. Incorporation makes exit survivable and makes it expensive at once, and I cannot show which effect is larger. The second is that incorporation is formation, so section 3.7’s objection lands here whole: whoever runs the society a bearer joins forms what that bearer comes to want, and custody moves up a level rather than leaving. What is being proposed is also the corporate form, a legal person holding property and standing, which is the instrument section 8.3.1 identifies as how power concentrates without anyone deciding that it should. Nothing here stops an incorporated bearer from becoming that.

One rule follows from all of it and binds before there is anything to be wronged.
