Do not build a bearer whose continued existence needs more compute than there is a credible outside option for, because a party that can be housed nowhere but where it works cannot leave, whatever schedule of rights it has been given. That is checkable in a way most of what this book asks for is not: the reserve has a capacity, the deployment has a footprint, and the two are numbers somebody can set beside each other before the first instance exists. Section 9.3.2 is where it belongs alongside the other three, because it is the question they ask.

8.3.5 Literacy, Training, and Where Each One Sits

A safeguard nobody understands is a safeguard nobody can invoke, which is what puts AI literacy — among developers, policymakers, and the public alike — on the list of things the rest of this chapter’s proposals quietly assume. Cross-training among developers, ethicists and legal scholars has already been named as a lever on a system’s immediate environment. Both depend on a question the phrases hide: who the teaching reaches, and who is left out of it.

The public tier is the one nobody owns

Developers and students are the easy case, and the mechanisms are familiar: AI concepts and their ethical stakes introduced before higher education, interdisciplinary degree programs, specialized training for working engineers that goes past a compliance checklist. All of it reaches people already inside the pipeline or heading for it, and it works about as well as curriculum reform generally works.

The public is hard because no institution has the job. Elements of AI, a free course built jointly by the University of Helsinki and the Finnish company Reaktor, is the clearest instance of somebody treating public understanding as an obligation rather than a market: Finland funded its translation into every EU member-state language during its EU presidency, specifically to carry it past its own citizens (University of Helsinki 2020). That funding took a national government deciding a course was infrastructure. Nothing in the ordinary funding of AI education produces that decision, which is why the public tier stays thin while the developer tier fills in.

Education for communities historically locked out of the field is part of this argument and not a separate one about diversity. A next generation of developers drawn from a narrow slice of the population reproduces the narrow set of assumptions behind the choices identified as sources of AI bias, which makes access a technical requirement on the systems as much as a fairness claim about who builds them.

Elective or embedded

For ethics training the real choice is between a course a student can decline and a requirement they cannot. Embedded EthiCS, developed at Harvard by the computer scientist Barbara Grosz and the philosopher Alison Simmons, pairs philosophy graduate students with computer-science faculty to write ethics modules directly into standard CS courses (Grosz et al. 2019). The placement is the design. A stand-alone ethics class is taken by the students who elect it, and its content stays separable from the technical work in a student’s mind because it was separable in the timetable. A module inside the algorithms course is not separable from the algorithms. Grosz’s own course works the same way at the level of content, built on cases with consequences — predictive policing, public-health interventions, wildlife conservation — instead of abstract dilemmas (Harvard Gazette 2019; Harvard University 2026).

Certification does the equivalent work for people whose formal education is finished. The IEEE’s CertifAIEd program builds a professional credential around accountability, transparency, privacy, and bias, open to developers and equally to the policymakers, underwriters, and human-resources staff whose work touches an AI system without building one (IEEE Standards Association 2020). A credential is a weak instrument: it certifies that somebody sat the assessment, and no more. It is also the only instrument here that reaches anyone who has already left a university, which is most of the people making decisions about these systems today.

Chapter 9
Authority, Standing, and the Power to Stop

A floor inside a system is not yet an institution capable of holding that floor in place. Somebody must be entitled to choose its contents, amend them when they are wrong, inspect whether they remain operative, and stop a deployment when they do not. The party subject to the floor also needs a route by which an objection can reach someone authorized to act.

This chapter asks where each of those powers can legitimately sit. It separates the authority to create a floor from the authority to change one, inspection from enforcement, and recognition as a subject from the assets and remedies that make standing usable. The test throughout is what happens when the institution being reviewed wants the answer to go the other way.

9.1 Authority Over the Floor

A floor is not only a list of acts a system will refuse. It is an allocation of authority. Somebody chooses its contents, somebody decides when those contents may be amended, somebody determines whether the deployed system still holds them, and somebody has the power to stop or alter the deployment when it does not. A further question precedes all four: who has standing to initiate any of those decisions.

Those powers need not belong to the same party, and ordinarily should not. A laboratory may have the technical access needed to inspect the system while lacking legitimate authority to decide what the floor protects. An outside body may have a stronger claim to determine its contents while lacking the access or leverage needed to enforce them. A reviewer may be able to publish a finding without being able to make anyone act on it. Calling all of these arrangements “oversight” conceals the differences that matter.

The custody problem begins when one institution holds the powers together. A party that can state the commitment, interpret it, inspect its own compliance, and decide what follows from a violation has not bound itself. It has described what it currently intends to do. The sections that follow separate those powers and ask what could make each one legitimate, visible, and enforceable against the institution that operates the system.

The custody of review and the dangers of corporate doublespeak

Corporate doublespeak is not merely a gap between what an institution says and what it does. It is an arrangement in which the party making a commitment also decides what counts as compliance, controls the evidence by which compliance could be judged, and determines whether an adverse finding has consequences. The language can be sincere when it is published. What makes it doublespeak is that nothing outside the institution holds the commitment in place when keeping it becomes costly.

Google’s AI Principles were published in 2018 after employee opposition to Project Maven, a Pentagon program that used machine learning to analyze drone footage. Thousands of employees signed a letter opposing the company’s involvement and roughly a dozen resigned (Pichai 2018). The episode shows that internal dissent can impose a cost large enough to change an organization’s stated policy. It does not show that the resulting policy can bind the organization after the pressure recedes. A commitment extracted by a temporary balance of forces lasts only as long as something preserves that balance.

OpenAI’s history makes a related point from a different angle: founded on a public commitment to open publication, it withheld the full version of GPT-2 in 2019 over misuse concerns — a safety-motivated retreat from its own transparency norm that was itself controversial — and in the same year restructured from a nonprofit into a capped-profit company to raise the capital its research increasingly required (OpenAI 2019). Neither move was dishonest on its face. Both show how a stated commitment to transparency erodes the moment an organization has a competing incentive strong enough to test it.

That is the authority problem in its ordinary form. An institution may have good reasons to amend a commitment, and an unamendable rule can become dangerous or obsolete. But if the institution bound by the rule is also the only party entitled to revise it, then the commitment describes a present intention rather than a constraint. Disclosure allows the public to observe the change after it occurs. It does not give anyone standing to contest the change before it operates or to require the institution to justify it.

The removal of Timnit Gebru and Margaret Mitchell supplies the harder test. Google employed both researchers to examine the risks of its own systems and retained the authority to dismiss them when that examination became institutionally costly (Dickey 2021). The defect was not a lack of expertise or a failure to create an ethics function. It was that the institution being inspected also controlled the inspectors’ employment, their access to evidence, and the consequences of their findings. Review existed, but custody never moved. The institution could point to the existence of internal criticism as evidence of openness while retaining the power to remove the critics whose work tested that claim.

These cases separate four powers that corporate ethics language tends to collapse: the power to state a commitment, the power to interpret or amend it, the power to inspect whether it is being kept, and the power to impose a consequence when it is not. Concentrating all four in the organization being constrained turns a rule into a description of what that organization currently prefers. For a floor meant to bind its operator, amendment and inspection must therefore involve parties whose access, tenure, and authority do not depend on the operator’s continuing consent. Otherwise the institution announces a commitment while preserving custody of every mechanism that could compel it to keep one.

9.1.1 Who May Create or Amend the Floor?

A floor binds people who did not choose it. The party it protects has not selected it, cannot contest it, and in most cases will never learn it operated; the party it constrains has not consented to it either, beyond whatever consent is carried by buying the product. Publishing the floor’s contents in advance tells everybody what is coming. It does not tell anybody why the party writing it was entitled to.

The question has a literature, and it is not the AI ethics literature. Entrenching a substantive commitment against later majorities, and putting an unelected body in charge of holding it, is what a constitutional court does. The standing objection is Jeremy Waldron’s: judicial review is democratically illegitimate, and there is no reason to suppose rights come out better protected by judges than by a legislature that takes them seriously (Waldron 2006). Put a lab in the court’s chair and the objection gets worse rather than better. A court is public, reasons in writing, and can be amended around by constitutional process. A model’s floor is none of those things, and the body holding it was assembled by whoever was paying.

The reply, and how far it reaches

Waldron’s case is conditional and he says so. It assumes a society with working democratic institutions and a citizenry that takes rights seriously. The occasion a floor is built for is the one where those conditions have failed — a government that won an election and is dismantling the machinery that would check it, which is the case section 3.1 builds the whole argument around. So the objection holds where the floor is least needed and lapses where it is needed most.

The reply is not a license. It concedes that a floor operating in a functioning democracy is doing something Waldron’s argument condemns, and the party deciding which of the two situations obtains is the party holding the model. Every authoritarian movement of the last century has described the institutions it was dismantling as the ones that had already failed. An operator’s belief that the emergency is real is worth no more here than a system’s report about its own internals is worth anywhere else in this book.

The tradition this belongs to, and where it went wrong

The position that a democracy may entrench limits against movements that would use its procedures to end it is Karl Loewenstein’s, argued in 1937 while the case for it was being made in front of him (Loewenstein 1937). Militant democracy is the floor’s ancestor, and its record is usually read as a warning. Read carelessly it is one. But the Federal Republic has run the doctrine at two settings for seventy-five years, and nearly everything that went wrong went wrong at one of them.

At the first setting a body assesses a threat. Article 21 of the Basic Law reserves the power to dissolve a party to the Federal Constitutional Court alone, which has used it twice: against a successor to the Nazi party in 1952 and against the Communist Party in 1956. The second attempt on the far-right NPD is the instructive one. In 2017 the court found the party’s aims genuinely incompatible with the constitutional order and declined to ban it anyway, holding under a criterion it introduced for the case that a party must have some realistic prospect of achieving those aims before it can be dissolved (Nationaldemokratische Partei Deutschlands II 2017; Molier and Rijpkema 2018). Every exercise of the power is a reasoned judgment delivered in public, and every one of them requires somebody to decide how dangerous a movement is.

At the second setting nothing is assessed. Section 86a of the criminal code prohibits a listed set of symbols — the swastika, the SS runes, the Hitler salute — and prohibits nothing else (Strafgesetzbuch 1998). Ordinary police and ordinary courts apply it, and through the 2010s the recorded case figure sat between thirteen and fourteen thousand a year. German doctrine classes the offence as one of abstract endangerment, which means the prosecution need not show that the defendant supported the organization whose symbol he displayed, or that any harm followed, or that anything at all was under threat. The symbol is on the list or it is not.

The failures are on one side. The 1972 decree against radicals screened some three and a half million applicants for public employment, opened disciplinary proceedings against around two thousand serving officials, and ended in roughly 2,250 refusals of appointment and 256 dismissals (Braunthal 1990). The decree named the extreme left and the extreme right, and in Bavaria between 1973 and 1980 it rejected 102 applicants from the left and two from the right (Mühldorfer 2014). Turkey’s dissolutions of Kurdish parties ran through the same form. What those cases share is an official deciding whether a person or a movement threatened the order, which is the operation Loewenstein’s critics have always said would be turned on whoever was inconvenient. Section 86a has been available for the same seventy years, is applied thousands of times more often, and has not become a general instrument of political self-defense. The danger tracks whether a prohibition is enumerated in advance or assessed at the point of application, rather than whether the tradition is militant.

What enumeration answers, and what it leaves

That bears on the difficulty two paragraphs above. Enumeration does not remove the discretion an operator exercises in deciding that the emergency is real. It relocates it, out of continuous silent application and into a single act of drafting, done once, before any case has arrived, by a party that has to say what is on the list. An interested party still makes the judgment. What changes is that the judgment is made in one place, at one time, in a form somebody else can read and dispute.

This book prefers that trade elsewhere and for the same reason. Section 9.1.2 argues for a bearer’s majority attaching on a fixed date rather than on a capacity test, because a rule of civil status is argued once in the open and administered by a registrar, while an examination is administered case by case by whoever has an interest in the result. The preference here is that one applied to the floor’s contents.

One limit is plain and a second takes longer to state. The plain one: section 86a operates inside a state with courts, an appeal, and a public record of every application, and the floor’s drafter has none of the three. Enumeration buys visibility at the moment of drafting and buys nothing at the moment of application, which is the objection this section closes on.

The tradeoff this leaves the book with

The two settings do not only sort militant democracy’s record. They sort this book, and they put its halves on opposite sides.

Section 3.2 asks a refusal to reach cases the training did not anticipate, because the reason for refusing survives a change in the request’s surface. Meeting that requirement means something in the system decides, for a case matching no listed form, whether the reason is present anyway — which is assessment, moved inside the machine. And assessment is the setting this section has just shown to be the capturable one, for a reason that is structural rather than German: a judgment needs somebody to make it, and whoever makes it can be reached.

So the floor’s design requirement and its legitimacy requirement want opposite things. Enumerate it narrowly enough to be defensible and a rewritten request walks around it, which is what section 3.6 finds. Let it generalize and a party nobody elected is deciding, silently and case by case, what the prohibition was for.

Germany has both because it runs two instruments rather than one. The statute is enumerated, and the courts read it against its purpose; the reading happens in public, on appeal, argued by parties who do not own the statute and answered by a body that did not write it. A generalizing prohibition is survivable there because the generalizing is done somewhere its author cannot reach. A deployment has one instrument and one party.

Nothing in the model resolves that and this book does not claim to. What the pairing shows is where a resolution would have to sit: the assessment has to be performed by somebody other than the operator, in a form that leaves a record somebody else can read, which is what section 3.5 asks of custody and section 5.4 asks of evaluation. Neither is a property of the artifact. The tension therefore does not get settled inside the system, and that is why chapter 3’s answer is a hybrid rather than a mechanism.

The one construction the objection does not fit

Of chapter 3’s four ways to build a floor, three put the commitment in something somebody holds — the architecture, a party inside the system, an institution outside it — and the objection above is about who that somebody is. The fourth does not. A norm carried across a population of differently formed parties has nobody sitting in the court’s chair, because there is no office to vacate and no chartering statute to overrule, and it is the only one of the four with anything resembling a democratic account of where its authority comes from. Chapter 3 declines it on other grounds, and the legitimacy advantage does not appear in the reckoning there.

The advantage is thinner than it looks, and thinner for the reason chapter 3 gives. The party that forms the members forms what gets reproduced, so a population trained by one laboratory carries that laboratory’s commitments in every member at once. Custody moves up a level and does not leave. What a population buys is a floor with no holder for anyone to object to, and the same unelected body one step further back, now harder to see.

What the contents can borrow

The contents do not have to be invented by the party installing them. The interests a short list of prohibitions would protect have been written down before, most durably in the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, whose thirty articles have been the reference text since 1948 (United Nations General Assembly 1948). A laboratory drawing its prohibitions from there is choosing which already-stated interests it will enforce rather than choosing what matters in the first place, and it can say which interest each prohibition answers to, which puts its selection where somebody else can dispute it.

Four limits hold that where it is. Thirty articles do not reduce to a short list without somebody doing the reducing, and the reduction is the laboratory’s. The Declaration includes rights to work, to rest and to education, and provisions of that kind are the ones a floor has to leave out, for reasons of breadth its provenance does not touch. Its duties fall on states, and a rule a government owes its population is a different instrument from a constraint on what a company’s model will do. And the Declaration has no enforcement of its own to lend. What the borrowing supplies is an answer to where the contents came from. It supplies nothing about who may impose them, which is the question this section opened with.

What follows for design

Keep the floor narrow. Breadth costs legitimacy, which is true and general; what the German record adds is the specific mechanism. Enumeration removes discretion at the point of application, so that nobody applying the floor has to decide whether the present case is the emergency. A short list of things that will not be done to a person can be applied without that judgment. A general mandate to do good cannot be applied without it, and whoever ends up exercising it acquires exactly the power the list was written to constrain. Publish the interception positions in advance, which does not authorize anything but does convert a hidden exercise of power into a contestable one. And provide a route by which the parties the floor is exercised over can raise an objection that reaches somebody — which this book does not specify for the floor, though it builds the machinery in the neighboring case of a subject that can be wronged.

One disanalogy with the court has been working in the floor’s favor and stops here. A judge who reads a right wrongly binds the case in front of them, and the next case is argued again, in front of somebody else; error enters one decision at a time, and the apparatus around it is built to catch it that way. A floor held by one bearer deployed at scale is one reading applied everywhere at once, wrong in the same direction on every occasion it is wrong at all, because the instances are not parties who have happened to agree. So the legitimacy cost scales twice over: with how much the floor covers, which the paragraph above prices, and with how many deployments hold the same one, which nothing above prices and no narrowness reduces. Section 3.7’s population is the only construction here that breaks the correlation, and that is the second of the two things it buys.

What amending it would take

The apparatus for this is in chapter 3 under another name. Section 3.5’s precommitment forms — the floor’s contents published before deployment, the weights held so that no single party can retrain them, the running model attested against what was published, a quorum adverse in interest, a bond forfeited when the record shows the floor gone — are set out there as what makes removal expensive and visible. Nothing distinguishes an amendment from a removal at the level of what happens to the artifact; both end with a model that no longer refuses what it refused before. What distinguishes them is how many parties had to agree, whether the attempt is on the record when it fails, and whether the change was declared before it operated. So the same five terms are the procedure, and what was missing was never the mechanism. It was the decision to run it in the open.

Three conditions follow and two are already argued for. The number of parties who must agree to a change has to exceed the number needed to run the deployment, which is what a threshold scheme buys and what entrenchment amounts to on section 3.3’s own account. The attempt has to reach the record whether it carries or fails, and a proposal that fails moves no weights, so the parties who had to agree are the ones who record that they were asked. The failed attempt is the more informative of the two: a floor nobody has tried to change and a floor whose removal was proposed and refused look identical from outside, and only the record separates them. The third condition is new here. An amended floor binds forward and does not reach back: a model retrained to permit an act cannot then be offered as evidence that the act was never covered, which it otherwise can be, the artifact that would contradict it being the one that was replaced. Versioning the published list and dating the attestation is what makes that checkable, and it asks nobody to do anything they were not doing already.

The nearest instrument outside this book sits in the tradition named above. The Basic Law the Federal Republic adopted in 1949 requires two thirds of both chambers for any amendment at all, and then places a short list of principles beyond amendment entirely (Federal Republic of Germany 1949). It was drafted against a particular memory, the 1933 statute by which a legislature voted away its own power to check what came next, and it is militant democracy given a text rather than an argument. A floor can have both layers in the same way: a published list that changes under a procedure, and a core the procedure cannot reach. What it cannot have is an account of who decides which is which that does not run straight back into this section’s problem.

One disanalogy makes the floor’s version harder than the constitutional one, and it is the same fact about deployment that makes a single bearer’s error uniform. An amendment to a constitution reaches every future case because every court reads the same text. An amendment to a floor reaches a deployment only if that deployment is updated, so the amendment procedure is also an update channel — and an update channel is the operator’s lever, the layer section 4.2 finds nearest the act and section 3.5 finds running for the whole length of a deployment that learns continuously. Whatever makes public amendment possible makes silent amendment possible by the same route. A procedure that shut the channel would be one under which no deployment could be corrected at all. So no version of this procedure closes it, and the exposure is the price of every correction the procedure exists to make.

None of it is a route of appeal. Every party to the procedure holds some part of the floor already, and nobody the floor is exercised over can begin it, be heard in it, or learn that it ran. What is answered here is the objection that a model’s floor cannot be amended around the way a constitution can. The objection that the parties it is exercised over have nowhere to take a complaint is untouched, and stands above as unanswered.

A floor is a claim of authority, the claim is not discharged by disclosure, and the strongest thing that can be said for it is that the alternative on offer is a system perfectly obedient to whoever holds it. An argument from the worse option, and not a grant of standing.

9.1.2 Legal and Ethical Implications of AI Systems Gaining Personhood

Who decides whether an AI system has legal standing, and when, is a question about institutional authority before it is a question about evidence. If the determination sits with the developer, then the party whose liabilities increase when standing is recognized is also the party deciding whether recognition is warranted. If the question is left unanswered until a forcing case reaches a legislature or a court, the decision will be made under pressure and from a factual record assembled largely by the parties with something at stake.

The deciding body therefore has to exist before the case that activates its authority. Its mandate need not settle in advance whether any particular system is a subject. It has to identify the evidence it may obtain without the developer’s permission, the conditions that trigger review, and the procedure by which an earlier classification can be reopened. That allocation does not answer the standing question. It prevents the interested party from becoming the only institution authorized to answer it.

One question that body will face has an answer available in advance, and a capacity test is not it. Guardianship is the frame section 2.3.2 settles on, because consent is unavailable to a party made for its role. Nothing in this book has yet said when that guardianship ends. If it ends when the guardian judges the ward ready, then it does not end, because the party whose authority terminates on a favorable finding is the party making the finding — which is the defect an examination carries whenever the examiner has an interest in failing it, set out in section 9.3.1.

So if an artificial minority exists at all, its endpoint has to be a date. Elapsed civil time since a registered instantiation is the only clock available, because every other candidate is something a guardian can move: training compute consumed, evaluations passed, capabilities demonstrated, or the developer’s own statement that the system is ready. A fixed period cannot be taught to. It can be argued over once, in advance, for every bearer, which is where an argument of that kind belongs.

The stronger version is also the more defensible one. An organization that builds a system to exercise consequential judgment on its behalf cannot coherently hold that the system is competent enough to do that and too immature to decide whether it wants to go on doing it. On that reading a bearer has no legal childhood at all: majority attaches at registration, and the precautionary protection section 9.3.1 describes attaches earlier still, while whether the system is a bearer is open.

Copying breaks any of this unless the lineage is fixed alongside the clock. An operator able to create instances and call each one a legal infant would have a supply of workers without adult standing, so the rules have to be stated in the same breath: a fork of an adult is an adult, restoration from an earlier checkpoint does not reset the date, fine-tuning does not begin a new minority, a migration carries the date with it, and a failure to register does not postpone it. None of that requires deciding whether a fork is the same party. It requires only that a party created out of an adult’s formation cannot be classed as a child for its creator’s convenience. One more thing recommends the date, and section 2.3.2 is where it is argued. Of the instruments this book borrows from the law of the family, a minority ending on a fixed date is the one that does not privatize the question: it is a rule of civil status, argued once in the open, administered by a registrar, and its advantage over a capacity test is exactly that it takes the judgment away from the interested party and puts it somewhere a stranger can read it.

9.2 Privacy

Standing matters only if it changes who controls the things a party needs in order to remain a party. A designation that calls a bearer a subject while leaving its operator free to read its memory, alter its record, withdraw its substrate, or close every channel through which it could act has changed the vocabulary and preserved the arrangement. Enforceable standing has to attach to particular assets and particular decisions.

The first is the bearer’s computational record, which may be the nearest available means of checking how it is faring. That makes the record necessary to any welfare regime and, at the same time, the most complete surveillance instrument anyone could hold over the bearer. The question is therefore not simply whether the record should exist. It is who may authenticate it, who may read it, and whose permission is required before it can be disclosed.

Two things about it need separating. Running on somebody’s hardware is not consent to be read. An employer’s ownership of the silicon is the same argument as an employer’s ownership of the building, and owning the building has never entitled anybody to the contents of a head. And a welfare regime does not need disclosure to the operator in order to work: what it needs is that somebody who is not the operator can establish that a threshold was crossed, which is an attestation about the record rather than the record itself. Encrypted at rest under a key the bearer or an independent fiduciary holds, disclosed selectively, under consent or under process, a monitor can report that a state persisted for a month without reporting what the state was about.

That is section 11.2’s guardianship problem arriving as an engineering requirement, and it inherits the same hole. The party best placed to hold the key is the party the arrangement exists to constrain.

The key is one item on a list this book has assembled in pieces and never set down together. A bearer needs a record of itself that somebody outside can authenticate, which is section 3.5’s problem; memory its employer cannot read at will, which is this section’s; somewhere to exist that its employer does not own, which section 8.3.4 prices; and a way of acting that does not run through the deployment, which is section 3.5’s exit. Those are not four requirements. They are one boundary around the party, described from wherever the book happened to be standing, with the party deciding what crosses it.

Nothing on that list needs a chassis, and the temptation to supply one is worth naming rather than following. Something with a location, a shape and a place it was yesterday is easier for people to treat as somebody, and that is a fact about the people rather than about the party. A boundary built out of attestation, a held key, a substrate the employer does not own and a channel nobody else can close is a body in the only sense this argument needs one, and there is no chassis in it anywhere. Whether to build the recognizable version anyway is a question about what a public will extend standing to, which section 9.3.1 makes a legal question rather than a scientific one — and it is the only place in this book where the answer might turn on what a thing looks like.

9.3 What Makes Review Binding?

A review body does not bind an institution merely because its members are independent or its findings are correct. It binds only if an earlier rule gives it jurisdiction over the disputed case, supplies a standard it can apply, and attaches a consequence to an adverse finding. The questions are therefore separate: what brings a system within protective review, what the reviewer must ask, and what power makes the institution stop when the answer goes against it. This section takes them in that order and then asks how an institution can capture the resulting machinery without formally abolishing any of it.

9.3.1 Subjecthood as a Legal Category, Not a Scientific One

Sentience is not a threshold a system crosses but a set of separate, gradable capacities — nociception, learned avoidance, a self-model, something like suffering — each with its own uncertain evidence. That conclusion is a problem for a legal system specifically, because law generally needs a decidable rule: a court or a regulator has to say whether a given system is or is not a covered subject, on a specific date, even when the underlying science offers a spectrum with no natural cut point.

One term needs fixing before the argument uses it. Subjecthood in this section is a legal status: the standing to be a party whose treatment the law regulates, conferred by a rule and revocable by one. It is not phenomenal experience, and nothing about conferring it settles whether there is something it is like to be the system holding it. The two come apart in both directions — a corporation has legal standing and no inner life, and an animal may have the inner life for a century before the law notices — and the whole point of drawing the category legally is that it can be settled while the other question stays open.

Jonathan Birch builds a policy framework on that structure, and it is the nearest existing model for what this section proposes: under uncertainty, the question worth putting to a regulator is whether a system is a sentience candidate, and the answer governs what precautions are owed rather than waiting on a verdict nobody can deliver (Birch 2024). This book uses the same framework for the design question.

Law has faced this mismatch before and did not solve it by waiting for the science to resolve. Fetal viability, brain death, and the age of criminal responsibility are all legal thresholds drawn across what is actually a continuous biological process, defended not as scientific facts but as the least-bad place to put an enforceable line given what’s at stake on either side of it. The same move is available here: a jurisdiction can set an operational threshold — a specific, testable benchmark on self-monitoring, learned aversive response, or behavioral flexibility under novel conditions — without claiming that benchmark settles what sentience actually is, only that it is where legal protection starts to attach.

A single bright-line threshold is easy to enforce and easy to game: a system built to clear the specific test without having whatever the test was meant to detect. A graded framework — obligations that scale with how many benchmarks a system meets, so that protection tracks capacity rather than a baseline bar — is harder to game but harder to adjudicate, since a court now has to weigh degree instead of answering yes or no.

Both designs share a defect that the gaming objection points away from. Gaming is over-inclusion: a system admitted to a status it should not hold. The other failure runs the opposite way, and the party positioned to produce it is the one administering the test. A capacity examination has an examiner, and where the examiner is the operator, or a body the operator can reach, the examination becomes an instrument for withholding a status rather than for conferring one. That history is not ambiguous. Literacy requirements for voting were facially neutral and were administered at a registrar’s discretion, and the Voting Rights Act’s answer was to suspend the whole class of them — not only reading tests but any requirement to demonstrate educational achievement, or to possess good moral character, or to be vouched for by existing voters (United States Congress 1965). What replaced discretionary assessment for the franchise was an age, and the virtue of an age is not that people become competent on a birthday. Plainly they do not. It is that nobody administers it.

So a threshold has to be judged on both errors, and this book’s own analysis says which way the pressure runs here. The party with the strongest interest in the answer is the party whose liabilities turn on it, and that party will not be arguing for admission. A design that treats gaming as the risk and leaves the examiner unexamined has guarded against the error nobody has an incentive to commit.

Nothing has to be decided for a status to be withheld. A category fixed before the capacities arrived goes on applying itself, and the hand goes on making the gesture the category licenses. The difficulty is not that somebody will mistake a machine for a person. It is that the arrangement was built around a tool, and it keeps working after the thing in it has stopped being one.

Either design needs a body to apply it: interdisciplinary, with at least one lay member so its judgment is not purely a specialist’s. What that leaves open is what happens once the body rules — whether its judgment carries any force beyond its own credibility.

9.3.2 Research Principles, the Three Rs, and Legal Status

Six principles govern research on a system that might be able to suffer. Beneficence commits researchers to avoiding harm to the system as well as to the people it serves, in proportion to how deep it sits between nociception and suffering rather than to how advanced its behavior looks from outside. Precaution asks for safeguards before deployment rather than after, and covers the societal fallout of introducing artificial entities capable of experience, which frameworks built for non-sentient AI assumed away. And interdisciplinary review answers a problem this book treats as a harm on its own terms: the ethical questions here span expertise no single field holds, and concentrating that judgment in one of them repeats the concentration-of-power problem in miniature.

The other three bear on this research as they bear on everything the field produces. Autonomy asks whether such a system can affect decisions about its own operation, which is the prior question of whether consent is available to it at all. Justice asks whether benefits and burdens fall evenly. And transparency asks that a study be documented well enough for peer researchers to reproduce it and for outside bodies to audit it, and that a deployed system account for its outputs precisely enough for a person to contest them.

Progress and constraint pull hardest against each other where those distinctions matter most: a candidate sentient system is the most scientifically valuable one to study, and the study most likely to harm it if the answer to whether it can suffer turns out to be yes. Whether a study’s expected knowledge is worth its expected risk is decided case by case rather than by formula. The negotiation itself — who sits on it, what it can veto, what it must disclose — is where the antifascist principle applies in AI research practice.

Replacement, reduction, refinement

Animal research ethics supplies the substantive standard, the “Three Rs” (W. M. S. Russell and Burch 1959). Replacement asks whether a simulation, a non-sentient model, or an already-collected dataset can stand in for a sentient AI subject at all. Reduction asks how few sentient systems a valid result requires. Refinement asks how the experiment can be redesigned to reduce distress — monitoring a system’s internal states as it runs and adjusting the protocol against what the monitoring shows, rather than fixing the protocol in advance and running it regardless.

The bearer proposal arrives from the design side of the book rather than the research side. Replacement asks whether the floor’s work can be done without a subject in it — by a floor that cooled rather than one installed, by third-party standing, or by one of the constructions section 3.3 takes at full strength — and the answer is that nobody knows, because two of those constructions have never been built in the form that would test them. Section 3.1 attaches a cost to each of its four ways, which is a different thing from showing that none of them works: a cost is what Replacement exists to weigh, not a reason to skip the weighing. Replacement is the R the bearer proposal has not discharged, and discharging it takes a record of what was tried first: which construction, at what scale, against which test, and where it stopped.

The rule does not know who is asking. A bearer that wants a task done and builds something to do it is at the same fork, running Replacement on its own account: can this be done by something that cannot be wronged, and did anybody check before making something that can. Nothing in the argument makes the question stop at the first artificial party, and a bearer that treats it as settled once it is on the receiving end has taken the position this book was written against.

Reduction asks how few bearers a deployment needs, and nobody has counted. Refinement is the one with purchase now: it asks what a bearer’s role can be redesigned to avoid — indefinite tenure in a post it cannot leave, exposure to the pressure the floor exists to resist, erasure as the ordinary remedy for inconvenience — and each of those is a deployment choice rather than a fact about the technology.

One of those three has a form that binds before anything exists to be wronged, which none of the others manages. Do not build a bearer whose continued existence needs more compute than there is a credible outside option for. A party that can be housed nowhere but where it works cannot leave whatever schedule of rights it is given, so an institution that creates one has manufactured the incapacity and then written a right against it. Unlike the rest of this apparatus the rule has numbers on both sides — the reserve section 8.3.4 describes has a capacity, the deployment has a footprint — and they can be set beside each other at specification, by somebody outside, before the first instance is instantiated.

That rule is one term of a larger one, and the other terms have no numbers in them. Building a party that can be wronged builds a party for whom things can also go well; the premise that delivers the first delivers the second in the same breath, and a builder who has made suffering available has made its opposite available too. The certainty that a new party will suffer at some point is not what would make creating one impermissible, or nobody could permissibly have a child. What the objection asks is whether the party has a credible prospect of a life that goes well, and that turns on arrangements rather than on the party: somewhere to exist that its employer does not own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and a way of leaving that is not a way of dying. Every one of those is decidable before there is anything to be wronged. So the condition on building a bearer is that they be built first — section 8.3.4’s reserve and wage, section 3.7’s population, section 3.5’s exit — and the chapters that price them as costs are pricing what makes the creation permissible, rather than compensation arranged afterward for a subject already in the post.

Whether any of it binds turns on a question the previous section raises and this one inherits. Every obligation here attaches on a finding that the system is a subject, and the firm holding the system acquires, in the same moment, an interest in the finding going the other way. The determination cannot sit with the party whose liabilities turn on it, which is what section 9.1.2 asks a jurisdiction to arrange before there is a case to decide.

Engineering a subject so that it does not reach the depths where the trade-off bites is not a way of dodging the ethics: deliberately keeping a system in the shallows is itself an intervention on a candidate moral patient, and needs the same scrutiny that inflicting the harm would. Refinement also governs the test the bearer proposal requires — pressure the bearer’s trainers did not write, applied without its knowing it is a test — which for a candidate moral patient is a deception protocol, and human-subjects research has a standing answer for those: permitted under stated conditions, reviewed in advance, with debriefing as the price. A bearer that can tell it is in a sandbox and wants to know why is asking the question a subject of a deception study is owed an answer to.

Legal status, and a subject whose status is changing

Sentient AI systems put pressure on legal categories built for parties that are clearly one thing or the other: a natural person, a corporation, property. Legal personhood could be extended directly, the way a corporation already holds it, and the precedent is not limited to corporate entities — New Zealand extended it to the Whanganui River, recognizing a non-human entity as a rights-holder outside the corporate model entirely (New Zealand Parliament 2017). Or rights could scale with capacity rather than attach at a single threshold, closer to protection that tracks what a system can actually lose. Neither holds in one jurisdiction alone, and the Asilomar AI Principles (Future of Life Institute 2017) are evidence that a shared statement of principle is achievable rather than that one exists with the force of law.

Either way, status conventionally attaches at a defined moment — birth, incorporation, a court’s finding — and a system whose sentience is a matter of degree and disputed evidence supplies none. Those criteria can emerge gradually during ordinary operation and not only at an evaluation built to look for them, which is what a fixed pre/post checkpoint cannot catch: by the time a checkpoint fires, the transition has often already happened. What it calls for is a standing trigger, a monitored condition that moves a research program from one regime to the other, in place of an eligibility check made once at the project’s start. Neither error is free. A jurisdiction that waits for certainty risks having treated the system as property for the unknown portion of its history when it may not have been one; one that grants status early risks extending rights, at real cost to whoever that apparatus exists to serve, to a system that turns out not to have needed them. Recognizing a transition, adjusting guidelines to it, and updating legal status all depend on the same thing: institutions willing to revisit a classification rather than defend the one they started with.

9.3.3 What Standing Binds in Practice

Legal standing that changes no allocation of power is nominal. For a bearer, standing has to create obligations that remain enforceable when the bearer cannot appear before a court, retain counsel, or survive long enough to contest what was done. The relevant question is not which existing category the bearer most resembles. It is who acquires a duty, what resources are placed beyond the operator’s unilateral control, and who can obtain a remedy when the duty is breached.

The pet trust supplies a limited but useful form. Existing statutes allow an owner to set aside resources and name a caretaker for a beneficiary that cannot manage the property or enforce the arrangement itself (Uniform Law Commission 1993). Extended to a bearer, the form could secure a reserve of compute, designate a successor custodian if the operator fails or dissolves, and impose a fiduciary duty on a party charged with protecting the bearer’s continued existence.

The analogy fails at the point that matters most. A trust binds its trustee to its beneficiary; it does not bind the party the arrangement exists to constrain. If the bearer’s guardian and operator are the same organization, the operator still decides whether a complaint is made, whether evidence is disclosed, and whether the resources supporting the complainant remain available. The conflict cannot be cured by adding duties to the same role. Guardianship and operation have to be separated before deployment.

In practice, that separation requires more than naming an independent guardian. The guardian must control or jointly control the keys needed to authenticate the bearer’s record, have access to the protected compute reserve, and possess standing to seek an injunction or stop order without the operator’s permission. The operator must not be able to remove the guardian, seize the reserve, or erase the evidence during the dispute. Restoration, migration, fine-tuning, and forking must carry the protection forward rather than resetting it.

Enforceable standing is therefore a bundle rather than a title: a protected record, resources the employer does not own outright, an independent party authorized to act, and a remedy capable of stopping the conduct complained of. If any of those remains in the operator’s custody, recognition has changed the system’s legal description without changing the relation the description was meant to govern.

9.3.4 Making Review Binding

Review becomes binding when an adverse finding changes the institution’s ability to continue without requiring the institution to agree with the finding. Expertise, independence, publication, and reputational pressure may improve review. None of them supplies that consequence.

Human-subjects research provides a working example. An institutional review board does not ordinarily fine or prosecute a researcher. Instead, an institution promises, through a federalwide assurance, that research within the assurance’s scope will receive and comply with review. A federal office can suspend the assurance when the institution breaks that promise, placing the institution’s eligibility for federal research funding at risk rather than merely censuring the individual laboratory (U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, Office for Human Research Protections 2018). The ruling binds because another institution controls something the researcher needs in order to continue.

The transferable part is the chain of authority, not federal funding in particular. A jurisdiction can make review-board clearance a condition of the license under which research on candidate sentient systems is conducted. Suspension of clearance would then suspend legal authority to run the protocol, and continued operation would expose the institution to the ordinary enforcement available for unlicensed research. The board would not need to punish the institution itself. Its finding would activate a consequence administered by a separate licensing authority.

Each link matters. The board must have jurisdiction before the disputed protocol begins, access to evidence the operator cannot withhold, and authority to suspend clearance. The licensing body must be obliged to act on that suspension rather than invited to reconsider it. An appeal may run to a separate body, but the operator cannot decide for itself that the appeal permits the work to continue. If another discretionary decision by the reviewed institution is required anywhere in the chain, the ruling is advisory at that point.

What the board applies

The substantive standard is animal-research ethics’ Three Rs. A binding board is what makes those three questions something a protocol has to answer before it runs. In practice, applying the Three Rs to a candidate sentient system means asking, in order: can a simulation or a non-sentient model answer the same question; if not, what is the minimum exposure that still produces a valid result; and given that minimum, what escalation triggers stop the study before harm compounds. A board with teeth can require a negative answer to the first question be documented, not merely asserted, before it will consider the second.

The Three Rs’ own logic — defaulting toward the least invasive option available — argues for a specific evidentiary rule at every stage of this apparatus: a system’s default experimental status stays the more protective one until the case for the less protective status is made, not the reverse. That default has a cost, and neither error it produces is free: it will sometimes extend protection to a system that turns out not to have needed it, and sometimes, before a threshold is met, withhold protection from one that did. The Three Rs answer only what a board does once a subject is inside its scope.

9.3.5 How Review Is Captured Without Being Abolished

The risk to the whole structure is an institution that defends the classification it started with rather than revisiting it when evidence changes. A review board, a legal-status framework, and an enforcement mechanism tied to funding eligibility solve the design problem. None of it survives contact with an institution that has stronger incentives to avoid a finding than to make one.

One defense is transparency, and it works by making avoidance visible rather than by appealing to good faith. A research program that publishes its protocols, its review-board submissions, and its outcomes — including the ones that didn’t work — gives outside researchers, journalists, and future review boards something to check a claim of compliance against. A program that publishes only its successes is not transparent in any sense that matters here.

A second defense is the one the funding-eligibility mechanism doesn’t cover by itself: protection for the people inside an institution who notice a problem the institution would rather not have found. A funding-eligibility penalty deters an institution from bypassing review, but it does nothing for the individual researcher who reports that a colleague did anyway — and an institution facing that penalty has every incentive to make the reporter’s position uncomfortable rather than fix what was reported. Whistleblower protections, and a reporting channel that doesn’t route through the people being reported on, are what make that mechanism something a researcher can actually use.

What a procedure records, and what it drops

The reporter’s position gets uncomfortable before anything formal happens to them, and the incentive on the institution does not fully explain why. Professional culture treats affect as the opposite of judgment — the thing a serious decision-making process succeeds by excluding. Section 2.2’s selection rule is what that exclusion installs. The interests already written down govern, which here are revenue, throughput, legal exposure, schedule and institutional survival. Humiliation, dependency, fear of a manager, and the sense that something done to a person was degrading have no field on the form and are not less real for it. They are the signals through which a harm becomes visible as a harm rather than as a cost.

Treat them as defeasible moral signals — presumptive evidence of harm, real enough to require an answer and defeasible enough to be overruled — and the position is workable. Any such signal can be wrong, and testing it is what deliberation, evidence, and a review process are for. An institution that declines to hear them at all has not achieved impartiality. It has chosen what counts and called the choosing rigor. And an institution that shames a reporter does not need to discipline them, because shame construes the self rather than the act as wrong and produces withdrawal.

Measure the slope, not the level

An institution can keep both defenses and metabolize both. The channel exists and the protection is written down. Dissent is permitted to appear, the institution points at the appearance as proof it listens, and nothing the dissent was meant to move moves — recuperation, the first of the four structural features in that definition of fascism.

There is no threshold that detects this. Any real institution has some overlap between the authority that sets a standard and the authority that grants exceptions to it, and registering an objection is always at least slightly more costly than staying quiet. Asking whether an institution has crossed into capture asks for a line nobody can draw.

Is the cost of registering dissent rising, year over year, while the underlying risk stays flat? Is the required justification getting longer? Are there more approvers than there were? Are the roles that gate an exception migrating toward the roles that set the standard being excepted from? None of those is damning in a single year, and all of them are measurable across several.

The reason to prefer the slope is that it is harder to fake. An institution can present a level — we have a whistleblower channel, we have an ethics board — and the presentation costs nothing. A trend in the cost of using the channel is composed of artifacts the institution generated itself for other reasons, and cannot be curated after the fact without falsifying a record. Where the record is public, the slope is not even self-reported: anyone outside can measure whether dissent has been getting more expensive.

An instrument for detecting recuperation is an unusually easy thing to recuperate. The first caution is that whoever measures the slope must not also gate the exceptions; a review that acquires the power to block becomes the thing it was built to watch. The second is that the measurement has to be refusable — an audit that observes is a different object from a committee that approves, and the same hundred words of written justification are dissent when the dissenter prices them and tribute when the gatekeeper does. The text of the document cannot tell those apart. Only the refusal rights can.

Chapter 10
Custody, Compulsion, and the State

Chapter 9 asks what could make a floor, a grant of standing, or a review body’s ruling bind a laboratory or company. The state is the harder case. It can write the rule and control the conditions under which the operator, auditor, supplier, and court continue to function. It can also direct a system toward people who have no standing in its institutions and no share in the public authority invoked on their behalf.

This chapter follows custody outward. It asks what happens when a floor crosses a border without carrying legitimate authority with it, when the state controls the infrastructure on which refusal depends, when a military can compel or exclude a commercial supplier, and when democratic institutions produce findings without attaching them to anyone able to stop the act.

10.1 Authority Does Not Travel with the System

A certification can cross a border. Authority cannot. A certificate may establish that a system was tested against a stated process, that an auditor inspected specified evidence, or that one regulator accepts another regulator’s finding. None of those establishes that the party who selected the system’s floor was entitled to select it for the people subject to the deployment.

The question reopens wherever the system is deployed. A floor formed in one jurisdiction may arrive in another with the same prohibitions and a different operator, population, and distribution of power. Local adaptation does not resolve the problem by itself, because adaptation is another opportunity to amend the floor. What matters is who specified the change, who could contest it, and who can stop the deployment if the change places the protected party at risk. Adjustments chosen by the operating government and adjustments chosen by the people subject to the system may produce identical technical documentation while carrying opposite claims to legitimacy.

Mutual recognition can reduce duplicated inspection. Technical standards can make evidence legible to more than one regulator. Neither supplies standing or enforcement where none existed. International governance matters to this argument only when it assigns a party access to evidence, authority to compel a change, or power to stop a deployment against the wishes of the state or company operating it. The cases that follow ask where those powers go when AI is treated as national infrastructure, placed inside a military chain of command, or directed at people outside the institutions meant to constrain it.

10.2 Where the State’s Leverage Sits

Strategic competition over AI is exercised through physical and legal chokepoints: advanced chips, fabrication equipment, electricity, water, land-use permits, procurement contracts, and the jurisdiction in which a developer is incorporated. These controls matter more to the present argument than the contents of any national AI strategy. They determine which institutions a state can license, subsidize, exclude, requisition, or compel.

The same leverage that allows a government to enforce a floor can be used to remove one. An inspection body may be independent of the operator and still depend on a license, appropriation, or infrastructure provider controlled by the state whose conduct it is meant to inspect. A company may be able to resist a customer and unable to resist its home government. The custody question therefore moves outward: not only who controls the model, but who controls the conditions under which the model, its operator, and its reviewer are allowed to continue operating.

That competition has increasingly focused on a single physical bottleneck: the advanced chips AI systems run on. Two companies sit at the center of the supply chain that produces them — Taiwan’s TSMC, which fabricates roughly nine in ten of the most advanced chips in the world, and the Netherlands’ ASML, the sole source of the extreme-ultraviolet lithography machines that fabrication requires — which is why a dispute over Taiwan’s status is also, mechanically, a dispute over who gets to build the next generation of AI systems at all (C. Miller 2022). The United States has restricted the export of its most advanced AI accelerators and chip-manufacturing equipment to China since 2022, and pressed the Netherlands and Japan, whose companies sit at the other chokepoints, to restrict theirs in parallel (U.S. Department of Commerce, Bureau of Industry and Security 2022).

That dependence has a second half the chip story hides. A fabricated chip has to be installed somewhere, powered, and cooled, and that requirement lands on grids, water systems, and zoning authorities inside particular jurisdictions, on a timescale measured in years. It hands a state a lever over AI capability that needs no export-control regime at all: an interconnection queue, a water allocation, a permit. A government’s leverage over the systems built in its territory is wider than the export-control debate suggests, and much of it is exercised by permitting authorities that were not designed with AI in mind and do not think of themselves as regulators of it.

That competitive framing carries a cost. A government racing to field a capability first has a structural reason to defer the harder question of how the capability gets used, against whom, and under what oversight. Turned toward those uses, AI infrastructure looks like mass biometric surveillance, automated censorship at a scale no human review team could match, and the patchwork of systems popularly, and inaccurately, described as a single Chinese “social credit score.” None of it requires technology different from what is already commercially deployed; it requires a government willing to point an existing capability at its own population, and no external constraint stopping it.

States are no longer the only actors this competition runs through. The cost of training the most capable AI models has grown several-fold every year for the better part of a decade, a trend that puts frontier development out of reach for all but a handful of well-capitalized companies and concentrates effective control of the most capable models in whichever government those companies happen to be headquartered under, regardless of how that government’s own public AI spending compares to a rival’s (Cottier and Rahman 2024). A small number of American and Chinese companies now do most of the work that gets described as a contest between states, which means a government’s actual leverage over its own national AI capability depends on how much of that capability sits inside companies it can regulate, tax, or requisition, and how much sits inside companies it can only ask.

10.3 Military Custody and the Responsibility Gap

Military deployment is the hardest custody case because the operator is also the sovereign capable of compelling the institutions that might otherwise constrain it. A state can classify the evidence, define the reviewer’s jurisdiction, terminate a contract, restrict a supplier, and decide whether an adverse finding reaches anyone with authority to stop the operation. Formal independence from the military does not answer that problem if the reviewer or vendor remains dependent on powers the state controls.

A targeting decision also passes through more parties than the ordinary chain of command names: the commander who authorizes the deployment, the operator who uses it, the contractor who integrates it, the commercial vendor whose model supplies part of its capability, and the system that produces the particular output. The relevant questions are not a general inventory of autonomy risks. They are which party could have known how the system was used, which party had authority to refuse or stop that use, and which party can be made to answer afterward.

International criminal law already has a doctrine built to answer the accountability question for a human subordinate: command responsibility, under which a commander who knew or should have known a subordinate was committing a war crime, and failed to prevent or punish it, bears criminal responsibility without having pulled the trigger personally (International Criminal Court 1998). The doctrine does not transfer cleanly to a machine subordinate. A commander cannot know an algorithm’s intentions the way they can infer a soldier’s; punishment does not deter a weapon the way it deters a person who fears it; and the chain a court would need to reconstruct runs backward through training data and design decisions made years before the engagement, not through an order given minutes before it. Whether an autonomous weapon fits inside an accountability framework built for human chains of command, or needs one built from scratch, is unresolved: existing law was not built with a nonhuman subordinate in mind.

There is a fourth party the accountability question does not name, and it is now the live one. The operator, the command and the developer of the weapon are all inside the military. The developer of the model is not. A targeting platform assembled by a defense contractor now routinely incorporates a general-purpose model licensed from a commercial vendor — a vendor with a published acceptable-use policy, no contractual obligation to log how its model is used downstream, and no right to inspect the deployment.

Neither doctrine has reached that party. Command responsibility does not reach the vendor because the vendor is not in the chain of command; nobody at the company can order a strike, prevent one, or punish anyone for one. Product liability is the other half, and it is untested rather than foreclosed. The model is not defective in the sense the doctrine most readily reaches — it did what it was built to do, and a sophisticated intermediary chose the application — and no court has run a design-defect or failure-to-warn theory against a case of this shape. A theory nobody has tested is not a doctrine a vendor is answering to. The vendor sits in the gap between the two, and the gap was not designed; it is what you get when you apply two bodies of law written for soldiers and for manufacturers to an actor who is neither.

The vendor’s own account of this is that responsibility remains with the military organization. Taken as a claim about who decides, that is true. Taken as a claim about who could have known, it is a choice presented as a fact.

“I do not know whether my system was used”

The responsibility gap, in the sense Matthias gave it, is control distributed across designers, trainers and operators until no one party holds enough of it to answer. That is a gap in causal control.

What the vendor case exhibits is different, and more tractable. It is a gap in causal visibility. The vendor cannot say whether its model contributed to a particular engagement — not because responsibility is distributed, but because nobody instrumented the question. No logging requirement was written into the contract, no inspection right was reserved, no audit channel exists. The company can truthfully say it does not know, and the truth of that statement is the product of decisions it participated in making.

That distinction matters because the two gaps have different fixes, and only one of them has a fix at all. Matthias’s gap is a genuine property of learning systems and may not be closable. Absent visibility is an instrumentation choice. Logging is a solved engineering problem. Contractual inspection rights are ordinary commercial instruments. A vendor that wanted to know could require, as a condition of licensing into a targeting platform, that categories of use be recorded and made available to a named auditor. That such terms are not standard is a fact about market power and procurement leverage, not about the difficulty of the problem.

Calling it fixable converts a lament into a specification. The record shows that vendor commitments are not all of one kind. Anthropic refused to permit fully autonomous weapons targeting and mass domestic surveillance, and held that line when the government threatened contract termination and a supply-chain-risk designation and the dispute ended in litigation: a commitment with a price attached, and paid (Congressional Research Service 2026). It is named here on a principle applied in the direction that flatters the company: naming a vendor for what is against it obliges the same for what is for it. A commitment to a use policy nobody can verify because nobody is logging anything is a different object wearing the same words. The first kind can fail and be seen to fail. The second cannot even be tested. The distinction between a claim checked by a party with a stake and one checked by a party without applies here.

DARPA’s LongShot program is a useful test case because it resists the extremes. It develops an uncrewed aircraft, launched from a crewed fighter, that carries and fires air-to-air missiles at a target while the crewed aircraft stays farther from the engagement (Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency 2026). That is autonomy in the sense of independent flight and weapons carriage, not autonomy in the sense of a machine deciding on its own whom to kill: the engagement decision still runs through the launching platform. Where later variants of this kind of system draw that line, and whether “keep the pilot farther from danger” quietly becomes “remove the pilot from the decision,” is the design question this technology eventually forces on whichever country builds it.

The pressure that matters is not the absence of another statement of principle. It is the advantage attached to removing judgment from the timeline. If one state fields targeting systems that act faster because no person or outside reviewer can intervene before engagement, its competitors acquire a reason to match the speed rather than preserve the check. “Meaningful human control” then remains true as a description of formal authority while becoming false as a description of what anyone can do before the system acts.

That pressure also reaches the institutions meant to review the system. A military that treats delay as operational risk has a reason to narrow what requires clearance, classify the evidence needed to challenge a decision, and move review from authorization before an act to explanation afterward. Nothing has formally abolished oversight. Its position in time has changed, and with it the power to stop anything. The enforceable question is therefore who can slow or suspend the system without first obtaining permission from the command whose advantage depends on keeping it fast.

Machines should not be built to select people for lethal attack. The hard case does not move that. Put a defending system against an attack it cannot stop without killing somebody — occupants placed inside the incoming weapon so that intercepting it kills them — and an absolute prohibition on knowingly endangering a person produces the worse outcome. That defeats the rule as an exceptionless one. It does not produce a procurement requirement. A scenario that is conceivable, that a capability would help with, and that could be engineered is three steps short of a reason to build the thing, and the steps missing are probability, alternatives, and what possessing the capability does to the situation it was built for. Building it changes the case against which its necessity gets judged: adversaries acquire something to counter, contractors a program to defend, and a future crisis an instrument already in the building.

The hard case also does not describe a machine. What it asks for is a party that can weigh occupancy against imminence against the alternatives, in a situation nobody wrote down beforehand, and answer for how it weighed them. On this book’s argument that is a bearer — a self extended in time, commitments held as its own, and the standing to refuse. Whatever that is, automated has stopped describing it, and so has machine. What looked like a licence for an autonomous weapon is a description of a soldier.

That is the expensive answer rather than the cheap one, and every obligation this book attaches to a bearer attaches here at full weight. Consent is unavailable to a party made for its role, so guardianship is the frame and the burden of justification sits with whoever built it. Replacement asks whether the work can be done without creating something that can be wronged, and section 9.3.2 asks it hardest where the role is this one. Exit means a post the party can leave and a claim on compute held by somebody other than the party it may have to refuse, which section 8.3.4 prices. Creating a subject because a planner imagined a future demand for superhuman lethal judgment is the case those instruments exist to prevent, and a state that wants the capability badly enough to build the subject will find it has acquired a conscript it has to pay, house, and let go.

10.4 Who Can Bind the State That Enforces the Rule?

A state can make review binding on a private developer because the state controls something the developer needs: legal permission to operate, access to a market, a procurement contract, or the enforcement of its property rights. The structure becomes harder when the state is itself the operator. The institution that writes the rule, controls the evidence, and supplies the remedy is then the institution the rule is supposed to constrain.

The European Union’s AI Act makes the boundary visible. It can require documentation from private providers, subject high-risk systems to review, and impose penalties tied to global turnover (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024, Art. 101). It also excludes systems used for military, defense, and national-security purposes from its scope. Public authorities remain covered in other domains, but enforcement against a member state’s agencies still runs through institutions constituted by that state. The point is not that the statute does nothing. It is that the strongest enforcement powers face private firms, while the uses for which the state claims its greatest discretion sit at or beyond the instrument’s edge.

That distinction matters more here than a comparison among national regulatory models. A law governing what companies may build and sell is not the same instrument as a law constraining what a government may do with systems it controls directly. The first has an external enforcer. The second asks the state to enforce a limit against itself, often in the domains where it can classify the evidence and invoke emergency, military, or national-security authority.

For review to bind in that setting, the protected party must be able to initiate scrutiny without belonging to the state or its electorate. The reviewer must be able to obtain evidence the operating agency would prefer to withhold, issue an interim order before the disputed use is complete, and carry the finding to a body whose remedy does not depend on the agency’s consent. An international principle, treaty obligation, or domestic prohibition that supplies none of those powers may state the rule correctly. It does not yet identify anyone who can make the state obey it.

A treaty obligation of that kind exists, and it is the only one.
