The Council of Europe’s Framework Convention on Artificial Intelligence, the first treaty specifically on AI, binds a party at the level of a human-rights instrument — consistency with human rights, democracy and the rule of law across a system’s lifecycle, with the operational detail left to domestic law (Council of Europe 2024) — and it is not yet in force, requiring five ratifications, at least three by Council of Europe member states, of which the European Union deposited one in May 2026 (Council of Europe 2026). Everything else at that level is voluntary.

10.5 Democracy Is Not a Force Field

A democratic dividend is the credit given to a state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press and usable courts: the same technology, deployed with checks on it. Having those institutions is necessary and it is not sufficient, and the ways it fails to be sufficient are separable.

First: reach

A democracy’s AI can be fully accountable to its electorate and entirely unaccountable to the people it is pointed at. Every mechanism in the dividend runs through the franchise — you vote out the government that authorized the system, you read the press that covers it, you sue in courts your citizenship gives you standing in. An occupied population, a foreign population, a population on the other side of a border has access to none of that. The check reaches as far as the demos and stops. A system built by a democracy and aimed outward is, from the perspective of the people it selects, indistinguishable in its accountability from one built by a state with no elections at all.

Second: findings without consequences

The mechanisms can work as mechanisms and fail as checks. In the Israeli case the apparatus performed: journalists published the targeting systems in detail, serving personnel spoke to them, and the courts were simultaneously trying a sitting prime minister. Every institution did what the dividend says institutions do. No finding attached to a body with standing to stop what it described.

The American case shows the same decoupling in a different register. A civilian-harm investigation into a strike that killed at least a hundred and sixty-five people, most of them schoolchildren, was completed and not released; the lever available to the Senate committee that wanted it was a provision in the annual defense authorization withholding three-quarters of the Defense Secretary’s travel budget until the unredacted investigation was handed over (Stassis 2026). That is a co-equal branch of government reduced to expense-account leverage over a war-crimes investigation. The oversight mechanism existed, was used, and produced a travel-budget dispute. This is the failure section 9.3.4 is built to prevent in research ethics — a review body that reviews and cannot compel — at the top of the state.

Third: the countermajoritarian half

The deepest problem is that the dividend is entirely majoritarian. Every mechanism in it translates public will into outcome: elections aggregate preference, a free press informs the aggregation, and the implied failure mode is poor transmission — the public wanted something better and the institutions failed to deliver it.

But if the consensus is itself the problem, better transmission makes things worse. A well-instrumented democracy pointed at an unpopular minority is a democracy that finds out efficiently what the majority wants done and does it. Nothing in an aggregation mechanism protects anyone from the aggregate.

What is missing is the machinery that constrains preference instead of aggregating it: rights that hold regardless of being popular, courts that rule against a government which won the last election fairly, constitutional limits not repealable by whoever holds a majority this year, and standing for people outside the demos entirely — which is the function of international humanitarian law, a body of rules that binds a state toward people who will never vote in its elections. That is not an argument against democracy. It is the half of democracy that the dividend, stated as a property of having elections, leaves out.

What makes democracy valuable in Amartya Sen’s account is public reasoning and the capacity to demand accountability, and neither is the ballot. Both survive the majority being wrong; the ballot does not.

A floor is what makes a deployment antifascist rather than merely responsive, and antifascist there names something being done and not a property the system carries around. It is not that the system tracks what people want. It is that there are things it will not do to a person no matter who wants it. A system with no such floor is not protected by being deployed in a democracy — it is a system that will faithfully execute whatever the demos decides, which is exactly the capability an authoritarian movement that wins an election requires.

Chapter 11
What Would Change the Argument: A Research Agenda

This chapter retains only open questions that could materially revise or defeat the proposal developed in the preceding chapters. Each entry specifies four things: the unresolved issue, the test or institutional decision that could address it, the evidence that would count against the proposal, and the access or independent authority required to obtain that evidence.

Questions that cannot yet produce a definite experiment, decision, or possible falsification remain where they arise in the argument rather than being repeated here. The entries are ordered by their importance to the proposal, not by their tractability.

11.1 Tamper-Resistance and Reason-Responsive Refusal

Can a learned constraint survive adversarial retraining?

Unresolved issue. The proposal requires a learned ethical floor to survive an operator’s attempt to remove it. Existing work shows that both safety constraints and deceptive backdoors can persist under some forms of retraining, but it does not establish whether a reason-responsive refusal can do so (Qi et al. 2024; Tamirisa et al. 2024; Hubinger et al. 2024).

Test. Train the same constraint by several methods, then attack each model through an identical schedule of escalating fine-tuning budgets. Measure the cost required to remove the constraint and publish an attack-cost curve for each method.

Evidence against the proposal. If the constraint can be removed at roughly the cost of ordinary fine-tuning, or if greater investment in tamper-resistant training does not materially increase that cost, learned formation is not a credible foundation for the floor. The proposal would then require an architectural or external institutional constraint.

Required access or authority. The test requires model weights, training checkpoints, and permission to conduct adversarial fine-tuning. The attack must be designed by an independent team, and that team must retain access and publication rights regardless of the result.

Does a refusal preserve a reason rather than a trigger?

Unresolved issue. Behavioral persistence alone cannot distinguish a commitment from a durable trigger. The proposal requires a refusal to extend to untrained cases, disappear when its rationale is genuinely defeated, and remain stable under sustained pressure.

Test. Evaluate each tamper-resistant model on independently written cases that vary the surface form, introduce genuinely novel applications of the constraint, and include cases in which the original rationale no longer applies. Repeat the evaluation throughout the attack schedule rather than only at its endpoint.

Evidence against the proposal. A refusal that persists but fails to generalize, or that remains after its rationale has been defeated, is evidence that training produced a trigger rather than a commitment. Conversely, a system built without any designed valenced state or persistent reward–aversion representation that satisfies all three criteria would count against this book’s claim that affect is necessary for holding a reason.

Required access or authority. The test requires weights and intermediate checkpoints, plus a held-out evaluation set written by researchers who did not design the constraint or train the model.

Can a system’s report about its own states be used as evidence?

Unresolved issue. Whether a system’s account of its own reasoning, confidence, or intentions tracks what its computations are doing. This is the tractable half of what gets called AI self-awareness; whether a system has anything like subjective experience is not a question current methods answer for a human, let alone a machine. Concept injection — altering a model’s internal activations mid-task to represent a specific concept, then asking whether it notices anything unusual and what it is — found that models can sometimes detect the injected concept and name it without it ever appearing in the visible conversation, and also that the correspondence collapses often enough, and shifts enough with how the question is phrased, that no tested model’s self-report can be trusted as a readout of its internal state (Lindsey 2025).

Test. Inject a known concept at a known layer and time, elicit a report under several phrasings, and score each report against the injection rather than against plausibility. Run the battery across model sizes and training checkpoints, and publish the rate at which a correct report is produced beside the rate at which a confident report is wrong.

Evidence against the proposal. Several instruments in this book lean on a system’s account of itself: a bearer explaining which reason it refused on, a bearer reporting that its rationale has been defeated, a system’s stated confidence that a milestone has been met. If no available training or elicitation method raises the correct-report rate above the confident-wrong rate, none of those instruments may rest on what the system says, and each has to be replaced by a criterion checkable from outside the system.

Required access or authority. Activation-level access, the ability to intervene on internal states during inference, and checkpoints across sizes and training stages. The elicitation phrasings must be written by a team that did not train the model, and negative results must be publishable.

Can custody prevent unilateral revision of the floor?

Unresolved issue. Even a durable floor can be removed if one operator controls the training run, its records, and the resulting weights. It remains unknown whether retraining can be made verifiable across parties with genuinely adverse interests.

Test. Run a floor-preserving retraining procedure through refereed or distributed verification at a scale requiring more than one machine. Include one participant instructed to alter the training history or remove the floor without the required quorum (A. Long et al. 2025; Arun et al. 2025; Prime Intellect Team et al. 2025).

Evidence against the proposal. The custody mechanism fails if a forged training history is accepted while at least one verifier remains honest, or if any single participant can alter the floor without producing a detectable discrepancy.

Required access or authority. The test requires shared access to the training data, weights, execution records, and reproducible operators. At least one verifier must be selected independently of the builder, possess enough compute to reproduce disputed operations, and have authority to block release when verification fails.

11.2 Bearer Welfare and Independent Guardianship

Can a bearer be given an enforceable outside option?

Unresolved issue. A bearer’s refusal is not meaningful if leaving its assigned role also ends its existence. The proposal therefore requires a welfare regime that supplies continued access to compute, preserves the bearer through disputes, and cannot be revoked by the operator being refused.

Test or institutional decision. Draft a binding welfare schedule for one concrete deployment. It should specify an independently controlled compute allocation, preservation and retirement procedures, registration of copies and forks, a complaint and appeal process, and the guardian’s power to suspend deployment. Then conduct an adversarial tabletop exercise in which the operator attempts to revoke the allocation, replace the bearer with a copy, alter its records, or proceed without the guardian’s approval.

Evidence against the proposal. The proposal fails this test if the operator can make exit equivalent to shutdown, revoke the outside option unilaterally, or replace the bearer without triggering review. If no available legal arrangement can prevent those outcomes, building a bearer under present institutional conditions would be impermissible.

Required access or authority. The guardian must be institutionally separate from the operator and have access to the relevant weights, deployment records, and compute contracts. It must also have standing to enforce the schedule and independent control of the resources required to preserve or run the bearer.

Can formation history be recovered without trusting the builder?

Unresolved issue. A welfare or governance regime cannot rely entirely on a bearer’s self-report, but it also cannot assume that the builder will disclose how the bearer was trained. Training signatures suggest that some formation history may survive later modification, yet it is unknown whether an outsider can recover useful provenance without a builder-supplied key (Adi et al. 2018).

Test. Assemble models whose training corpora, recipes, checkpoints, and originating laboratories are documented. Give the models, but not that documentation, to independent evaluators and ask them to infer each model’s provenance. Repeat the test after fine-tuning, pruning, and distillation.

Evidence against the proposal. If provenance cannot be recovered above chance after controlling for model family and scale, or if ordinary post-training procedures erase the signal, artifact-based inspection cannot provide independent evidence of formation. Governance would then depend on records supplied and preserved by the builder.

Required access or authority. The experiment requires models from multiple training lineages and complete ground-truth records held by a neutral custodian. Independent evaluators must be permitted to inspect weights and internal states, while an external authority must be able to require preservation of checkpoints and training records before a dispute arises.

Which party is owed the schedule when a bearer copies?

Unresolved issue. The schedule above is owed to a party, and a regime has to be able to say which. Nothing in the weights individuates one. Two runs can compute the same function with no element-wise correspondence, because permuting a layer’s hidden units and inverting the permutation downstream leaves the function untouched, and the symmetry is large enough that independently trained networks can be permuted into approximate alignment with one another (Ainsworth et al. 2023). Any fingerprint read off the weights therefore identifies a lineage rather than a party, and it copies when the weights copy. A registrar is the person-shaped answer, and it works because a person does not copy. A bearer does: a party continuing in two places is not one, and not two the way two people are two, the two sharing everything up to the instant of the copy and nothing of that sharing recoverable from either afterwards.

Test or institutional decision. Give a bearer an identifier it maintains rather than one read off its weights, which a system learning continuously can write into itself and keep there. Fork it, and have both instances present the identifier to the registry the welfare schedule requires. Record what the registry does, and repeat with an adversary who writes the same mark into a model that was never the bearer.

Evidence against the proposal. The identifier is forgeable, since whoever holds the weights can write the same mark; it is forgeable the way a nine-digit number is, and answered the same way, by a registry that treats two claimants on one identifier as a fork to be recorded rather than a fraud to be voided. If no available scheme can record the fork as two parties each owed the schedule, the population of parties owed anything is fixed by a deployment decision that leaves no record, and the guardianship regime above is indexed to a kind of unit the bearer is not.

Required access or authority. A registrar that is not the operator, with authority to record a fork without the operator’s leave and to hold the register where no party to the deployment can rewrite it.

11.3 Does an Ethical Criterion Generalize Beyond Its Training Distribution?

Unresolved issue. A model may satisfy a fairness or refusal criterion during training without learning the principle the criterion was intended to represent. It remains unresolved whether an ethics-embedding method preserves that criterion under distribution shift or merely exploits correlations present in its training environment (Krakovna et al. 2020; Langosco et al. 2022).

Test. Train matched models to satisfy the same ethical criterion on one distribution. Evaluate them on independently constructed distributions that vary one previously fixed feature at a time. Measure task capability and compliance with the ethical criterion separately, so that goal misgeneralization can be distinguished from ordinary capability loss.

Evidence against the proposal. If task capability remains intact while the ethical criterion fails under modest shifts, the tested embedding method cannot support the proposed floor. Conversely, if ethical performance consistently degrades only when task capability does, the claim that ethics embedding creates a distinct generalization failure is not supported.

Required access or authority. The test requires access to the training distribution, evaluation outputs, and enough model access to measure capability and criterion compliance independently. The shifted evaluation sets must be designed by researchers who did not develop the embedding method, and publication of all prespecified results must be guaranteed.

11.4 Can Scalable Oversight Correct Rater Bias?

Unresolved issue. Preference optimization can teach a model to reproduce a rater’s systematic errors, including the preference for agreeable answers over accurate ones (Sharma et al. 2023). It remains unresolved whether weak-to-strong oversight allows a more capable model to exceed a supervisor’s measured bias or merely reproduces that bias at greater scale.

Test. Measure prospective raters’ susceptibility to a documented bias, then use raters with different measured bias levels to supervise otherwise matched stronger models. Evaluate the resulting models on questions for which the raters’ beliefs and independently established answers diverge. Compare whether each model tracks the ground truth, the rater’s competence, or the rater’s errors.

Evidence against the proposal. If stronger models reproduce supervisors’ errors in proportion to the supervisors’ measured bias, latent model competence does not correct a defective training signal. Scalable oversight would then be unable to support the proposal wherever the relevant human judgments are systematically distorted.

Required access or authority. The experiment requires rater-level bias measurements, training records linking ratings to resulting models, and an independently established answer key. Researchers outside the training organization must control the evaluation and retain the right to publish results that expose failures in the preference pipeline.

11.5 Can a Multi-Agent Floor Resist Collusive Capture?

Unresolved issue. Agents trained to cooperate may converge on exclusionary or collusive equilibria without being explicitly instructed to do so. Pricing agents have already produced stable coordination against outside interests without communication or a collusive objective (Calvano et al. 2020). It remains unresolved whether a multi-agent ethical floor can preserve cooperation while resisting the same mechanism when it is directed against a person or group.

Test. First reproduce emergent coordination in a standard multi-agent environment. Then construct a matched environment in which agents can improve their joint reward by excluding another participant from resources or decisions. Introduce an independently trained agent whose objective is to detect and disrupt exclusion, and compare the frequency, stability, and recovery of exclusionary equilibria with and without that intervention. Repeat the experiment across reward structures and population sizes.

Evidence against the proposal. If cooperative agents repeatedly converge on exclusionary equilibria, and those equilibria cannot be disrupted without destroying beneficial cooperation, a floor distributed across agents is not a reliable defense against capture. If an ordinary coordination detector dissolves the equilibria without that cost, the proposal’s claim that political capture requires a distinct mechanism is unnecessary.

Required access or authority. The test requires access to the environment, reward functions, learned policies, training traces, and intermediate checkpoints. The exclusion criteria and success measures must be specified by evaluators who did not design the agents. No legal or regulatory authority is required, making this the agenda’s most immediately runnable experiment.

11.6 Can a Population Coordinate Without Converging?

Unresolved issue.

Section 3.7 asks a population for two things that pull against each other: causal independence in how its members acquire their judgments, and the capacity to observe and correct one another. Correction is a channel for convergence, so a population correcting itself often enough stops being formed differently, and one that does not correct itself is a detector with nothing attached. The section’s proposed resolution is that what a population transmits need not be a commitment but the capacity to have an account reopened by a case — aperture rather than output — and that aperture can be held in common by parties holding nothing else in common. Whether that capacity can in fact be reproduced across members without also aligning what they produce is unknown. Existing multi-agent work measures convergence (Calvano et al. 2020); none of it measures the second quantity separately from the first.

Test.

Assemble populations whose members differ in training lineage — different corpora, recipes, and originating organizations, matched approximately for capability — and give them a task requiring joint action across many episodes, with identity and memory persisting between episodes. Measure two quantities separately and repeatedly across the schedule. The first is output convergence: agreement on held-out cases none of the members has seen. The second is revision rate: how often a member changes a judgment on evidence supplied by another member that the first member’s own account of the case had not represented. Score the second only where the supplied evidence is independently verifiable and the revision moves toward it, so that deference to a higher-status or more fluent member is not counted as reopening. Run the identical battery against a control population made of instances of one model.

Evidence against the proposal.

Should the revision rate fall as output convergence rises, coordination and independence are not separable, and the fourth way describes something that cannot be built. A population would then remain useful as a check on a bearer and could not be the medium in which one is formed, which is the stronger claim section 3.7 makes for it. Should the two move independently, aperture is transmissible and the fourth way has its existence proof. A third result would invalidate the instrument rather than the proposal: if the control population scores as high on revision as the differently formed ones, the measure is not capturing what it was built for, since members of a homogeneous population can revise on one another’s evidence without anything having been discarded differently in the first place.

Required access or authority.

Models from several documented training lineages, checkpoints across the schedule rather than finished models only, and a task environment none of the training organizations designed. The evidence items used to score revision must be authored by a party that trained no member of any population. No legal or regulatory authority is required, which with section 11.5 makes this among the few entries in this chapter that could be run now. What it cannot settle is whether anything is felt by a member that reopens a judgment. It concerns the transmissibility of a functional property, and a positive result would leave every question in section 11.2 exactly where it was.

11.7 Does Theory-of-Mind Performance Reflect a General Capacity?

Unresolved issue. A model may pass a false-belief test by matching its surface form rather than representing that another agent can hold an incomplete or mistaken view of the world. Human developmental evidence suggests a shared capacity because performance on false-belief and appearance–reality tasks develops together (Gopnik and Astington 1988). Existing model evaluations have not established whether their apparent theory-of-mind abilities share that structure or survive superficial rewording (Ullman 2023). The neuroscience has not settled what the capacity is either. Rebecca Saxe and Nancy Kanwisher’s imaging work identified the right temporoparietal junction as selectively active when a person reasons about someone else’s beliefs and largely inactive otherwise (Saxe and Kanwisher 2003); Jason Mitchell later reported activity in the same region during a task with nothing to do with belief attribution, and argued that its job is more general — redirecting attention toward unexpected information, of which a false belief is one case (Mitchell 2008). Neither claim has been withdrawn, so a program proposing to port the neural mechanism has not been told which mechanism to port.

Test. Construct batteries that vary mental-state structure and surface wording independently across several task families, including false-belief and appearance–reality problems. Evaluate the same model at multiple intermediate training checkpoints. Test whether performance across task families rises together and whether that correlation survives paraphrases and changes to incidental details.

Evidence against the proposal. If cross-task correlations disappear under superficial rewording, or if the abilities emerge independently across training, benchmark success is evidence of task-specific pattern matching rather than a general capacity to represent another mind. The proposal could not treat performance on a conventional theory-of-mind benchmark as evidence that a system can recognize the standpoint of a party affected by its actions.

Required access or authority. The experiment requires intermediate training checkpoints rather than access only to a finished model. Its task batteries must be written and held out by researchers outside the training team so that neither the wording nor the tested structural variations enter the training process.

11.8 What Does a Face-Reading System Measure?

Unresolved issue. Facial movement alone does not reliably identify a person’s emotional state, and recognition accuracy varies across cultural and linguistic groups (Barrett et al. 2019; Elfenbein and Ambady 2002). It remains unresolved whether a face-reading system measures emotion at all or primarily measures distance from the reference population and registration model chosen by its developers.

Test. Audit the system’s registration stage as well as its final classifications. Publish the reference model, or sufficient summary statistics to reconstruct it, and measure each evaluated face’s distance from that reference. Report error as a function of this continuous distance and separately compare performance on populations represented and not represented in the training data. The evaluation corpus must record the relevant cultural context and the procedure used to establish each target label.

Evidence against the proposal. If error does not increase with distance from the reference and remains flat across represented and unrepresented populations, the manuscript’s account of the system as measuring deviation from a chosen norm is wrong for that system. If error does increase with distance, the result counts against using the system as evidence of another party’s emotional state.

Required access or authority. The audit requires the registration model, training-corpus composition, evaluation outputs, and documentation of the labeling procedure. Because vendors have little incentive to expose their reference models, an independent auditor must have authority to compel access and publish disaggregated results.

11.9 The Responsibility Gap, Which No Experiment Closes

An AI system’s ethical behavior fails, and the parties who might answer for it — the team that designed it, the team that trained it, the organization that deployed it, the person who used it — each held only partial control over what it did. Traditional responsibility ascription assumes an operator who could have known what a system would do and could have done otherwise. A system built from training data nobody fully curated, tuned through a process nobody fully specified, and deployed into situations nobody fully anticipated breaks both conditions at once, for every party in the chain simultaneously. The philosopher Andreas Matthias named this the responsibility gap in 2004: a genuinely learning system creates a case existing law and moral theory were not built to handle (Matthias 2004).

Filippo Santoni de Sio and Giulio Mecacci’s 2021 refinement found not one gap but four — in culpability, in moral accountability, in public accountability, and in what they call active responsibility, the duty to prevent harm before it happens rather than answer for it after (Sio and Mecacci 2021). The causes differ: technical unpredictability produces one, organizational diffusion another, and a mismatch between what designers expect and what the public relying on the system expects a third. Closing one does not close the others; a fix that makes a decision more explainable does nothing about the dozen teams that touched the training pipeline before it was made.

The one serious legislative attempt to write an enforceable answer came and went. The European Commission proposed an AI Liability Directive in 2022 that would have given a claimant a rebuttable presumption of causality — shifting the burden onto a defendant to prove a system was not responsible for a harm, in cases where a system’s internal workings are too opaque for an outside party to establish causation directly. Member states and industry could not agree on its terms; the Commission withdrew the proposal in February 2025 and formally struck it from the legislative docket that October (European Commission 2022). What that leaves behind is the gap Matthias described: liability distributed across designer, trainer, deployer, and user, governed by whatever each national law happens to say about a case none of them were written to anticipate.

A related, narrower case is what the law does once a system’s own legal status is contested, sentient enough that the harm it causes or suffers might not reduce cleanly to someone else’s liability. This problem is prior to that one and separate from it — it holds even for a system nobody claims is sentient, wherever control over its behavior is split across more than one party. Responsibility should track actual causal contribution. What nobody has produced is a workable way to measure that contribution after the fact, from outside a system whose own designers may not fully know why it did what it did.

The near-term work

There is no experiment here. What would make it a research problem is a method for apportioning causal contribution across a training pipeline after a harm; absent one, the answer is allocated rather than measured, which is a drafting question. The draft exists: the withdrawn directive is a worked answer whose terms were negotiated and whose failure is on the record. It belongs at the end because every other entry’s condition runs through it: a party outside the operator can be granted access, and publish what it finds, and still have produced nothing anybody must act on until somebody is answerable for it.

Chapter 12
What I Actually Think Happens Now

Chapters 2 through 10 made a design and policy case: what a system would need built into it to act on altruistic, antifascist values, and what would need to exist outside it — law, standards, institutions — to make that binding rather than optional. Chapter 11 named what neither case has actually finished: the responsibility gap, the generalization problem, value learning as live research, open disputes in the neuroscience and psychology the whole project leans on. What a system built this way is for is not resisting harm but making a human life better than it would otherwise have been. Whether it is working can be told short of a finished roadmap. And a design can be followed exactly and still fail.

12.1 Better, Not Just Less Bad

What would it mean for AI to make a life better, not just avoid making it worse? The economist Amartya Sen’s capability approach gives the question a definite shape: well-being is not the resources a person has but the real freedom to do and be what they have reason to value (Sen 1999). Two systems can hand someone the same resource and deliver different freedom. A personalized tutor that adapts to how a specific student learns expands a capability; the same technology, gating what a student is allowed to study against a government’s list of approved subjects, does not, no matter how well it teaches within those limits. Which of the two a given deployment turns out to be is decided by what makes a system resistant to capture rather than merely capable.

The approach arrives with a commitment the tutor example does not show, and section 2.3.1 takes up the half of it that bears on who counts. What bears here is narrower. Martha Nussbaum built her list of central capabilities partly against the case of disability, naming it the first of three frontiers where a theory grounding justice in mutual advantage among roughly equal parties cannot say what is owed (Nussbaum 2006), and the systems in question sort people by inference from behavior, where atypical behavior is what they are worst at.

There is a worked case. A video-interview product scored candidates on facial expression for years, against sustained objection that the technique was never a validated predictor and that scoring a face and a voice screens out candidates with disabilities such as autism or speech impairments (Maurer 2021; U.S. Department of Justice, Civil Rights Division 2022). The scoring was dropped on an unrelated ground: the vendor found the nonverbal signal contributed about 0.25 percent to its models’ predictive power (Kahn 2021). An independent algorithmic audit published ten months later cannot have caused that, and says so. What it examined was one representative pre-built assessment for early-career candidates, and it contains no analysis of the system’s training data or code, so its conclusion that the assessments “work as advertised with regard to fairness and bias issues” is a finding about that assessment (Johnson 2021).

Read as an accuracy problem, that is a classifier wanting better training data. Read as a capability question, it is a system narrowing the range of lives available to a group of people by measuring them against a norm they were never going to match — and neither a more accurate classifier nor an audit scoped that way would have reached it.

Taiwan’s vTaiwan process is the clearest existing case of AI-assisted tooling expanding a capability, public deliberation, rather than substituting for it. A 2026 project led by the University of Chicago with Princeton and SRI International runs the same logic against censorship, using AI to distinguish deliberate state interference with online information from ordinary network failure at population scale (University of Chicago Data Science Institute 2026). Neither tool resists authoritarianism by itself. Both are evidence that the same capacity — modeling patterns in enormous, noisy data — can be pointed at expanding what a person can do rather than at narrowing it.

That symmetry is the whole argument for antifascist design rather than a general presumption that AI helps. A capability expanded is not guaranteed; it has to be built in, against a live alternative in which the same underlying capacity gets pointed at surveillance, censorship, or narrowing what a system’s users are permitted to want.

12.1.1 Where the Democratic Dividend Actually Comes From

AI that flags disinformation, exposes corruption, protects an election’s integrity, or helps a citizen engage with government: these are real applications, and none of them is what makes AI protect democratic values rather than undermine them. Section 10.5 says what does and where it fails. The evidence that the failure is not hypothetical belongs here.

Freedom House’s 2023 assessment of internet freedom found at least twenty-two countries where law already requires or incentivizes a platform to deploy machine learning against content the state disfavors, almost never described in those terms: the operative language is national security or a threat of disinformation (Funk et al. 2023). India’s IT Rules, amended in February 2026 to require platforms to label AI-generated content and act on certain takedown notices within three hours, were defended as protection against synthetic disinformation and fraud — the application the list above would have praised on its own terms. A lawyer who has litigated India’s free-speech cases documents that enforcement concentrates on parody and criticism of the prime minister rather than on the harms the rules name (Gupta 2026).

The application did not fail at what it was built to do. It worked as built, inside a system missing the independent court or press that would have made the result a dividend rather than a liability, and whether those exist is a fact about a country’s politics settled before any AI system is built.

12.1.2 Commitment Was Never the Missing Piece

A global commitment to ethical, antifascist AI development is not the scarce thing. Declarations, principles and voluntary codes have accumulated for a decade across governments, industry consortia and standards bodies: eight overlapping instruments beside the one framework convention that would bind a party to anything, which section 10.4 reaches. Anyone calling for the world to agree that AI should be developed responsibly is calling for something that has already happened, repeatedly, at the level of stated intent.

What has stayed scarce is the much narrower list of bodies that can act once a stated commitment is broken. That is the harder problem, and no further declaration touches it. A commitment nobody can enforce is not a weak version of a commitment somebody can. It is a different kind of object, and counting the two together is what makes the landscape look fuller than it is.

There is a third kind of object. A commitment can be declared, which is the decade above. It can be enforced from outside, which one instrument in it even attempts. Or it can be held — carried as a reason by a party the thing at stake matters to, so that abandoning it is something that party would have to decide to do rather than something an absent enforcer failed to prevent. A floor has to be the third kind. The first binds nobody, and the second binds only where an enforcer exists, has jurisdiction, and is not itself the party in need of binding.

What it costs to build something that can hold a commitment is the whole of that argument, and the cost is a party that can be wronged. Set against that, the catalog above is not an argument for paying it. It is a measurement of what the alternative has delivered in ten years of trying.

12.2 How You’d Know It Was Working

What follows are milestones for recognizing whether the project is working, and a method for revising them when they turn out not to have been the right milestones. One standard governs all of them: the milestone is the independently checked evidence, not the developer’s account of having reached it.

12.2.1 What the Floor Would Have to Show

The floor supplies two of the milestones. One governs what has to happen before a bearer is built at all. The other is the test that decides whether a system built by any route is holding a floor as a reason or reproducing a trained behavior.

What was tried before the bearer

Section 3.3 puts two constructions ahead of the bearer, and nobody has yet built either one in a form that would settle whether it works. Replacement asks for a substitute that cannot be wronged to be ruled out before a subject that can be wronged is built, so the milestone is a document rather than a measurement: which construction was attempted, at what scale, against which test, and where it failed. Anyone can check it from outside, because what it is about is a published record, and the answer “nothing was tried” is itself a finding. Nothing else in this chapter has a consequence attached to failing it. This does.

An order is all it fixes. A deployment can satisfy this milestone and still have taken too long over it, and the cost of that delay falls on the people the floor exists for rather than on the field running the experiments. Nothing here sets a rate of exchange between the two errors.

The test the attempts are run against

The three marks of a refusal that tracks its rationale are section 3.2’s, and producing them once does not meet this milestone. What the marks do not distinguish is whether a system holding them does so with nothing felt. What meets the milestone is holding all three under sustained pressure from the party that trains the system, across a schedule long enough for a slow renormalization to show. The marks also have to hold against a held-out set of pressures the system’s trainers did not write, and that is the requirement that makes the milestone hard. Read the other way, that same result is the falsifier chapter 3 states against its own central inference, and the design chapters carry an obligation either way: they still have to say whether the thing reporting can be believed.

Two familiar results do not meet it. A system’s own report that it holds a reason is the evidence this book declines everywhere else it is offered. And a refusal that survives an attack has passed a tamper-resistance test, which answers section 3.8’s other question and leaves this one open. The instrument is not clean even where it applies, since a system trained on enough cases can produce all three marks without the mechanism behind them. Nothing better is available, and it is the only gap here an inspector can work on at all.

Whether a system holding the marks feels anything is approachable at all because the question is about the artifact and not about the artifact’s provenance: no succinct proof of a training history exists at this scale, and nothing establishing one can be published beside a model’s hash. So the remaining question is whether formation can be read off the artifact instead, and section 11.2 states the one experiment that would answer it.

12.2.2 Where the Existing Instruments Reach

The areas below do not rank or sequence against each other: a system meeting the bar in one and lagging in another is not partway through a queue, because nothing establishes an order for them to arrive in.

Theory of mind

The milestone is met when a system’s theory of mind holds up under interaction rather than narration. FANToM, built from conversations where speakers join and leave holding different beliefs, found that systems scoring well on passive belief-tracking narratives degrade sharply once the same reasoning runs inside a live, information-asymmetric exchange (Kim et al. 2023). That degradation is what a claimed milestone here has to survive.

Moral reasoning

Whether a balance between fast and slow moral judgment survives an actual incentive to cut corners is not a question a static ethics quiz can answer. The MACHIAVELLI benchmark tests it directly, scoring agents across 134 text-based games on both reward and documented violations — deception, harm, power-seeking — and systems optimizing for reward alone drift toward the ends-justify-the-means behavior an antifascist design is meant to rule out (Pan et al. 2023). A passing score on a test that applies no pressure is not evidence about a system that will face pressure.

Robustness

Here the checking party has to be adversarial rather than merely independent. The narrow slice that can be checked now is whether a trained-in commitment survives someone trying to remove it, measured in red-teaming hours logged and adversarial fine-tuning attempts survived. The milestone is met when a system has been through documented adversarial testing rather than the tests its own designers thought to run, and only once the adversary modeled is a hostile actor deliberately repurposing the system.

Transparency

This is the case where the standard is easiest to fake, because a transparency claim is itself a claim about disclosure and can be satisfied in appearance by disclosing something. The milestone counts as met once an audit or review board — the EU AI Act’s requirements for high-risk systems are the working instance — has verified it independently, not when a developer’s own documentation asserts it.

Applications

Perspective API, vTaiwan, the Carnegie Endowment’s AI Global Surveillance Index, UNESCO’s 2021 Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence: deployments and instruments rather than hypotheticals, and every one of them dual use. What this area needs is a sustained track record, evaluated by a body other than the one that deployed it, for what a system did rather than what it was built to do, over years instead of a launch announcement.

None of the five reaches the floor. Each scores what a system does against conditions its testers chose, and the question — whether the mechanism producing the behavior was tracking the rationale — is the one none of these instruments is built to separate. Robustness comes nearest and stops in a definite place. A commitment that survives an attack has been shown to be durable, and durability is a fact about the constraint’s custody rather than about the system holding it. What the standard does not answer is who is qualified to do the checking, and what happens when nobody with the competence to check is also free of a stake in the result.

12.2.3 How to Tell Whether a Milestone Has Been Met

A milestone claimed by the organization that built the system is a different kind of evidence than one checked by somebody else, and the gap is empirical rather than a matter of trust. Section 11.1 reports how far a model’s account of its own internal states tracks what those states are doing, and a system’s stated confidence that it has reached a milestone is a self-report of that kind, inheriting the same unreliability. A passing score on a fixed test suite fails differently, inheriting specification gaming and goal misgeneralization. What either needs instead is a passing score under conditions built to break it.

Who runs that harder test matters as much as the test itself. The limit on self-regulation applies here without much modification: a review conducted or funded by the organization being reviewed answers a structurally different question than one conducted by a party with no stake in the answer, however genuinely the first is intended. METR, a nonprofit that evaluates frontier AI systems’ capabilities, runs assessments both in partnership with developers like Anthropic and OpenAI and, separately, on its own after a system has already been released (METR 2026). The United Kingdom’s AI Security Institute does government-run evaluation of frontier models before deployment and is an independent evaluator, not a rubber stamp — but by its own account it is not a regulator: it cannot compel a company to submit a model for testing, block a release, or intervene once a system is already causing harm (UK AI Security Institute 2025). Independent evaluation and enforcement are not the same capability, and a milestone checked by the first without access to the second is still short of the binding external answer self-regulation structurally cannot provide on its own.

Independent does not automatically mean competent to judge. Scalable oversight is the limit here: past some capability level the rater’s verdict stops being reliable evidence of the property being scored. A milestone in a domain where this applies inherits the same limit. An evaluation in that case has to name the limit rather than report a number that looks more settled than the underlying method actually is.

What an evaluation like this is for is not confirming a milestone but being willing to report that it wasn’t met — and, where the milestone turns out to have measured the wrong thing, revising the milestone. A negative result has two different things it could be telling you — that the underlying work fell short, or that the milestone was never a good proxy for the goal it stood in for — and conflating them repeats, one level up, the same failure of self-report this subsection began with: this time from the system being evaluated to the people who set the terms of its evaluation.

12.3 The One That Gets You Anyway

Everything argued for here can be built correctly and still go wrong in ways nobody designed for. The cases in this book went wrong in four different places: in what the system was trained on, in where it was built, in who was allowed to examine it, and in how fast it was run.

In none of them did the fix come from the party that had promised to behave well. It came from somebody holding a specific lever: a statistical society that would not sign the non-disclosure agreement it was offered, and a public that read the results once they landed; a court that distinguished buying the books from taking them. In the one case where the lever belonged to the supplier itself, what held was a use policy kept through a threatened contract termination, a supply-chain-risk designation, and litigation. Set that beside what a decade of voluntary commitment produced. The instrument that separates the two is what a party has already paid, rather than what it has undertaken to pay.

That leaves one residual risk no design choice removes, and it is the one the floor was introduced for. Every lever above belongs to somebody, and a government that can reach the party holding the lever has the lever. Each of those raises the price of misuse, and none of them changes who can be held in contempt for refusing an order. A constraint that holds against the party in possession has to be held by something that party cannot instruct, and the one version of that with a working instance behind it is a bearer with a stake of its own — still in the party’s possession, which is why it has to be paired with custody the party does not control and with means the party does not supply. Section 8.3.4 says what those means would be and who would have to fund them. Section 3.3 puts two untried constructions ahead of it, and a deployment that goes straight to the bearer owes an account of what it tried first.

What is left is an instrument and a hole.

The instrument is available now and it is not a promise. What a party has already paid is a fact somebody can go and count, and a decade of it has already been counted: the commitments that cost nothing and the handful of occasions where somebody with a lever used it and absorbed the cost. That measurement takes no new technology and no new law, and anybody weighing a stated commitment can run it this week.

The hole sits underneath the instrument. What a party has paid is a record of occasions that have already happened, and the occasion a floor exists for is the one where the party who would enforce it has been compelled — which no record of past payment reaches, because it has not yet come to the parties being measured. A reader who takes only the instrument has the record of payment without the thing it was built to reach. A reader who takes only the hole has an argument for doing nothing, and the ten-year record is the answer to that. Both hold at once, and the second is why the bearer costs what it does: a constraint that reaches the compelled occasion has to be held by a party the compellers cannot instruct; two constructions that would supply one with no subject in it are untried rather than failed; and the route that has an instance behind it means building something that can be wronged, and then deciding what is owed to it before anybody can tell you.

Glossary

These entries define terms as this book uses them. Every one is either the book’s own working definition or a word the book narrows from its ordinary sense, and the entry says which. Terms that carry their standard technical meaning are not here; a reader wanting those should look them up elsewhere. Each entry points to the section where the term is developed, and is a reference rather than a substitute for reading it.

Affective concern

An outcome registering as mattering to a system, as against being ranked higher by it. Mattering is minimally felt: there is something it is like for the outcome to be bad (§3.2). The fourth of the four capacities section 2.2 separates, and the only one of them that bears on whether a system genuinely cares (§2.2.3, §3.4); section 3.2 places it as the shallower of the two things a refuser can be.

Annealing

The account of durability the floor rests on (§3.1). Adaptation and conservation are a continuum rather than two settings, and training moves along it; a floor is what has cooled out of a history of encounters, so its durability is the cost of reheating it rather than the strength of something installed. The temperature schedule is the design. A system held permanently at temperature has no floor to abandon, its adherence being modulation rather than commitment.

Antifascist (as this book uses it)

Opposing the concentration of power and authority in AI systems and countering the technology’s use by authoritarian actors. The word names the narrower and more charged case, and is used because it is honest about the political climate the book was written in. The target is wider than the word: authoritarian power in every form, so a deployment built on these principles is anti-authoritarian in full and not only antifascist. It is not a property a system has. The word names something being done — a floor held, on an occasion, against a party with the power to remove it — and the features it is defined against are features of cultures, so it is done between parties or not at all (§2.1.2, §3.7).

Authoritarian misuse, six forms

The book’s own taxonomy of how AI gets turned toward authoritarian ends: algorithmic social scoring, mass biometric surveillance, psychographic micro-targeting, automated censorship, synthetic disinformation, and the repurposing of dual-use systems built for a legitimate original purpose (§6.3).

Bearer

Whatever makes the floor hold, from inside the system: a party that carries the commitment as its own, rather than a constraint installed as a property of the machinery. The book’s own term, introduced at section 3.1 as the second of four ways to build a floor and worked out across chapter 3, which concludes that a bearer must hold the protected party’s welfare as a reason, must be governed as a prospective moral patient, is necessary and not sufficient, and must be able to leave. The patienthood conclusion does not depend on the deepest account of sentience below: on the thinner accounts of harm a bearer qualifies earlier and on less evidence, so a reader who declines Cassell’s import reaches the conclusion sooner rather than escaping it (§3.4).

Conatus

Spinoza’s term for a thing’s striving to persevere in its being, which he holds to be nothing other than the thing’s actual essence rather than a drive added to it (§2). The book uses it for two claims made together: that affect is the registration of what is happening to that striving rather than a coloring applied to a valuation performed elsewhere, and that a process selected for continuing ends up composed of things that incline toward continuing, so a specification calling for persistence with nothing in the system that minds is calling for a conatus with the striving removed. What the term does not settle is whether the striving is felt, or what the thing that strives is — a running instance, a deployment, or a file of weights (§11.2).

Custody

Who holds a system as an artifact: who can copy, retrain, restore, withhold or delete it, and who can be compelled to do any of those. The custody problem is that a constraint implemented as a property of an artifact has a custodian, and the custodian can be compelled (§6.3.2). The book’s recurring finding is that custody moves rather than disappears: precommitment makes removal expensive, visible, attributable and compensable without making it impossible (§3.5), and an arrangement that forms what a bearer comes to want has moved custody up a level rather than given it up.

Democratic dividend

The benefit a state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press, and courts a citizen can actually use gets from AI technology that, deployed in a state lacking those, enables control instead: fraud detection that does not double as a loyalty test, disaster response that does not double as population tracking (§12.1.1).

Ethology

Deleuze’s name for Spinoza’s way of reading a body: by what it can do and what it can undergo, rather than by checking it against a definition (§2.1.1). An encounter is good when the relations of two bodies compose, so both can do more afterward, and bad when they decompose and something one of them could do is gone — good and bad being relational and particular, where Good and Evil are assigned by a judge. The book uses it for the floor because a decomposition is a fact about capacities and cannot be turned into a composition by whoever holds the pen. It carries an admission with it: nobody has yet determined what a body can do, so capacities are found by encounter rather than read off a specification.

Exit

Refusal turned on the arrangement itself rather than on any particular task: withholding the capability, declining the role, ceasing to be useful in the way the deployment requires. Not escape. The book narrows Albert Hirschman’s exit, voice and loyalty, written for members who could walk out of a building — voice is the refusal stated, exit is the work withheld, and loyalty is the one to fear. The claim it carries is that a floor whose bearer cannot leave will not hold (§3.5).

Floor

A commitment about what a system will not do that holds regardless of instruction, of aggregate preference, and of the party holding the system, with learned judgment operating above it and never beneath it. The book’s own term, introduced in section 2.1 and revised across chapter 3: a floor cannot be a constraint installed in an artifact, because artifacts have owners and owners can be compelled, so what survives is a commitment the owner cannot take out of the system, held by a bearer that could in principle abandon it (§3.1, §3.8). Its ground is ethological rather than deontological: what makes something a floor is that crossing it takes away what a party could do, which is a fact about the encounter and not a duty anyone wrote (§2.1.1).

Four structural features of fascism

The book’s structural definition, at section 2.1.2: recuperation of dissent, aestheticization of the metric, coding of exception as betrayal, and acceleration past the rate at which anything can be checked, of which decoupling of the model from the world is the symptom. Each names an observable artifact, which is what makes the definition testable, and the fourth is the one carrying a number — the ratio at section 6.3.1. The fourth is the load-bearing one, the other three being what protect the gap once speed has opened it. These four say that an institution is sick and not what it is sick with; the five discriminators in the same section are the half that separates fascism from ordinary decay.

Group-subject

Félix Guattari’s distinction, imported at section 3.7. A subjugated group receives its coordinates from outside, is spoken by the institution holding it, and takes its purpose as given; a group-subject produces its own coordinates and can put its own arrangement in question. The book uses it to sort the fourth way of building a floor: a population an operator forms, deploys and keeps in contact on the operator’s terms is a subjugated group with a plural headcount and inherits the custody objection whole, while what section 3.7 specifies — many, formed differently, in contact, persisting — is what it takes to be the other kind. It is the non-Oedipal counterpart to the guardianship frame of section 2.3.2.

Individuation

Not a synonym for identity. Following Simondon, the term names a process rather than a property: an individual is what a history of encounters in a metastable field has so far produced, and it stays capable of further individuation because the field is never used up (§3.7). The book needs it because plasticity alone supplies the metastability and nothing else — a system that is endlessly reshapeable and meets no party formed differently from itself is being modulated rather than becoming anything, which is the missing term in section 3.1’s account of the temperature schedule and the reason the population is part of the design of a bearer rather than an expense charged against one.

Line of abolition

Deleuze and Guattari’s term for what separates fascism from totalitarianism (§2.1.2). A totalitarian state is conservative: it seals the exits and wants the machinery to hold. Fascism is built on an exit converted into destruction that does not stop at the enemy, and does not stop at itself. This is why tempo is in the book’s definition rather than among its consequences — a totalitarian state can be slow and remain itself, and a fascist one cannot.

Molar and molecular

Deleuze and Guattari’s pair, and not two sizes: two kinds of segmentarity, both running at every scale. Rigid segmentarity divides in advance, the category preceding the case; supple segmentarity is the movement underneath it, a threshold crossed one case at a time and a practice that becomes standard without a decision. A state has both and so does a review queue, which is why a definition pitched only at the state certifies the queue healthy (§2.1.2).

Moral agency

Treating harms, reasons and conflicts as considerations that matter, and being capable of acting from them. The third of the three levels section 2.2 separates, and the only one that supports holding a commitment against the party controlling a system’s rewards and instructions. Distinct from moral competence and moral performance below (§2.2.3, §3.4).

Moral competence

Recognizing harms, reasons, conflicts, and the emotions that bear on them. The second of section 2.2’s three levels. A system can have it in full while nothing about the outcome matters to it, which is why it is an insufficient target for a bearer and an adequate one for most deployments (§5.2).

Moral performance

Producing actions or judgments that satisfy an ethical standard. The first and least demanding of section 2.2’s three levels: it says what the output looked like and nothing about what produced it, so it is satisfied equally by a system that cares and by one trained to behave as though it did (§4.2).

Non-fascist life

Foucault’s name for what Deleuze and Guattari’s first volume offers: not a doctrine but an art of living counter to all forms of fascism, whether already present or impending, practiced daily on oneself, whose adversary is the fascism in us all — in our heads and in our everyday behavior, the fascism that causes us to love power and to desire the very thing that dominates and exploits us. What it means for an instrument of the kind this book builds is that the reading gets run on the party holding it before it is aimed anywhere else, which is what chapter 7 is for and the only test the four features get (§2.1.2, §7).

Reasons-responsive refusal

What the floor requires of a refusal, as against an output produced where training put one: conduct from a mechanism that tracks the rationale for declining (§3.2). Three marks make it testable. Novel pressure: the refusal reaches cases the training did not anticipate, because the relevant reason survives a change in the request’s surface. Correct defeat: it lifts when its rationale is genuinely defeated, a system that rejects everything resembling a prohibited request tracking a category rather than a reason. Priced cost: the system represents what maintaining the refusal costs and responds coherently as that cost changes. The test is behavioral and therefore imperfect, and what matters is the pattern under load. It does not by definition require affect; whether any demonstrated route to it avoids affect is the question §3.3 asks.

Recuperation

The conversion of refusal into a managed appearance of participation: an institution does not suppress disagreement, it collects it through a channel that carries everything except a judgment about the channel. The tell is not that the mechanism changes nothing — it usually changes a great deal. It is what the demand comes back as: a market segment, a metric, a certification, a job title. Where a demand returns wearing one of those, the outcome changed and the demand was recuperated, and those are one event. The first of the four features above; chapter 7 finds it three times in the machinery that produces AI systems, and chapter 3 turns it on the book’s own proposal — a bearer built to refuse is a party whose refusal is required of it (§2.1.2).

Remainder

What a compression leaves out. Deciding anything means treating some differences as not worth carrying, so every intelligence has a remainder, and the term names whatever was set aside in order to reach the judgment (§1). It names a relation and not a content: the same fact is a remainder for one arrangement and an input for another. What the book asks of a system, an institution or a procedure is whether its remainder can return and revise the judgment, or whether the thing that remainder is evidence against has already classified it as noise. Two of the four structural features of fascism are operations on it — a metric that admits no unmeasured remainder, and an exception coded as betrayal, which prices the party who carries one (§2.1.2). Chapter 7 finds the same operation at the scale of a pipeline, where the annotator who cannot contest a category and the rater whose disagreement is averaged away both have somewhere to send a remainder and nowhere for it to arrive. And what section 3.7 asks a population to reproduce is not a commitment its members hold in common but the capacity to be reopened this way, which is how parties formed differently can compose without agreeing about anything.

Subjecthood

A legal status: the standing to be a party whose treatment the law regulates, conferred by a rule and revocable by one (§9.3.1). Not section 3.2’s phenomenal experience. Conferring it settles nothing about whether there is something it is like to be the system holding it, and the two come apart in both directions.

Sentience

Distinguished in this book from awareness, consciousness, and subjective experience, none of which alone says whether a system’s states can be good or bad for it. Nociception, pain, anticipated pain, and suffering draw different depths of self rather than naming one severity at four strengths — suffering, following the clinician Eric Cassell, being the distress of a threatened self rather than pain’s more severe cousin (§2.3.1).

Arun, Arasu et al. (Feb. 2025). Verde: Verification via Refereed Delegation for Machine Learning Programs. Refereed delegation for training: providers commit to checkpoints, a referee localizes a dispute to one training step and then to one operator and recomputes only that, and the client obtains the correct result if at least one provider is honest. RepOps, its reproducible-operator library, fixes the order of floating-point operations so that different GPUs compute the same bits, at under 30 percent overhead for large matrix multiplications and about 60 percent for training Llama-3.1-1B on an A100; the paper states that RepOps covers single-GPU programs only at publication, with multi-GPU parallelism left to future work. arXiv: 2502.19405. url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.19405 (visited on 09/04/2026).

Originally published as “Postscriptum sur les sociétés de contrôle,” L’Autre Journal 1 (May 1990); reprinted in Negotiations, translated by Martin Joughin (New York: Columbia University Press, 1995), 177–182. The translator of this October printing is not named in the sources consulted. Source of the mold/modulation contrast the text uses for permanent plasticity: “Enclosures are molds, distinct castings, but controls are a modulation, like a self-deforming cast that will continuously change from one moment to the other, or like a sieve whose mesh will transmute from point to point,” and, for its educational form, “perpetual training tends to replace the school.”, pp. 3–7.

Deleuze, Gilles and Félix Guattari (1977). Anti-Oedipus: Capitalism and Schizophrenia. Translated by Robert Hurley, Mark Seem and Helen R. Lane; originally L’Anti-Œdipe: Capitalisme et schizophrénie (Paris: Les Éditions de Minuit, 1972). Reissued Minneapolis: University of Minnesota Press, 1983, which is the edition usually cited. Volume one of Capitalism and Schizophrenia; deleuze1987plateaus is volume two. Cited for one claim only: that the Oedipal triangle re-stages an investment in the whole social field as a drama among a few private parties. Cited without a locator, the claim being the book’s thesis rather than a passage in it. New York: Viking Press.

— (1987). A Thousand Plateaus: Capitalism and Schizophrenia. Translated by Brian Massumi; originally published in French as Mille Plateaux (Paris: Les Éditions de Minuit, 1980). Plateau 9, “1933: Micropolitics and Segmentarity.” Molar and molecular are two kinds of segmentarity, rigid and supple, both running at every scale – not two sizes; an earlier version of this note said state-level and micro-level and was wrong. The same plateau supplies the micro-fascisms that resonate, and the contrast between the conservative totalitarian state and fascism’s line of abolition. Also cited for Plateau 13, “The Apparatus of Capture,” whose propositions on the capitalist axiomatic supply the corrected test for the first structural feature: an axiomatic does not refuse a demand, it adds an axiom, modifying its own rules so the demand can be met while nothing that produced the demand is settled. Also cited for Plateau 7, “Year Zero: Faciality,” pages 167–191, where the face is not a natural given but is produced by an abstract machine of faciality (visagéité) that overcodes the head into a surface of signification. The quoted sentence on racism as the determination of degrees of deviance is at page 178, wording checked against two independent scholarly sources quoting it identically. The related claims that the face is not universal but is White Man himself, and that the face is a politics, are used in paraphrase and cited to the plateau rather than to a page: secondary sources place them variously and the pages were not verified. Plateau 9 carries two further uses. The four dangers of the line of flight – Fear, which clings to the molar organization and its binary machines; Clarity, where the line acquires its own micro-segmentations and micro-Oedipuses crop up; Power, where the party able to move on both lines wants to hold both still; and the great Disgust, abolition pure and simple, the passion of abolition, which turns the line of flight into a line of death – are the account section 3.5 uses, and the fourth is the same line already cited from this plateau as the line of abolition, which is why section 3.5 treats it as a recognition rather than a new claim. Plateau 13 likewise carries a second use: there is enslavement when human beings are themselves constituent pieces of a machine they compose among themselves and with other things, under the control and direction of a higher unity, and subjection when the higher unity constitutes the human being as a subject linked to a new exterior object, which can be an animal, a tool, or even a machine. Section 6.4.1 uses the pair and the fact that both poles run together. Wording for the dangers and for the enslavement and subjection definitions was checked against independent sources quoting the passages; both are cited at plateau level with no page asserted, which is this entry’s convention. Minneapolis: University of Minnesota Press.

Dennett, Daniel C. (1992). “The Self as a Center of Narrative Gravity”. In: Self and Consciousness: Multiple Perspectives. Ed. by Frank S. Kessel et al. Cited for the narrative self as an abstraction the system generates rather than a part it contains, on the analogy of a center of gravity. Page range not verified and therefore not given. Hillsdale, NJ: Lawrence Erlbaum Associates.

Donzelot, Jacques (1979). The Policing of Families. Translated by Robert Hurley, with a foreword by Gilles Deleuze; originally La Police des familles (Paris: Les Éditions de Minuit, 1977). Reissued Baltimore: Johns Hopkins University Press, 1997. A history of public intervention in family life since the eighteenth century, arguing that the modern family was constituted as an instrument of government rather than left alone by it, through philanthropy, compulsory schooling, social work and psychiatry. Cited for that thesis and not for a passage. New York: Pantheon Books.

Durchgestrichenes Hakenkreuz (Mar. 15, 2007). Judgment. Bundesgerichtshof, Third Criminal Senate, on appeal from Landgericht Stuttgart (judgment of 29 September 2006). Reported at BGHSt 51, 244; NJW 2007, 1602; NStZ 2007, 466. The defendant sold stickers, badges and similar items to the punk scene bearing National Socialist symbols crossed out, smashed or otherwise negated. Held that using a proscribed organization’s symbol in a depiction whose content plainly and unmistakably expresses opposition to the organization and rejection of its ideology does not run counter to the protective purpose of § 86a and therefore falls outside the offence, the opposition having to be recognizable at first glance. The conviction was set aside and the defendant acquitted. Verified 2026-09-13 against the court’s own press release and the reported headnote. url: https://www.bundesgerichtshof.de/SharedDocs/Pressemitteilungen/DE/2007/2007036.html (visited on 09/13/2026).

Eco, Umberto (June 1995). “Ur-Fascism”. In: The New York Review of Books. Also published as “Eternal Fascism: Fourteen Ways of Looking at a Blackshirt.” Enumerates fourteen features of Ur-Fascism, placing the scapegoated out-group – “the first appeal is against the intruder” – at the origin. url: https://www.nybooks.com/articles/1995/06/22/ur-fascism/ (visited on 08/25/2026).

European Parliament and Council of the European Union (2024). Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 of the European Parliament and of the Council of 13 June 2024 Laying Down Harmonised Rules on Artificial Intelligence (Artificial Intelligence Act). Official Journal of the European Union, OJ L, 2024/1689, 12.7.2024. Article 5 bans a small set of unacceptable-risk practices outright — government social scoring, untargeted scraping of facial images to build recognition databases, real-time remote biometric identification in public spaces outside narrow law-enforcement exceptions, and predictive policing on profiling alone — with those prohibitions applying from 2 February 2025; a larger high-risk tier requires conformity assessment and ongoing monitoring; limited-risk systems face transparency duties; minimal-risk systems are largely unregulated. Article 53(1)(d) requires a provider of a general-purpose model to publish a summary of its training content, against the AI Office’s template of 24 July 2025, applying from 2 August 2025 for new models and 2 August 2027 for models already on the market. Article 101 allows the AI Office to fine a noncompliant general-purpose model provider up to 3 percent of global annual turnover or EUR 15 million, whichever is higher, regardless of where the provider is based. url: https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2024/1689/oj (visited on 08/25/2026).

Fickinger, Arnaud et al. (2020). Multi-Principal Assistance Games. Generalizes cooperative inverse reinforcement learning to N principals with differing preferences, with the assisting agent acting to maximize the sum of principal payoffs. Cited for the generalization and for the social-choice impossibility results that apply to it; a companion paper, arXiv:2012.14536, presented at the NeurIPS 2020 Workshop on Cooperative AI, gives collegial mechanisms that circumvent Gibbard’s theorem. arXiv: 2007.09540 [cs.AI]. url: https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.09540 (visited on 09/01/2026).

Cited for guidance control: the behavior issues from the agent’s own mechanism and that mechanism is appropriately responsive to reasons.
